[Footnote 156: _Ibid._]

 II

 THE CONQUEST OF EGYPT


The measures necessary for executing the design on Egypt were detailed
by Bonaparte in a despatch dated March 5th. For taking possession of
Egypt and Malta he demanded twenty to twenty-five thousand men, who were
to be stationed at Toulon, Marseilles, Genoa, Cività Vecchia, and
Corsica and to be ready for embarkation by the end of April.[157] The
Directory, on the same day, acceded to his various demands. A commission
of four was appointed to direct and supervise the complex preparations,
which included the refitting of ships of war, the collecting of
transports and the moving of troops.[158]

The proposed expedition, however, was not to be purely military.
Bonaparte contemplated nothing less than bringing back to the light of
day a civilisation long buried under the sands of the desert, and ending
the long Egyptian night. An Arabic and a Greek printing press were to be
set up for the war of propaganda.[159] Orders were given for placing at
his disposal a staff of over a hundred savants, mechanics, engineers,
architects, authors, and interpreters.[160]

He made his preparations with a zeal such as those about him had never
before seen him show, and on such a scale as would ensure success and
preserve his glory from all risk. He recruited in every quarter—thus
anticipating the brilliant period of the Consulate—officers and
soldiers, men of letters and artists. Republicans or Royalists, nobles
or Jacobins, all were the same to him, provided they served his end.
Kléber, who heartily disliked him, enlisted in order to see, as he
energetically expressed himself, “Ce que ce petit b . . . là avait dans
le ventre.”[161] Such was then his fame that he was able to enrol under
his standard his enemies as well as his friends.

Admiral Brueys, who arrived at Toulon on April 2nd, was instructed to
have his squadron ready to set sail by the 20th.[162] On March 31st, in
order to put the English on a false track, the Directory ordered
Bonaparte to proceed to Brest, and take under his command the forces
preparing for the invasion of England.[163]

By the middle of April, however, the preparations were well advanced and
the moment arrived when the expedition could be formally decreed.
Bonaparte was appointed to the command of the land as well as the sea
forces and was ordered to include the Ionian Islands within the area
under him.[164] He was, moreover, charged to take possession of Egypt,
to expel the English from such of their Eastern establishments as he
could reach, and, especially, from their Red Sea _comptoirs_, to take
all measures for securing to the Republic the exclusive navigation of
that sea, to cut a canal through the Isthmus of Suez, and to ameliorate
by all possible means the lot of the Egyptians. This wanton aggression
on the territory of a friendly power was justified by the fact that the
beys were in league with the English and were perpetrating the most
shocking cruelties on the French, “who were being daily tortured,
plundered and murdered,” by the necessity of securing this route to
India in order to destroy England’s satellites there and cut off the
source of the wealth employed by that power in corrupting the
world.[165] He was, likewise, to take possession of Malta if he could do
so, without compromising the success of the more important operations
entrusted to him. The Order of St. John—so ran the decree—had declared
its hostility to France as early as October, 1794, and since then had
not ceased to manifest it in various ways.[166] Finally, the French
frigates and the transports stationed in the Mauritius were to proceed
to Suez and be at Bonaparte’s disposal.[167] In connection with this
last decree, it may be added, letters of credence addressed to various
Oriental potentates, but in which the name of the envoy was left blank,
were prepared.[168] Nelson’s victory made them of no use.

The _Révélateur_ of September 16th and 17th, protesting against this
policy of usurpation, ironically remarked that the Porte had not
appointed the French his police-officers.[169] That was precisely the
_rôle_ which the Directory professed and diplomatic action was
determined upon to bring the Porte to view the presence of the French in
Egypt in that light.

The diplomatic preparations of the Directory consisted entirely in the
appointment of an ambassador who was to obtain the consent of the Porte
to the French occupation of Egypt or, at any rate, its acquiescence.
Talleyrand drew up the necessary instructions and submitted them to the
Directory on March 16th.[170] After a lengthy historical exposition of
Franco-Turkish relations, he emphasised the interests of the Republic in
preserving the integrity of Turkey and avoiding a rupture with that
power and pointed out that the ambassador should leave France for his
post towards the beginning of May. On hearing of the landing of the
French in Egypt—which, probably, would be in August—he was to take
measures for the protection of French residents and protract the
negotiations so as to give Bonaparte time to establish himself. For the
guidance of the plenipotentiary, Talleyrand drew up two plans. According
to the first of these, the Porte was to be represented at Cairo by a
Pasha, who should be the nominal head of the administration and whose
expenses should be defrayed from an annual tribute to the Sultan
amounting to 1500 “purses” (2¼ million francs). The French, moreover,
were to pledge themselves to respect the property and religion of the
inhabitants. In return for these “concessions,” Djezzar, the Pasha of
Acre, should be deposed. According to the second plan the Porte was to
surrender Egypt to the French and to have, in compensation, the Ionian
Islands. The Ambassador was also to declare that France was willing to
help Turkey to recover some of its lost provinces, for example, the
Crimea.

According to Miot de Melito, Talleyrand did not sincerely believe in the
efficacy of the negotiations which he proposed.[171] Although the ex-
bishop was accomplished in the art of false-witness, it is difficult to
accept Miot’s statements in face of the persistence with which the
Minister expressed his hope of avoiding a rupture with Turkey.
Bonaparte, however, proposed that the Ambassador charged with this
delicate mission should be no other than Talleyrand himself. According
to Miot, he acted in this matter in accordance with his usual practice,
which was “to implicate those men whose advice he had followed in any
risk that might arise from acting on it, thus obtaining a guarantee
against treachery or desertion.”[172] Talleyrand, in order to escape
from the persistent hostility of Rewbell, accepted the post.[173] As
will be explained later on, he did not leave Paris; and it was not till
May 11th that he wrote to Ruffin—French chargé at Constantinople—of the
Egyptian Expedition, of the intention to send an ambassador, and of his
hope that an “accommodation” would be reached, and instructed him to
take measures, in the meantime, for the protection of French
residents.[174]

While Bonaparte was still lingering in Paris, the first symptoms of the
complications upon which he was counting appeared. At Rastadt the
Austrian plenipotentiary had opposed France’s demand for the entire left
bank of the Rhine; and in Vienna the French Ambassador, Bernadotte, had
given umbrage to an etiquette-ridden Court, and, on April 13th, incited
the populace to an anti-French riot, in consequence of which he was
obliged to leave the country. The situation was critical and war seemed
imminent. Rewbell considered the affair as a “guet-apens abominable” on
the part of England and Russia, while Talleyrand and Bonaparte, on the
other hand, laid the blame on Bernadotte.[175] The Prussian envoy,
Sandoz, also employed his influence on behalf of peace; conciliatory
communications promising satisfaction, moreover, arrived from
Vienna.[176] The Directory, thereupon, decreed, on April 24th, that
Bonaparte should proceed to Rastadt and settle all outstanding
difficulties with Austria.[177] He seemed to be disposed[178] to
undertake the new task and announced to Cobenzl his intended
departure.[179] Hearing, however, that the latter had left for Vienna,
he postponed his own departure for Rastadt.[180]

His letters to some of his lieutenants spoke of the Bernadotte affair as
only delaying for a few days the great expedition on which he was
engaged.[181] Miot relates in his _Memoirs_ that the incident gave him
the position he coveted by placing “once more the fate of France and of
her government in his hands,”[182] while Mathieu Dumas asserts that his
thoughts turned anew to a _coup d’état_ and a dictatorship.[183] He
himself, at St. Helena, attributed his hesitation to patriotic anxiety
as to the safety of France.[184] The Directory, however, did not take
this view and insisted on his departure to Toulon, where he arrived on
May 9th. Immediately on his arrival, he reviewed some of the troops and,
in a proclamation, promised each of them six acres of land.[185]

While waiting for a favourable wind, the question of the presence of the
English Fleet in the Mediterranean came to the forefront. The French
Ambassador at Madrid had been already instructed to persuade the Spanish
Government to order its ships blockaded at Cadiz to manœuvre in such a
way as to prevent the English fleet under St. Vincent from leaving the
station.[186] Nothing, however, came of this. But the news at Toulon was
that, though the English were present in the Mediterranean, they were
not in such numbers as to constitute a serious menace.[187] The gigantic
armada composed of about 38,000 troops, of thirteen sail-of-the-line and
six frigates left Toulon, accordingly, on May 19th.[188]

We have now to trace the history of the most decisive move in the
history of the Expedition, namely, the decision of the English
Government to send a fleet to the Mediterranean.

The secret of the expedition had been so well kept that the English
Cabinet was not able to worm it out.[189] Certainly Pitt’s spy system
was far less efficient than had been imagined. There was even the
suspicion that two French servants of Lord Grenville’s household
communicated to their Government the names of all his visitors and, in
this way, it was alleged that all the English spies in France were
perfectly known to the Directory.[190] With ordinary activity, however,
the Oriental scheme could have been found out from one of Barras’
mistresses or from some official at Toulon. The engaging of Arabic
interpreters and the setting-up of Arabic printing presses might have
enlightened an agent of average intelligence. The Cabinet, however, was
utterly in the dark as to the destination of the armada, and its members
indulged in many wild conjectures based on all sorts of fantastic
clues.[191] Dundas seems to have been the only important member of the
Cabinet who guessed right.[192] Some other conjectures were
correct—notably that of the British Consul at Leghorn,[193] and some of
the intelligence received was such as to shed light on the destination
of the Expedition.[194] This information, however, reached the Cabinet
too late to influence its decision to send a fleet to the Mediterranean.
The grounds upon which it was based have to be sought elsewhere.

This measure, tantamount to a revolution in English military policy,
reflected the revival of the national spirit and the desire, in the
words of a Foreign Office despatch, “to reduce France within her ancient
limits” and thus secure “the future tranquillity and independence of
Europe.”[195] The fitful Paul was certain to weary of his peaceful mood.
His chivalrous nature revolted at the depredations of France in
Switzerland and Rome. The growth of indignation at St. Petersburg begat
new hopes in Vienna. The Queen of Naples—the sister of Marie-
Antoinette—was fuming against the hated Jacobins and pestered her
nephew, the Emperor, and the English minister, Sir William Hamilton,
with appeals for protection and for help. The latter communicated these
appeals to his Government in March and to St. Vincent directly in
April.[196] These Neapolitan clamours must have weighed with the
Austrian Court, for on March 18th, Thugut, the Chancellor, instructed
the Ambassador at London, in a despatch of fifty-five pages, to find out
whether England would, among other things, send a fleet to the
Mediterranean.[197] Grenville stated in an interview with Starhemberg,
the ambassador, that the measure was greatly desired by the Cabinet, but
that they could not take it unless Naples would express her readiness to
provide the English ships with the necessary supplies.[198] Pitt
embraced the suggestion eagerly. With a friendly port in the
Mediterranean, and occasionally even without one, he could not see much
difficulty in stretching a helping hand to Naples.[199] The First Lord
of the Admiralty, Earl Spencer, however, thought differently. He
estimated the forces equipping at Toulon at no more than ten sail and
could not see his way—with a deficiency of 8000 men—to detach a
respectable force for service in the Mediterranean. He suggested,
however, that St. Vincent, with the fleet under his command, should take
a sweep round that sea and do all the mischief he could to the French
Navy.[200] Grenville, on hearing this, became despondent;[201] but Pitt,
on the contrary, was not convinced. He saw many symptoms of the spirit
of the country awakening and was determined to brave the risks of
invasion in preference to a lingering war.[202] His view, as in other
cases, prevailed.

This wise and courageous decision to resume the offensive deserves
nothing but the highest praise and marks the turn of the tide in favour
of England in Europe.[203]

On April 29th, accordingly, the Cabinet issued orders to St. Vincent to
take such measures as he deemed necessary to thwart the projects of the
Toulon armament. It was left to his judgment whether to go in person
with his whole fleet, or to send a detachment of not less than nine or
ten ships-of-the-line under a competent flag officer. If possible, the
Government wished him to maintain the blockade of Cadiz as it had been
established since the Battle of St. Vincent; but everything was to yield
to the necessity of checking the sailing of the Toulon expedition or of
defeating it if it had already started. A speedy reinforcement to supply
the ships that might be detached was promised.[204]

A private letter from Earl Spencer reflected the views and anxieties of
the Government:[205] “The circumstances in which we now find ourselves
oblige us to take a measure of a more decided and hazardous complexion
than we should otherwise have thought ourselves justified in taking; but
when you are apprized that the appearance of a British squadron in the
Mediterranean is a condition on which the fate of Europe may at this
moment be stated to depend, you will not be surprised that we are
disposed to strain every nerve and incur considerable hazard in
effecting it.” The rest of the letter is a commentary on this almost
solemn pronouncement and emphasises the significance of the choice of
the officer who was to command. Spencer strongly recommended Nelson for
that post if St. Vincent would not command in person.

Before receiving these important orders, St. Vincent had, on May 2nd,
sent instructions to Nelson which, after reciting that he had received
intelligence that a considerable armada was preparing at Toulon and a
number of transports collecting at Marseilles and Genoa for the
embarkation of troops, directed him to proceed, with as much of his
squadron as might be at Gibraltar, up the Mediterranean and endeavour to
ascertain the destination of the Expedition which, according to some
reports, was bound for Sicily and Corfu and, according to others,
Portugal and Ireland. If Nelson found the enemy intending to go down the
Mediterranean he was not to suffer him to pass the Straits of Gibraltar
before him.[206] Upon receiving on May 10th the above despatches,
however, the Admiral’s first step was to order Nelson to return at once
to the fleet to take charge of the detachment from the beginning.[207]
More urgent instructions from the Admiralty, dated May 2nd, reached St.
Vincent on the 19th, with news that a reinforcement of eight ships-of-
the-line was on its way from England. St. Vincent, recognising the
importance of this, at once hurried off a brig (“La Mutine”) to notify
Nelson that a reinforcement of ten ships would shortly reach him. On the
21st this detachment sailed under Captain Troubridge.[208] St. Vincent,
in his new instructions, charged Nelson to use “his utmost endeavours to
take, sink, burn or destroy” the French armada and authorised him to
exact supplies, whenever needed, from the territories of the various
Mediterranean powers.[209]

The “Mutine” joined Nelson. His little squadron had had so far a
checkered career. Sailing from Gibraltar on May 8th with, in addition to
the “Vanguard,” the “Orion” and the “Alexander” and five smaller
vessels, he made his dispositions for getting information.[210] On May
17th the capture of a French corvette gave him nothing but conflicting
reports as to what was going on at Toulon.[211] On the night of the
20th, a violent gale roughly handled the “Vanguard.” Fortunately for
Nelson, the other two ships escaped without much injury and the
“Vanguard” was taken in tow and was anchored under the Isle of St.
Pietro, at the southern extremity of Sardinia.[212] These delays made
possible the junction of Troubridge, of whose approach—thanks to the
dispersion of the frigates during the storm—Nelson was entirely
ignorant.[213]

On the very day that Nelson took shelter at St. Pietro, the French
armada was under sail along the coast of Corsica. The convoy of Genoa
joined it on May 21st and that of Corsica on the 27th.[214] On June 1st
and 3rd, it was learnt that Nelson was in command of three ships, that
he had suffered heavily in a storm and that he had left St. Pietro on
May 28th.[215] Bonaparte decided, under these circumstances, not to wait
for the convoy of Cività-Vecchia, but to attack Malta as soon as
possible. On June 9th he arrived before the island and found that Desaix
with the convoy from Cività-Vecchia had been waiting there for a few
days.

On the arrival of Desaix, a Maltese messenger went on board his ship to
convey the Grand Master’s welcome and to announce that no more than four
ships at a time could enter the port. This was, as General Belliard
frankly states in his diary, the very declaration desired by the French;
for it would serve as an excellent pretext for the use of force.[216]
Bonaparte, on his arrival, sent Marmont on shore to fetch the French
Consul, who confirmed the declaration already made to Desaix.[217]
Orders were at once given for operations at four points.[218]

These decided upon, other methods were also to be employed to gather the
fruits of the work of Poussielgue in the island. Treacherous letters,
couched in terms to allay the anxiety of the knights, were sent from,
amongst others, Dolomieu, the mineralogist and ex-knight, who was one of
the “savants” attached to the expedition.[219] The French Consul wrote
to the Grand Master that Bonaparte had decided to obtain by force what
should have been voluntarily offered.[220]

In spite of the fact that the Order had ample warning of what was to
befall it, all was confusion and treachery.[221] Malta, nevertheless,
had her fortifications deeply embedded in the live rock. The defenders
would have only to retire behind the ramparts and weary down the
attacking force, which would be sufficiently occupied with the difficult
task of demolishing the rocky bastions and with the problem of finding
fresh water in an arid coast. A delay of a few days might have seriously
compromised the success of the expedition. There now came to the help of
the French that sapping of all discipline and of all sentiments of
loyalty which their propaganda had helped to create, and one day of
skirmishes and another of negotiations sufficed to bring about the fall
of Malta.[222]

On June 11th, the Grand Master proposed a suspension of hostilities, to
which Bonaparte readily acceded.[223] Poussielgue and Dolomieu were then
sent to “sound the intentions of De Hompesch and report on the spirit of
the inhabitants.” The verbal instructions of these agents clearly prove
that the fall of Malta was not as inevitable as has been generally
supposed. Bonaparte was ready, if necessary, to concede to the extent of
preserving the sovereignty of the Order in return for the right of the
French to control the port and garrison the town of Valetta.[224] At
midnight, however, the envoys of the Grand Master arrived on board the
“Orient.” In half an hour was concluded the “convention” for the
capitulation of the Order and the end of its sovereignty over
Malta.[225] The next day the French entered the town, took possession of
the fortresses and anchored their fleet in the harbour. The news of this
first success reached Paris on July 1st, and was the more welcome as
great anxiety had been felt for the safety of the army under Bonaparte
when the presence of an English fleet in the Mediterranean became
known.[226]

Bonaparte remained for six days in Malta, during which he demonstrated
the full capacity of his organising genius. Entrusting the
administration of the island to a commission of nine and to a
commissioner appointed by the French Government,[227] he ordered an
inventory of all the property of the Order,[228] dispersed the
knights,[229] proclaimed the right of the Maltese to equality, abolished
feudal titles and slavery, prohibited the wearing of liveries, liberated
the Mohammedan galley-slaves, put an end to the representation of
foreign powers in the island,[230] expelled the monks and nuns, and gave
the vacated livings to native Maltese and set limits to religious
endowments.[231]

The fall of the Order, moreover, was bound to impress the Muslem powers
of the Mediterranean. Capital was also to be made of the liberation of
the Muslem galley-slaves, who were, for the most part, former subjects
of the Barbary regencies of North Africa.[232] General Chabot,
commanding at Corfu, was directed to announce the news to Ruffin at
Constantinople and to Aly of Janina.[233] Other French agents were
ordered to spread the news far and wide and especially among the Greeks
of the Morea.[234] Bonaparte, moreover, thought it useful at this stage
to gain over Aly and thus, in addition to abetting the rebel Paswan
Oglou of Widdin and to lulling the anxiety of the Porte by declaring the
aim of the expedition to be the punishment of the unruly beys, he would
be able to keep Turkey quiet. He, accordingly, sent his aide-de-camp,
Lavallette, to that Pasha and instructed him to impress Aly with the
strength of the forces under his command and with his power of
increasing his glory should he be willing to take the part of the
French.[235]

Leaving General Vaubois in command, Bonaparte sailed from Malta on June
19th.

As for Nelson, he proceeded on the 27th to search for the frigates which
had separated during the storm. Their absence hampered his operations
from the outset. On May 28th, for example, he learnt from a vessel out
of Marseilles of the sailing of the French armada and would have pursued
it had he possessed the frigates—indispensable for such a service.[236]
On June 5th, by the arrival of the “Mutine,” he learnt that he was to
expect the reinforcement “which converted his division into a fleet and
enlarged his mission from one of mere reconnaissance to the duty of
overtaking and destroying a great expedition.”[237] He also learnt that
the “Mutine” had fallen in with the frigates three days before on their
way to Gibraltar, persuaded that Nelson could not take the sea with his
impaired ships.[238] On June 7th, Troubridge, with the reinforcements,
effected his junction.[239] The French having had a long start, Nelson
would not delay an instant. “You may be assured,” he wrote to St.
Vincent, “I will fight them the moment I can reach their fleet, be they
at anchor, or under sail.”[240]

With this resolution, he began the wearisome chase of the French which
ended only in the victory of the Nile. He sailed towards Naples and
Sicily, points mentioned in St. Vincent’s instructions as probable, and
on June 14th learnt from a Tunisian cruiser, that ten days before the
French had been seen off the end of Sicily steering to the eastward. “If
they pass Sicily,” he wrote the next day, “I shall believe they are
going on their scheme of possessing Alexandria and getting troops to
India—a plan concerted with Tippoo Sahib, by no means so difficult as
might at first view be imagined.”[241] Sending Troubridge ahead to
Naples to communicate with Sir William Hamilton and with General Acton,
the Neapolitan Prime Minister, to gather information and to see what
help Naples would give, the squadron hove to in the Bay of Naples on
June 17th and Troubridge rejoined.[242] Naples sent assurances of good
will and promises of surreptitious help. The information gathered was
that the enemy had gone towards Malta and this gave direction to the
next move.[243] On the 22nd, however, Nelson learnt near the south-west
of Sicily, that the French had taken Malta on the 15th, and that their
fleet had sailed on the 16th for an unknown destination.[244] This was
not true, for, as we have seen, it was not till the 19th that the French
sailed from Malta. Guessing that Egypt was their destination, Nelson set
sail at once towards Alexandria. His reasons are clearly set forth in a
despatch to St. Vincent.[245] With information from Naples, so it ran,
that that kingdom was reassured by the French as to the aim of their
expedition,[246] with knowledge of the extent of the preparations, of
the presence of naturalists, astronomers and other “savants” and of the
easterly direction of the prevailing winds, he conjectured that their
aim was to establish a colony in Egypt and to use it as a basis for
operations against India. The opinion of these captains “in whom he
placed great confidence” confirmed him in his determination to sail to
Alexandria and to lose no time in frustrating the designs of the French.
On the 26th, the “Mutine” was sent ahead to deliver despatches to the
British Consul in Alexandria and to gather information.[247] Two days
later the squadron appeared off that town. Captain Hardy, of the
“Mutine,” reported that he was not able to obtain any news as to the
French.[248] Nelson was now to suffer from an excess of eagerness and
singleness of purpose. “His active and anxious mind,” wrote Captain
Berry, “would not permit him to rest a moment in the same place; he
therefore shaped his course to the northward for the coast of Caramania,
to reach as quickly as possible some quarter where information could
probably be obtained.”[249]

Only two days later the French armament appeared off the low-lying coast
of Egypt. Leaving Malta on June 19th, it did not take the direct route,
but skirted the south coast of Crete. It was off that island that its
Commander-in-chief learnt of the presence of seventeen English ships.
His anxiety as to the safety of the convoy was greatly increased. Twenty
days of navigation had demonstrated it as incapable of manœuvring for an
engagement as of effecting its escape.[250] On June 27th, the “Junon”
was sent ahead to fetch the French Consul at Alexandria. Rejoining the
fleet on the 29th, the “Junon” reported the appearance of an English
squadron of fourteen sail under Nelson.[251] This news changed
Bonaparte’s plans. His original intention was to enter the Delta by the
two mouths of the Nile. An examination of the strategic geography of
Egypt proves the soundness of this. As the possession of Cairo was the
immediate objective of the campaign, the obstacles were the desert and
the Nile. The Mamelukes could add to these natural obstacles a
formidable cavalry and a fairly strong river-flotilla. The French had,
consequently, to avoid the desert and the crossing of the river.
Damietta and Rosetta were, therefore, the proper points for the
disembarkation of the troops and the starting of the march on Cairo.
Alexandria, on the contrary, did not possess this advantage. It was then
almost completely cut off from the rest of Egypt by the encroaching of
the desert on the cultivated land.[252] The presence of the English
fleet, however, did not leave the French any other choice than land
where they were. Thus, if Nelson had failed to encounter the French
fleet, he was able—by the mere fact of his presence—to effect a not
unimportant result.

The disembarkation was effected under difficult circumstances a few
miles to the westward of Alexandria, and an immediate march on that town
was ordered.[253] The notables, alarmed by the appearance of the English
fleet, had, in the meantime, appealed to the Mameluke governor of the
neighbouring province for help.[254] A Turkish ship-of-the-line and a
few frigates anchored in the harbour had, at the appearance of the
English, begun to send their guns ashore.[255] The Beys at Cairo treated
the matter lightly. They declared their perfect readiness to trample
with their horses over all the “Franks” of the Earth.[256] The mob of
Mamelukes, ferocious Beduins and simple peasants armed with long flails
gathered for the defence of Egypt’s first port was quickly dispersed by
French advanced detachments, and before the evening of July 2nd, the
French were masters of the place. There does not seem to be any
foundation for the reports of a massacre of the inhabitants.[257]

Bonaparte now put into execution the long-matured policy of propitiating
the Egyptians; the French were to appear to them rather as liberators
than as conquerors, and as if they had come to put an end to the tyranny
of a caste recruited from the slaves of Caucasia, and to ensure to the
oppressed cultivator of the soil the fruit of his toil. They were also
to demonstrate that their presence in Egypt would not at all affect the
friendly relations between Turkey and France, for the Porte had been as
much as the French the victim of its nominal vassals. While still at
sea, the soldiers were admonished to respect the religion of the
Egyptians and the “muftis.” “The peoples,” so the soldiers were told,
“whom we are about to encounter treat women differently from us, but in
every country the man who wrongs a woman is a scoundrel. Plunder
enriches only a few and dishonours all; it destroys our resources and
makes us hated by those whom it is to our interest to have as friends.
The first town on our route was built by Alexander. Every step we take
will awaken memories of great deeds worthy of exciting the emulation of
Frenchmen.”[258] But his profound study of the Koran, which, together
with the Bible, the Vedas, and Montesquieu’s works, he grouped in his
library catalogue under the heading of “Politics,” had borne fruit in
the remarkable proclamation in Arabic addressed to the Egyptians.[259]
It must be stated, however, that the French original composed by
Bonaparte is truer to the style and spirit of the Koran than the
Levantine interpreters’ Arabic version.[260] The intermediaries between
the people and the French were to be the “Ulema,” who were the nearest
approach to a clergy and who were favoured by Bonaparte on account of
their pacific dispositions and their relative enlightenment.[261] As
regards the Sublime Porte, he was careful to preserve the symbols of its
suzerainty. Villages, for instance, were to fly the Turkish as well as
the French flag.[262] The Pasha of Egypt—as a fellow sufferer from
Mameluke violence—was requested to join Bonaparte and “curse with him
the impious race of the Beys.”[263] The commander of the Turkish ship-
of-war was not allowed to sail, but was reassured as to the friendly
intentions of the French.[264] The French envoy at Constantinople was
directed to convey similar assurances to the Porte and was informed of
the speedy arrival of an ambassador.[265]

Without absolutely convincing the Egyptians, the proclamation, at any
rate, seemed to appease them, especially that portion of it professing
friendliness to the Commander of the Faithful. Under the influence of
their religious leaders, who were sincerely opposed to any violence, and
submitting to the “right” of the strongest, they easily transferred from
Mamelukes to French the signs of an allegiance, which, at heart, they
felt for neither.

The French now began to suffer all the miseries of disenchantment. The
“town built by Alexander” of which their general spoke so eloquently was
only a twelfth part of what it had been in the days when his Macedonian
model had given it his name; the rest of the city was simply refuse and
ruins surrounded by arid stretches. The six acres promised at Toulon
well seemed to the soldier an undesirable domain. The ferocious Beduin,
ever on the alert to cut off stragglers, cured the disciples of Jean
Jacques of their admiration for the “natural man.”[266]

The disillusionment was further embittered by the sufferings of the
troops on their march towards Cairo. Breaking camp on July 7th,
Bonaparte sent the main body of his army along the desert road instead
of the more comfortable but longer one by Rosetta and the banks of the
Nile.[267] Only one division, that under Dugua, was sent by the longer
route, while a flotilla, under Perrée, was to ascend the river from
Rosetta.[268] The troops suffered all the horrors of a desert
march—hunger, thirst, and heat.[269] In the miserable villages there
were none of the appliances of civilisation; grain there was in
abundance, but neither mills nor ovens, and nothing to drink but the
slimy water of the cisterns. After tasting the delights of Capua, they
had now to content themselves with water-melons, of which they partook
so ravenously as to justify the contemporary English cartoonist who
represented the ghost of Bonaparte attributing the destruction of his
legions to the water-melon and the Arab.[270] The officers encouraged
their soldiers in expressing their discontent.[271] These murmured,
threatened to mutiny, and scoffed at the “savants” whom they believed to
have deceived their general. Stories of the indignities to which the
Beduins subjected their prisoners kept the highly strung soldiers on the
alert all the time. The strain drove many to commit suicide, even in the
presence of the general.[272] The latter, by word and deed, did all he
could to restore the _morale_ of the troops. Passing near a group of
disaffected men, he was accosted: “Well, General, are you going to take
us to India?” “No,” was his cutting reply, “it is not with your likes
that I should undertake the journey.”[273]

On hearing of the landing of the French, the Beys and notables had
decided to write at once to Constantinople for help, and gave the
command to Murad. The latter marched with his cavalry towards the enemy
after giving orders for the construction of a thick chain across the
river.[274] The first “affair” between the Mamelukes and the French took
place on July 10th, and was described by General Belliard as a
“joke.”[275] It restored, however, the confidence of the French soldier
and showed him that his magnificently accoutred enemies who tempted his
cupidity, could effect nothing against the “square” formation adopted by
his general. Three days later there was a second engagement of a more
serious character.[276] The Mamelukes, dazzling in gold and silver,
armed with pistols and carabines of the best English make and with the
finest sabres of the East and mounted on horses of the purest breed,
dashed upon the squares, caracoled round the flanks seeking a feeble
point, but were stopped everywhere by a wall of raised bayonets, and
some of the bravest expired within the French lines. The Mamelukes at
last retired. In the meantime a lively engagement took place between the
flotillas in which the Mamelukes acquitted themselves with honour and
would have been victorious had it not been for the advance of the French
troops towards the bank of the river and the sail of one of their ships
catching fire accidentally.[277]

The news of these defeats reached Cairo on July 15th.[278] The panic of
the populace knew no bounds. The gossipmongers abandoned the bazaars and
burglars plied their nefarious trade. The proclamation of Bonaparte
spread by the liberated galley-slaves, whom the populace believed to be
as much infidel as the French, did not produce the desired effect.[279]
The Beys did not take the trouble to employ a spy or a scout to discover
the route taken by the French and showed themselves not less panic
stricken than the people. Ibrahim Bey, Murad’s rival, utterly in the
dark as to the movements of the French, decided to remain on the Cairo
side of the river. The Government of the Mamelukes had tumbled down. The
Beduins emerged from their desert retreats and began pillaging the
settled population, and villages renewed their traditional feuds. The
“Franks” and the other Christians were subjected to insults and threats.
Murad had, on the 16th, encamped at the village of Embabeh, on the left
bank of the Nile, opposite Cairo. He thus committed the fatal mistake of
not placing the river between himself and his enemies and thus imposing
upon them the necessity of finding means for crossing it. His superior
flotilla could have seriously hampered that operation and his cavalry
could have fallen upon the French detachments as they effected their
landing on the other side.[280]

It was not to be, and in the engagement which took place on July 18th
and which marked the last effort of mediæval warfare, the famous cavalry
was shattered by the world’s greatest soldier, and the most
indispensable condition for the rise of a New Egypt was realised.[281]

The “Battle of the Pyramids” delivered Cairo into the hands of the
French. Ibrahim, unable to go to the rescue of his brethren on the other
bank, had departed with his cavalry, his women and his riches towards
Syria. Others, who could, had followed his example. The “Ulema” of the
Azhar University communicated on July 21st with Bonaparte, who repeated
his former assurances and entered Cairo on July 24th.[282]

The Mamelukes, having dispersed in all directions, he hastened to extend
the dominion of the French over the chief provincial towns and to re-
establish his communications with the coast which, during the march on
Cairo, had been cut off by the Beduins and the peasants.[283] He
attempted, moreover, to open negotiations with Murad, who after his
defeat had blown up his flotilla and retreated towards Upper Egypt.
These negotiations would give Bonaparte time to destroy the other
Mameluke chief, Ibrahim, who was then lingering on the eastern frontier
of Egypt to lay hands on the richly laden caravan from Mecca.[284]
Accordingly Rosetti, the ex-Imperial consul and the confidant of the
Beys, was instructed to offer Murad the province of Girgeh up to the
cataract in return for his acknowledging the suzerainty of the French,
paying them the “miri,” or tax on land, and keeping no more than 500
cavalrymen.[285] But inklings of the great victory of Nelson seemed to
have reached Murad, who rejected Bonaparte’s terms and offered, in his
turn, to make the French a money payment if they consented to leave
Egypt.[286]

Before he knew of this answer, Bonaparte had sent his troops in movement
against Ibrahim. On August 11th the French cavalry had a lively
engagement with the Mamelukes, and Ibrahim was able, thanks to the delay
of the French infantry in coming up to battle, to effect his escape with
all his immense baggage into Syria.[287]

It was on his way back to Cairo, after making a futile overture to
Ibrahim Bey, that Bonaparte learnt of the destruction of the French
fleet in the Bay of Abukir.[288]

 III

 THE ANGLO-TURKISH ALLIANCE


After Brueys’ squadron had successfully escorted the convoy, the
important question as to its ulterior _rôle_ had to be settled. As long
as the conquest of Egypt was not achieved there was a good reason for
keeping the fleet near at hand. To station it, moreover, on the Egyptian
coast would facilitate the arrival of the expected second convoy. But
such a position, on the other hand, exposed the fleet to serious
risks.[289] How did Bonaparte conciliate two such conflicting interests?
The question has given rise to a wearisome controversy, but the evidence
of contemporary documents is unequivocal.

Immediately on the fall of Alexandria, Brueys was directed to anchor his
ships in the old harbour. But should careful sounding prove that there
was not enough depth and should he be unable to take a military position
in the Bay of Abukir, he was to set sail for Corfu.[290] Brueys, on the
same day, wrote that the line-of-battle ships could not enter the
harbour, and that the Bay of Abukir would provide a good shelter,
especially in summer.[291] There is evidence that the immediate
departure of the fleet to Corfu, which was certainly the original
arrangement,[292] would have materially deranged Bonaparte’s plans, for
he was counting on a speedy return to Europe. It was arranged before his
departure that on the termination of the Egyptian Expedition he should
resume the command of the combined forces for the invasion of
England.[293] There is a despatch dated April 13th and addressed to the
Directory in which he calculates that after “the termination of the
Mediterranean Expedition” the fourteen sail-of-the-line under Brueys
thus available would join the Brest squadron and so bring up the number
by October or November (when he expected the Egyptian campaign to be
over) to fifty war vessels. It would then be possible to take 40,000
troops to some point on the English coast, while other 40,000 were
preparing to embark, and the Dutch fleet with 10,000 troops could
threaten Scotland. In this way the landing in England was almost certain
to be in November or December, as the English would be obliged, by the
expedition in the East, to detach part of their Channel fleet for India
and the Red Sea.[294] Who but himself could carry out such an invasion?
At Toulon he addressed the troops, “You are a wing of the Army of
England!” and in his orders he occasionally signed himself “Commander-
in-Chief of the Army of England.” His secretary, Bourrienne, affirms
this. In letters to his brother Joseph he spoke of his intention to
return in the Autumn of 1798 and asked him to purchase for him an estate
in Burgundy.[295]

Jaubert, a naval employee, explicitly states in a confidential letter to
the Minister of Marine which was afterwards intercepted and published by
the English Government, that it was by Bonaparte’s order that the fleet
did not sail to Corfu, and adds, “Nous sommes ici sous le vent du
fatalisme, et son souffle ébranle un peu mes principes.”[296] Brueys,
accordingly, anchored his squadron in the open roadstead of Abukir.
Artillery officers were sent to strengthen his position by the
establishing of land-batteries,[297] but they were of opinion that the
fleet could not count on much support from the shore.[298]

The sounding operations were, meanwhile, in full activity. On July 13th
it was reported that the fleet could enter the old harbour.[299] Brueys,
however, was not convinced.[300]

His correspondence reveals the dearth of provisions in the fleet, which
prevented him from as much as sending a frigate to gather information on
the movements of the English forces, of whose presence he had received
various reports,[301] the unworthiness of the soldiers serving as ships’
garrisons,[302] the bad health from which he was suffering[303] and
which was, in all probability, the cause of his not taking measures to
render his position more secure, and his belief that he could not sail
from Egypt without an express order from Bonaparte.[304] Such an order
was never sent. After the battle, Bonaparte, in a long despatch to the
Directory, ignores that it was he who suggested the alternative of
anchoring in the roadstead of Abukir, and declares his surprise when he
heard, on July 27th, that the fleet was still there.[305] On July 30th,
the despatch continued, Jullien, an aide-de-camp, was sent with
instructions for Brueys to sail immediately. Jullien was, however,
murdered _en route_. But on that very July 30th Bonaparte was still
hoping that the sounding operations would prove a success, and expressed
his belief that the English, who had only an inferior force, would be
contented with the blockade of Malta.[306] Moreover, if his intention on
that day was that the fleet should sail for Corfu, nothing would have
been more natural than that he should have informed General Chabot, the
Governor of the place, of it. Writing to him on August 3rd, he speaks of
establishing trade relations, but not a word of Brueys’ fleet.[307]

The only possible conclusion, therefore, is that if he ever entertained
the idea of sending the fleet away from Egypt he preserved till the very
end the hope of finding another course of action more subservient to his
designs.

After beating back from Egypt and “spreading the fleet over to the coast
of Asia,” Nelson anchored at Syracuse on July 19th. “Having gone,” he
wrote to Sir William Hamilton, “a round of 600 leagues at this season of
the year with an expedition incredible, here I am as ignorant of the
situation of the enemy as I was twenty-seven days ago.”[308] Persuaded,
however, that the French were somewhere in the Levant,[309] that they
were neither (so he learnt from spoken vessels) to the westward of
Sicily nor at Corfu,[310] he sailed from Syracuse on July 25th and sent
Troubridge on the 28th to look into the Gulf of Koron. The latter
rejoined with the information that the French were seen four weeks
before steering in a south-easterly direction off the coast of
Crete.[311] The fleet immediately bore up under all sail and in the
afternoon of August 1st the long-sought enemy was discovered anchored in
Abukir Bay.

Nelson, with the eye of genius, at once observed, “that where there was
room for our enemy’s ships to swing, there was room for one of ours to
anchor.”[312] His ships manœuvred with such consummate skill that some
of them were able to pass between their opponents and the shore. Caught
thus between two fires, the French were destroyed one after the other.
Two ships-of-the-line and two frigates were all that Villeneuve could
save by flight.

In considering the causes of the disaster, it is necessary to go beyond
the immediate circumstances into the more far-reaching factors, to go,
for instance, beyond the explosion of the flagship “L’Orient” and the
heroic death of Brueys, the non-participation of the rear under
Villeneuve in the action and the resolution not to fight under
sail—important as all these were—into the many deficiencies of a
disorganised service which led Brueys to exclaim: “Je trouve qu’il faut
bien du courage pour se charger de conduire des flottes aussi mal-
outillées.”[313]

As for Nelson, there now remained to gather up the spoils of the field,
and to realise the consequences of the battle, great and small, near and
remote. Little by little, he grasped the greatness of what he had
accomplished. After the victory, he announced his intention of sailing
down the Mediterranean on the 19th,[314] but batches of letters from the
French began to fall into his hands. The “Intercepted Correspondence”
thus early started playing its important—and hitherto unnoticed—_rôle_;
namely, that of influencing the opinions of British statesmen and men of
action and of shaping their decisions. These letters—written under the
stress of privation and home-sickness—showed how bitter was the
disillusionment, how widespread was the disaffection and how dispirited
were the troops. The eager enemy did not allow for the obvious
exaggeration of disenchanted warriors, but took the wailing at its full
face-value. Under these circumstances, Nelson realised how much his
victory would mean to the French. Had he possessed a few bomb-ships, he
would have destroyed the transports in the harbour of Alexandria.[315]
As it was, he hastened to inform British India. Writing to the Governor
of Bombay, he summed up briefly the events of the past few months and
the number and character of the French army in Egypt, and expressed the
hope that special care would be exercised against the departure of ships
from India, to convey this huge force thither by the Red Sea. On the
side of the Mediterranean, their fate was settled by the recent victory.
They could receive nothing from France, they could not advance freely
into Syria, as water transport was essential for much of their
equipment; even in Egypt itself they were hampered by the difficulties
of communication—on land by the guerilla hostility of the Egyptians, and
now on the water through his own presence and control. The Nile, through
its Rosetta mouth, had been heretofore the easiest communication between
Cairo and Alexandria. The garrison of the latter depended largely for
daily bread upon this route, now closed by the fleet in Abukir Bay. By
land nothing short of a regiment could pass over ground where, even
before the battle, the French watering parties from the ships had to be
protected by heavy armed bodies. He intended, therefore, to remain where
he was as long as possible.[316] On the 15th, however, most secret and
urgent orders came to him from St. Vincent, to return to the Westward
and co-operate in the reduction of Minorca.[317] By that time, several
of the lost frigates had joined him. Three of these with three ships-of-
the-line were left with Captain Hood to carry on the blockade of
Alexandria and to suppress the enemy’s communications by water along the
coasts of Egypt and Syria.[318] That, to all intents and purposes, was
to be England’s contribution. Turkey was to do the rest, and this was
not great. The Porte, according to Nelson, would have only to send a few
ships of war and the transports in the harbour of Alexandria would be
all destroyed, and 10,000 troops and the town itself would be
captured.[319] He declared himself ready, if Naples would give him the
bomb-ships, to return and do the work. The three countries, England,
Turkey and Naples, had only to exert[320] themselves for three months
and the French might be destroyed. On the 19th, he sailed for Naples.

On August 22nd the news of his great victory reached Constantinople,
where it helped to end the wavering of the Porte and to confirm it in
the decisions it had already taken. The Porte’s suspicions of the
intentions of the French had been aroused by Bonaparte’s intrigues with
the disaffected Greeks, the Pasha of Janina and other Albanian chiefs,
and the assurances of the French Ambassador that Bonaparte’s ardent
proclamations were only polite answers to letters he had received, could
not deceive even the Turks.[321] The extensive preparations for the
Egyptian Expedition therefore could not but greatly alarm them. The
Porte, however, did not feel itself strong enough to demand an
explanation.[322] The most disquieting rumours were spread and were
readily believed by the populace, which was greatly excited by the
symptoms of concern and anxiety observed on the faces of the members of
the Divan.[323] Turkey had to choose between three courses of action,
firstly, to oppose openly and by force of arms the French Expedition;
secondly, to enter into an alliance with the Republic; and thirdly, to
profess acquiescence in the retention of Egypt by the French, but to
throw, secretly, every impediment in their way. As for the first course
the Porte had more than one reason not to take it. For if it did it had
to contract alliances with its hereditary enemies and expose the
security of the heart of the Empire to the menace of Russian and
Austrian troops marching there ostensibly for its succour. Moreover, the
French troops stationed in the Ionian Islands could easily march on
Constantinople and punish the Porte before the arrival of its allies.
The discontent of the Greeks and the rebellion of Paswan Oglou fomented
by the French were additional obstacles. The Porte, however, could not
with any pretence to dignity take the second course. The third was more
conformable to the spirit of the Turkish Government. Its characteristic
in political affairs was indecision and a method of uniformly acting in
such a way as might subsequently be explained in accordance with its
interests. The nature of the Government was such that, in all parts of
the Empire, and more particularly in Egypt, the Porte could send orders
which it never meant should be carried out, nay, it could send orders
which were meant to be resisted. The Porte could, consequently, set
against the French in Egypt armies from Arabia and Syria as effectively
as if it had declared war and answer the protests of the French
Government by declaring its inability to control the unruly pashas and
showering imprecations on their heads. This policy would, moreover,
result in weakening the vassals to such an extent as would enable the
central Government to reassert its authority.[324]

We find, accordingly, that while the Grand Signior was solemnly
declaring that he would not give “a handful of sand of the Egyptian
territory,” calling upon the Beys not to place any belief in the
stratagems of these infidels, and promising to give them every
succour,[325] proclamations were sent to the provinces to the effect
that the French were still the friends of Turkey and should be well
treated, in spite of the fact that “a certain wretch called Bonaparte”
had invaded Egypt, for that general, being a rebel to his own
Government, had abused its confidence and attacked Egypt with troops
intended for another enterprise![326]

In this way the French had some chance of preventing a formal rupture.
Received by the Reis Effendi on June 19th, their envoy—only a _chargé
d’affaires_ whose financial embarrassments had diminished his
influence—was placed in a difficult position by his lack of official
information and instructions from his Government.[327] In this and other
audiences, however, Ruffin adopted the policy of letting the Turks infer
that an Ambassador would soon arrive at Constantinople. The Porte was
obviously pleased at this[328]; but its hesitation did not come to an
end. “There was,” wrote Spencer Smith, the English Minister, “nothing
but council upon council and conference upon conference without any
visible result.”[329] Spencer Smith was not idle in “abridging” these
deliberations, or, in his own words, he “was not backward in taking a
due share in meeting every demonstration that a palpable community of
interests can have inspired, nor in taking upon himself a due share of
responsibility in the language of his assurances and offers in
reply.”[330] Other circumstances came to his help. The populace at
Constantinople, alarmed for the safety of Islam and discontented with
the manipulation of the prices of articles imported from Egypt by
monopolists and “profiteers,”[331] began to murmur at the suspected
connivance of the Porte in the schemes of the infidels.[332] Rumours of
the presence of a formidable English squadron and even of its having
obtained a great victory over the French, of the determination of the
Beys to defend Egypt at all costs, of their having assembled as many as
50,000 men and of having embodied an Arab militia 100,000 strong, were
widely circulated, and the people were overjoyed.[333]

The Russian mission, “which naturally fixed the attention and confidence
of the preponderant connection of this Empire,” and which had hitherto
“manifested the least share in the hustle of the season,”[334] began to
show signs of activity. In spite of the fact that Paul had started his
reign with a violent fit of “burning all that his Mother, Catherine, had
adored,” Russian policy in Europe and Asia derived its springs of action
from the very nature of things and was marked by a singular continuity
under the most varying _régimes_ of insane emperors, of foreign-born
ministers, and of eccentric empresses. While declaiming against the
Revolution, “subversive of all law, of rights, of religion and of
morality,”[335] Paul remained at peace with France. The occupation of
the Ionian Islands and the formation of a Polish legion in Italy,
however, disquietened him. He feared the extension of French influence
in the Near East to the detriment of the Russian monopoly and the
contamination of Holy Russia by contact with revolutionary doctrines,
and began to declare the necessity of “taming the French in the
interests of general tranquillity and of thwarting their ambitious
designs.”[336] The conquest of Malta by the Republic decided him, and
generous offers of help were sent to Turkey. On July 24th, “the Russian
envoy had his expected conference of several hours with the Reis
Effendi.”[337]

Four days later it was the English minister’s turn.[338] In an interview
“lasting from nine till five in the afternoon and marked by extreme
cordiality on both sides,” the Turkish Minister, after giving assurances
of doing all that was possible for the English fleet in the
Mediterranean, expressed his regret that the real intentions of the
Porte were misunderstood and that its conduct was only the result of
existing circumstances and its desire to enter into an “intimate and
confidential connection with Great Britain.” The Turk expatiated—obvious
tactics—on the dangers to British India from the presence of the French
in Egypt, while Smith, on the other hand, took for his text that it was
the duty of him upon whom the storm first burst to be duly prepared.
After this dialectic, the Reis Effendi came to the point and inquired if
Smith “had any powers to meet him in such a negotiation which he was
sufficiently authorised to be able to conclude this very morning.” Smith
obviously had no such powers. He pointed out, however, that his
Government—at such a distance—“must naturally have a large measure of
confidence in his discretion,” and offered, on condition of its being
kept confidential and of its not being considered as binding on his
Court, to draw up a project of a treaty of alliance, which, after the
necessary modifications by the Divan, would be returned to him as an
overture from the Porte to His Britannic Majesty.

The project stipulated that Turkey would engage to oppose to the French
an army of 100,000 men and its whole Navy, and that Great Britain, on
the other hand, would undertake to maintain in the Levant a
“respectable” fleet and not to lay down arms till the integrity of the
Ottoman Empire had been secured.[339]

Good progress was, in this way, accomplished. But still “there was a
spoke in the wheel.” As late as August 3rd, a council of state was still
“considering the Russian business” and the “fetva” or dispensation
necessary to enable the Grand Signior to declare War.[340] The Porte
still wavered between French “friendship” and Russian alliance, and was
loth to start a veritable diplomatic revolution. “It was evident that
some high-placed Turks considered that the French solely required a
passage through Egypt and that this really interested England more than
the Porte.”[341] It was amidst this hesitation that the news of Nelson’s
victory arrived and the first batches of the gloomy correspondence of
the French Army. The Porte took courage. “Bonaparte,” wrote Prince
Ypsilanti, its dragoman, “did not believe that fortune would abandon
him. From the intercepted letters you will gather the deplorable
condition of the French Navy and the fact that their Army, too weak to
advance, has been brought to a standstill between Rosetta and
Alexandria.”[342] The people began to murmur loudly.[343] An old woman,
whose belongings were destroyed in a great fire, boldly interpellated
the Commander of the Faithful himself, and attributed the misfortune to
his hesitation to declare war on the infidels, who have violated Egypt,
the “Key to the two Holy Cities.” The effect was immediate. The Grand
Vizier and the Grand Mufti, both in the “French interest,” were deposed
and sent into exile. Another fire demolished the house of Prince
Ypsilanti himself. The Porte could hesitate no longer. “The next twenty-
four hours brought forth the solemn ‘fetva’ of the pontiff and the
indignant declaration of the Sovereign against the invaders of the
Empire.” “We go on since ‘au pas de géant’,” wrote the English
Minister.[344] At the end of September, the French envoy with his
countrymen were sent to the Castle of Seven Towers, which meant, in the
usages of Turkish “international law,” that the Porte had declared
war.[345] Early in December it issued its anti-French manifesto.

As for England, while the Cabinet was divided, as was seen, in its
conjectures as to the destination of the expedition, two reports,
without absolutely convincing it, gave weight to Dundas’ anxiety for
British India.[346] One was from the British Consul at Leghorn and the
other from an agent in Frankfurt. Dundas’ mind had already been made up.
Ever since his connection with the administration of India, he wrote, he
had “been at great pains to collect information by every means and had
long made up his mind to a conviction that the possession of Egypt by
any great European power would be a fatal circumstance to the interests
of this country.”[347] Here we have one of the earliest, if not the
earliest, formulations of what has come to be one of the commonplaces of
diplomacy. He had even composed several memoranda on the subject, but
they have become dispersed. He, therefore, though expecting great
results from sending Nelson to the Mediterranean, thought that other
measures were necessary for the security of British India, for Bonaparte
might (as he actually did) give Nelson the slip and save his army. The
danger to India, moreover, would not be removed, if the French were, for
the moment, satisfied with Egypt only. “The accomplished feat would
still be a masterly stroke, nay, the most masterly stroke they have ever
yet done for their future aggrandizement.”[348] After examining the
available routes for an invasion of India, Dundas discussed the
measures. If the Turks had really consented to the invasion of their
territory, England was to set the Russians upon them. In any case, the
Government of India should be quickly apprised and a naval force should
be despatched to cruise at the “mouth of the Red Sea” or of the Persian
Gulf. The border tribesmen, moreover, could be persuaded to destroy the
French _en route_. The best measure, however, would be to reinforce the
European troops in India. Three days after he was able to announce to
Mornington—better known as Wellesley—a speedy reinforcement of 4500
troops.[349]

In face of this praiseworthy vigour, even Grenville’s scepticism had to
give way. But the landing of the French in Egypt was daily receiving
fresh confirmation. On August 20th, a despatch from Smith based on the
testimony of the Turkish harbour-master of Alexandria, left no room for
doubt. The feelings on this occasion were mixed. There was the
satisfaction at the escape of the British Isles, and especially Ireland,
from invasion;[350] there were, on the other hand, the anxiety at the
absence of news from Nelson and the fear that Turkey, even if she was to
declare war on France, would be nothing more than a burden.[351] The
Cabinet was not, under these circumstances, without the benefit of
“expert” advice.

Colonel Fullarton, who had seen service in India during the
administration of Lord Cornwallis, submitted a memorandum on the
subject.[352] Discussing the various possibilities of success and the
measures that could be taken by the presidencies, for instance, the
barring of the Bab-el-Mandeb Straits by a naval force, he considered the
steps to be taken nearer home. These almost entirely consisted in
rendering the Turkish Army more efficient by incorporating in it
regiments of Albanians under experienced British officers. Fullarton was
also one of the first to state the interest of Great Britain in
controlling Egypt. “It is taken for granted,” he wrote, “that the
possession of a permanent establishment in Egypt is essential to the
preservation of the English Empire in the East, and, when coupled with
the naval superiority of England, affords the only effectual means of
preventing the French from disturbing that Empire. . . . In a
transaction so novel and alarming as the present, if the daring and
powerfully combined projects of the French are opposed by old and
ordinary routine, as they have been by the continental powers, the
result must be such as already has disgraced every nation in Europe
except Great Britain.”

A second memorandum on the same subject was written by no other than
Warren Hastings, and was submitted directly to the King.[353] The
opening words are remarkable: “My official situation in India led me,
many years ago, to contemplate Egypt as affording, by the position of
the isthmus of Suez between the two seas, which form the communication
with the Atlantic and Indian Oceans, greater commercial advantages than
any other land upon the globe.” He stated that from the very first he
guessed that Egypt was the destination of the Expedition and believed
that France would offer Turkey in compensation for “so capital though
unprofitable a portion of the Mahomedan system, the conquest and
restitution of the Crimea and an offensive and defensive alliance
against the Emperors of Russia and Germany or either.” The measure he
suggested for defeating the French design was the occupation and
fortification of the little island of Perim and, in this way, the
Straits of Bab-el-Mandeb would be effectively guarded.

Under the influence of communications such as these and of the knowledge
of the Porte’s inclination to enter into more intimate relations with
Great Britain, Grenville drew up his programme for thwarting French
designs in the East. Of this programme it may be generally observed that
it was a middle course between Dundas’ desire for intervening in Egypt
with a British force and Spencer’s limitation of England’s _rôle_ to a
naval blockade.

Early in September, Grenville suggested to the First Lord of the
Admiralty sending a special Naval Contingent under Sir Sidney Smith—the
_protégé_ of the Grenvilles and the “expert” in Turkish affairs—to co-
operate with the Turks. Lord Spencer could not see his way to
accept.[354] He declared that the English forces in the Mediterranean
exceeded by a third his most sanguine expectations. But he passed over
in silence what was probably his real reason for rejecting the
suggestion, namely, his knowledge of the strong dislike felt by St.
Vincent and Nelson for Sidney Smith. A few days later, the news of
definite overtures from the Porte and of the attitude adopted by Russia
towards its old enemy led Grenville to reopen the subject of co-
operation with the Turks.[355] But all that Spencer offered to do was to
write to St. Vincent “an outline of the Cabinet’s ideas as to the
employment of the fleet in the Mediterranean.” He was, however, willing
to send officers to guide Turkish naval operations and thought that
there were many reasons for preferring Sir Sidney and that the
difficulties in the way were not insurmountable.[356]

Grenville’s view prevailed. Sidney Smith was to proceed to
Constantinople with the eighty-gun ship, “Le Tigre,” and was empowered
by his instructions to “take the command of such of His Majesty’s ships
as he may find in those seas, unless by any unforeseen accident it
should happen that there should be among them any of His Majesty’s
officers of a superior rank,” and was directed to act with such force in
conjunction with the Russian and Ottoman squadrons for the defence of
the Ottoman Empire and for the annoyance of the enemy in that
quarter.”[357] As a mark of special favour to Sir Sidney and to give him
a commanding position in the councils of the Associated Powers, he was
accredited in a diplomatic capacity[358] and was joined with his
brother, Spencer Smith, in the “full powers” instrument.[359]

After these resolutions had been taken, the news of the victory of the
Nile arrived at London (October 2nd). Lord Spencer thought that “there
is not now the occasion there appeared before for sending another ship
to the Mediterranean,” that “Nelson has taken the only measure which in
his state he could take and which appears the only one required for the
present of blocking up Alexandria and the mouth of the Nile by a good
squadron; and by that means entirely cutting off the French Army from
its supplies, of which all the intercepted letters represent them as
being in the most pressing want,” that “the Turk has decidedly taken his
part in a most magnificent declaration of war,” that the Russians have
sent supernumerary officers to guide Turkish operations, and, finally,
that “everything seems to be in so perfectly good a train in the
Levant.”[360] In accordance with this news St. Vincent was instructed
that the Mediterranean Fleet should perform four services: the
protection of the kingdom of Naples and, in the event of a war in Italy,
co-operation with the armies of Austria and Naples, the cutting off of
all communications between France and Egypt, the siege of Malta, and
lastly, co-operation with Turkish and Russian squadrons in the
Archipelago. Of these four duties, the first was declared the most
important.[361] Grenville wavered, but it was only for a moment.[362]

Thus was executed one item in his programme. The next one was calculated
to satisfy to some extent the requirements of Dundas, namely, to send
Colonel Koehler, who had distinguished himself during the siege of
Gibraltar by the Spaniards and who had been “formerly employed by
Grenville on a confidential mission” in Turkey,[363] with a staff of
officers and a present of artillery, to render the Turkish Army more
efficient. Pitt liked the idea and would have preferred Lord Mulgrave to
undertake the mission.[364] Submitting his views to Grenville, Koehler
declared that this was not the time for revolutionising the military
system of Turkey and that his work would consist in the formation of a
corps of Janissaries with a European field artillery and officered by
Europeans “which would serve as a steady and permanent defence to check
the ardour of pursuit and as a foundation and as a support to rally
upon,” and in helping the Turks “to consider every means practicable to
harass, alarm, wear out and dispirit as well as diminish by fatigues and
perpetual attacks an army devoid of every possible resource of receiving
reinforcements from their country until they are found to be reduced to
such a situation as to be fallen upon in all directions by one and
general impetuous attack and with a moral certainty of total destruction
which must sooner or later happen and most probably in a very short
time.”[365] Grenville expressed himself in full accord with these
views.[366] But the Duke of York—the Commander-in-Chief—“entirely
disapproved of the whole business.”[367] He thought that there was great
risk in giving a man like Koehler such ample discretion, that he would
disappoint the hopes he had raised, and that the officers selected were
young and raw and one of them was a lad of seventeen.[368] Shortly
after, however, the Duke withdrew his objections and here again
Grenville’s view prevailed.[369]

Koehler, who was now given the rank of Brigadier-General, was instructed
to offer every aid he could for “the defeat and destruction of the
French Army in Egypt and also of their transports and other vessels in
the port of Alexandria,” and to be “particularly solicitous to direct
the efforts of the Porte in such a manner as may destroy even the
probability of General Bonaparte’s using any part of his force to the
annoyance of the British dominions in India.”[370] His departure for
Constantinople, however, was delayed by sanguine anticipations nourished
by rumours of the assassination of Bonaparte in a tumult in Cairo.
Dundas wrote to Grenville remonstrating against the folly of accepting
mere conjecture for ascertained fact,[371] and Koehler left England in
the early days of December.

As regards the project of alliance sent by Spencer Smith, Grenville
thought “that it will be better on account both of the friendship
subsisting between the Emperor of Russia and His Majesty and of the
public and ostensible union which should subsist between powers acting
in concert against the common enemy to give the treaty rather the form
of an accession on the part of England to the alliance between Turkey
and Russia than that of distinct and separate negotiations.”[372] Hence
the differences between the _projet_ and the _contre-projet_ drawn by
Grenville. While Turkey engaged to raise at least an army of 100,000
troops and to equip naval forces for co-operation with those of the
allies, England undertook to maintain such naval forces in the
Mediterranean as would suffice, with those of the allies, “to annoy the
common enemy and frustrate his designs and, above all, to prevent any
attack on the dominions of the Ottoman Porte.”[373] The “mutual
guarantee” article was so interpreted as to cover either “the
possessions of each party as they stood at the commencement of the war
or as they may stand at the conclusion of a peace made by common
consent.”[374] Fearing that Turkish activity would slacken or cease with
the defeat of Bonaparte in Egypt or with his departure for India,
Grenville instructed Spencer Smith to take all measures to procure the
expulsion of French subjects from the territories of the Sultan and thus
secure the alliance against their intrigues.[375]

On November 11th, the _contra-projet_ was communicated to the Reis
Effendi, and Smith asked to know the stage reached by the Russo-Turkish
negotiations. After a long discussion, he found out that they had come
to a standstill over the amount of the subsidy to be paid to Russia. The
dispute was referred to Paul,[376] whose answer, entirely satisfactory
to the Porte, arrived on December 29th.[377] By that time Sir Sidney
Smith had arrived at Constantinople and the treaty had been fully
discussed.[378] On January 5th, 1799, it was signed with befitting
solemnity.[379]

British interests at Constantinople had hitherto been so insignificant
as only to require a diplomatic mission of the lowest rank. But the new
alliance opened such a prospect of political influence and expanded
trade with the Levant[380] that in November, Grenville accepted the
offer of Lord Elgin, former Minister at Berlin, to proceed to
Constantinople as Ambassador Extraordinary and Minister
Plenipotentiary.[381] Elgin’s departure was delayed from various causes,
and in the end he was to carry the ratifications of the concluded
treaty.

Grenville’s programme thus launched, the Cabinet became engrossed in the
hopes, anxieties and disappointments of the Second Coalition, till the
failure of most of its schemes and the Convention of El-Arish forced the
question of Egypt upon its attention.
