 IV

 THE NEW DEPENDENCY


War with the French thus decided upon, the Turks set about for a plan of
operations. Returning to the palmy days of Selim the Grim, the campaign
which added Egypt to the Ottoman Empire in the sixteenth century was to
serve as a model for his successors in the last days of the eighteenth.
The Pasha of Damascus, the Governor and the Beys of Egypt, so the Turks
calculated in their vague way, at the head of 60,000 soldiers “on the
upper side of the Egyptian line,” and Djezzar, the powerful Pasha of
Acre, at the head of no less than 50,000 men, were to co-operate in the
reduction of Cairo. This, with the help of Allah, would inevitably lead
to the fall of Alexandria. Russian and Turkish ships would probably co-
operate and a corps of select Janissaries would act along the
coast.[382] “Firmans” inciting the Moslems to a holy war against the
French were spread far and wide.[383]

The war, in this way, would not be very costly. The various
insubordinate pashas and beys, whether victorious or not, were sure to
be ruined in the struggle with the French; and, at the right moment, the
Porte would step in to reassert its authority.

The English minister, however, could hardly be put off with such
calculations. He had, moreover, before him Nelson’s recommendations as
to what the Turks should do.[384] The Divan readily approved Nelson’s
views, for they had already had enough of the neighbourhood of the
French to the disaffected Greeks. The present opportunity of expelling
the restless Republicans from the Ionian Islands was not to be lost. In
this they were confirmed by the determination of Paul, who was, as
Nelson complained, “more intent on taking ports in the Mediterranean
than destroying Buonaparte [_sic_] in Egypt,”[385] to employ the Russian
naval contingent against the French in the Adriatic and in the
Archipelago. The Porte—with memories of the intrigues of Catherine—could
hardly prefer the Russians to the French and had to employ the major
part of its Navy to co-operate with the Russian squadron and thus avoid
giving them a free hand in those quarters. The minor part, after lengthy
arguing by Smith, were sent to co-operate with Captain Hood, commanding
the blockade of the Egyptian coast.[386]

Sixteen Turkish and two Russian sail, accordingly, arrived before
Alexandria on October 19th and 21st. Together with the English ships
they made an attempt to capture the castle and camp of Abukir, “but
having no troops to land it was impossible to do anything when the guns
were silenced and the camp put to rout,” and the allies had to content
themselves with a mere blockade.[387]

The arrival of the Turkish ship coincided with a serious rising in Cairo
against the French “liberators.” This rising cannot be understood
without careful examination of Bonaparte’s organisation of Egypt.

Already, as a young lieutenant, he had deeply interested himself in the
recorded legislative experiments of the chief nations of ancient and
modern times. Indeed, he considered history chiefly as a school for
political training. Here is one of his comments while studying ancient
history in Rollin: “I find that the writers of history give no detailed
information as to the nature of the taxes raised by the sovereign, and
as to the manner of gathering them and assessing them throughout the
country. They give no details as to how the ruler makes his will known
to his subjects.”[388] His political apprenticeship began in North Italy
in the year 1796, when the organisation of the conquest opened for him
the government of men. In Egypt, however, his energies found a new
sphere.

The young general contemplated the ancient land in the manner, not of
the poet, but of the severe observer who neglected nothing.[389]
Geography, customs, religion, problems, resources, he analysed all, he
measured all. He calculated as an engineer and pondered as a statesman.
There were, for instance, the various strata composing the inhabitants
of Egypt; how were they to be held in check? What relations would each
of them bear to the French? There was the annual inundation; how was the
water to be equitably distributed?

Unlike superficial observers from the West, Bonaparte saw that beneath
the ungainly form, beneath the misery and degradation, was real worth.
The first principle of his government was to allow the inhabitants great
latitude in the management of their affairs and, especially in the
administration of justice, which was entirely founded on their holy
books.[390] He thought, however, that the Egyptians required regular
institutions to ensure for themselves the “great benefits of an
organised community, namely, security of person and of property.”[391]
Such institutions would enable the people to give expression to their
innermost thoughts and thus enlighten the French as to what was required
for Egypt’s welfare. The class which he chose to staff these
institutions or _divans_ and to act as the intermediaries between French
and Egyptians were the “Ulema” and the notables. These were the natural
leaders of the people, they were the interpreters of the Koran (“And the
greatest obstacles proceed from religious ideas”), they could not mount
a horse and were—witness their flowing robes—unwarlike in their habits
and, as leaders of a revolt, would cut a poor figure. They were rich,
loved justice and were actuated by good principles of morality. Moreover
they hated both Turk and Mameluke and had a vision of a new Egypt.
“C’était un fibre de laquelle on pouvait un jour tout espérer.”[392] As
for the other sections; the Copts or Egyptian Christians had, by their
mastery of the intricacies of Egyptian finance, made themselves
indispensable to the new administration; the Beduins and Mamelukes were
veritable pests and were to be extirpated; the foreign residents
composed of the dregs of the Levant were to be kept under so as not to
give the Egyptians any umbrage.

On September 4th, consequently, he summoned an assembly of notables, who
were to be selected by the French generals commanding in the fourteen
provinces.[393] The mathematician Monge and the chemist Berthollet were
appointed commissioners to the Assembly and were instructed to ask it to
deliberate on the following matters: firstly, the best organisation for
provincial assemblies; secondly, the formulation of a criminal and a
civil code; thirdly, the systematising of the laws regulating
succession; and, fourthly, the improving of the laws relating to
property and taxation.[394]

Chosen under conditions which should ensure their docility, the
deliberations of the notables were slow “partly on account of the
calmness and reticence of the Oriental character and the novelty of the
affair and partly owing to the difference of usage from one province to
another.”[395] Their answers were, on the whole, marked by an
intelligent conservatism, and Bonaparte intimated his intention to carry
out as many of their suggestions as possible.[396]

But the Egyptians did not take the thing seriously. They even disliked
it, and the cause is not far to seek. The institutions under which they
had hitherto lived were mainly of the tax-collecting type. Their rulers
exercised force over them of the most violent kind for certain purposes
at certain times, but they did not impose laws as distinct from
occasional commands. The subjects had to submit when the agents of the
ruler descended on them for taxes, recruits, or forced labour, but in
the general tenor of their lives their actions and forbearances were
regulated by authorities with which the rulers never interfered.[397] An
orderly system of government and, especially, of taxation, so far from
securing to them their property, seemed to the Egyptians, on the
contrary, to close every loophole for escaping the payment of the tax,
as they might occasionally have done under the old _régime_. The
Egyptians, moreover, seemed to have considered that the destruction of
the Mamelukes meant their emancipation from any form of imposition.[398]
Bonaparte’s forced contributions soon undeceived them. He was obliged to
have recourse to these through the difficulties of collecting the taxes.
The peasants did not pay the impost till the last extremity. The tax-
collectors had to be accompanied by strong military escorts, often to
find the villages deserted.[399]

Bonaparte’s nature, moreover, was too fiery, his self-confidence too
deep-rooted, his energies too many-sided, to keep him within the bounds
of prudence. Fussy interferences such as the order that every Egyptian
should wear a tricolour cockade or that every householder should have a
lantern outside his door irritated the Moslems.[400] The advancement of
Levantines, although Bonaparte disapproved of the principle, alarmed
them as to the safety of their religion. His remarkable proclamations,
far from reassuring, probably overshot their mark,[401] nor was the pomp
with which he celebrated, for instance, the birthday of the Prophet,
more successful.[402] The drunkenness and the frivolity of the French
and the liberties they took with women, scandalised a society
punctilious on these points.[403] The Egyptians suffered all the
miseries of a military occupation. When the French took their quarters
in a village, the poor peasant would find his utensils, ploughs, doors,
roofs, “everything, in short, of a combustible nature” burnt for
cooking, his earthen pots broken, his corn consumed, his fowl and
pigeons roasted and devoured and, worst of all, his daughters
ravished.[404]

Furthermore, since the change of policy at the Porte, Turkish agents had
been reaching Egypt through Syria and were inciting the people to
revolt.[405] Inflammatory firmans from the Commander of the Faithful
were read aloud in the mosques, ascribing to the French the intention of
destroying Islam, and the faithful were called upon to resist. Rumours
were current that Djezzar of Acre was advancing on Cairo and that
Bonaparte would burn the town before evacuating it.[406] The full extent
of Nelson’s great victory was perfectly known and Captain Hood entered
into relations with the disaffected elements in the population,
especially in Alexandria. He reconciled Murad with the Porte and with
Djezzar.[407]

Passions were roused to white heat. The cessation of the exemption of
religious endowments from taxation, and the imposition of a tax on
houses gave the signal for revolt.[408] It broke out on October 21st,
and, by its suddenness, placed the dispersed French garrison in a
critical situation. But the first shock of surprise over, a judicious
disposition of artillery enabled the men of the 13 vendémiaire to
suppress the rising. Drastic measures were taken to prevent its
recurrence.[409]

Undaunted, nay, rather nerved to greater efforts by the disaster to the
French fleet, he had ordered[410] Desaix to open the brilliant campaign
in Upper Egypt which was to take the French to its southern confines, to
disperse the Mamelukes, to force the indefatigable Murad to take shelter
in the oases, and to destroy the Meccans impelled into the Valley of the
Nile by religious fervour and material needs,[411] and which was not to
end till the French flag flew over the solitary port of Kosseir, on the
Red Sea. Fully occupied as he was with restoring the spirit of the
army—punishing, appeasing, promising, attending to countless details of
military and civil administration—he had planned the Institute of Egypt
destined to be his lasting monument in the Valley of the Nile and to
give to modern Egypt its marked “Gallophil” tendencies. He organised it
in four sections—Mathematics, Physics, Political Economy, and the
Arts.[412] At the first session, which was held on August 23rd and in
which Monge was elected president and Bonaparte vice-president, the
latter suggested these questions for consideration. Could the baking-
ovens of the army be improved? Was there any substitute for hops in the
brewing of beer? How could the waters of the Nile be purified for
drinking purposes? Was a windmill or a watermill the more serviceable?
Could gunpowder be produced in Egypt? What was the state of law and of
education in Egypt? and how could they be improved conformably to the
notions of the inhabitants?[413] “It is doubtful whether a learned
society has ever received a more imperious impulse towards the
practical.”

His main occupation, however, was to thwart the forces generated by
Nelson’s victory. In spite of the grand self-confidence which led him to
write to Kléber, “If the English relieve this squadron by another, and
continue to overrun the Mediterranean, they will perhaps compel us to do
greater things than we intended,”[414] or to say to Marmont: “This
country was once before a mighty kingdom; in any case it is a good
starting-point for conquests when the Turkish Empire breaks up, and a
base for offensive operations against the English,”[415] and in spite of
evidence that he did not fully realise the constricting effect of sea-
power,[416] the blow had cut deeper than he cared to show. For one thing
it shattered his schemes of a speedy return to France, for another it
jeopardised the position of France in Egypt.

“It is in such moments,” he said, “that men of superior character prove
their mettle.” He rose to the occasion. Measures were taken to protect
the land communications between Alexandria and Rosetta. Naval
combinations whereby all the French, Venetian and Maltese vessels in the
Mediterranean were to be rallied and to contain at least the Turkish
fleet and escort the expected reinforcements from France were
devised.[417] Overtures for trade relations were made to Tripoli.[418]
Receiving reports of hostile preparations in Syria,[419] he attempted to
win over Djezzar. Beauvoisins, a cavalry officer, was sent to that pasha
with assurances and promises.[420] The mission, however, was a complete
failure.[421] Similar overtures were made to the Shereef of Mecca.[422]
The Grand Vizier was informed of the appointment of Talleyrand as
ambassador and of his being furnished with full powers to settle all
difficulties. Suspecting, however, that Talleyrand might not have left
France, he invited the Grand Vizier to send a trusted representative to
Cairo.[423] Staff-Captain Mailly de Chateaurenaud was sent with another
letter of the same nature.[424] Nothing came of these efforts.

He announced to the Directory that his return would not take place in
October as he had promised, suggested more naval projects and measures
whereby the Grand Turk would be frightened into acquiescence in the
accomplished fact, but hinted, however, that if the negotiations at
Rastadt proved fruitless, Egypt would be a sure means of concluding a
glorious peace with England. Other courses of action were, moreover,
still open to the Directory. It might, for instance, transfer the
theatre of the naval war to the Mediterranean and leave Ireland alone.
It was not yet too late to employ diplomatic methods. It was essential
that Talleyrand should keep his word and proceed to Constantinople.[425]

All doubts as to the attitude of Turkey should have been removed by the
arrival of the Turkish sail and their participation in the blockade.
Flags of truce were sent to find out the real state of affairs.[426] The
information gathered by the French messengers about the formation of a
new coalition against the Republic was true to the hilt,[427] but it was
of so sombre a character that the Governor of Alexandria considered it a
mere English “ruse.”[428] Without abandoning the naval combinations he
had sketched, Bonaparte seemed to have entertained the idea of opening
negotiations with his enemies. This was widely believed in the
army.[429] He, accordingly, decided to release the Turkish battleship
detained in the port of Alexandria and to send on board Beauchamp, ex-
consul at Muscat, with definite overtures to the Porte. “If ever the
question is made to you,” so ran his instructions,[430] “‘Will the
French consent to evacuate Egypt?’ Why not,? provided that the two
emperors should put an end to the revolt of Paswan Oglou and give up the
project of dismembering European-Turkey; as for us, we will do
everything that may prove of utility to the Ottoman Empire and secure it
against its enemies, but the indispensable preliminary to all
negotiation as well as to all accommodation is a ‘firman’ liberating
Frenchmen wherever they have been imprisoned, especially in Syria.” If
this was refused, Beauchamp was to threaten the Porte with an invasion
of that province.

The negotiator did not leave Alexandria before February 13th. Near
Rhodes, he was captured by an English squadron under Troubridge and his
important despatches intercepted.[431]

The design to invade Syria took some time to mature. On December 17th he
announced to the Directory that hostile preparations were going on in
Syria and declared that had it not been for the seven days’ desert
journey he would have demanded an explanation.[432] But reports that the
Mamelukes of Ibrahim and the troops of Djezzar were actually moving
determined him to make the “seven days’ journey.”[433]

 V

 THE SYRIAN CAMPAIGN


Arriving at Constantinople on December 26th, Sidney Smith’s first
endeavour, after signing the treaty of alliance, was to impress upon the
Porte his own plan of operations. Considering that Alexandria was the
first objective, and that, according to the best reports, its garrison
did not exceed 2000 troops, there was not one moment to lose in taking
possession of it. To obtain this result the best thing was to make
simultaneous attacks on many points by light-draught vessels. No more
than 4000 men were necessary for disembarking, while 2000 more—forming a
reserve—were to be posted at Rosetta and the gun-boats were to push up
the river to the town of Rahmanieh and intercept the communications
between the headquarters at Cairo and the coast. Sir Sidney could not be
put off by the arguments of the Turks, and finally it was agreed to lay
the new burden on Djezzar’s broad back and fall upon him for the 6000
soldiers needed.[434]

But Sidney Smith could not be expected to be satisfied with the prosaic
dictates of common sense. He was determined that the struggle for the
evacuation of Egypt should have no smaller result than converting the
veterans composing the army of the East into deadly enemies of the
Republic. He seemed to have shared in this respect the views of more
illustrious men, Burke and Windham. “Believe me, my dear lord,” he wrote
to Admiral Lord Keith, “there is no way of finishing this long contest,
in which we are engaged, but by putting our opponents one against the
other; what force have we got to set limits to their influence
otherwise? I speak from the experience I gained at Paris, when I say
France can only be set to rights by Frenchmen and till she is set to
rights in the internal springs of her government, we never can have
peace; for the necessitous, the turbulent and the proscribed part of
their 28 millions of men look to war as the element in which they can
best live.”[435] He fully believed in the current notion that the French
in Egypt were of “the dissatisfied part of the army which the republican
tyrants wished to get rid of.”[436]

He was, moreover, of opinion that no dependence could be placed upon the
Turks for driving the French from Egypt; in this he was correct.
Djezzar, upon whom the whole Turkish plan of campaign turned, was
handicapped by his fear lest the Porte was merely playing him a trick.
The pasha, moreover, “had very little idea of the abilities of the man
he had to oppose,” and Captain Hope, after a visit to Acre, was obliged
to declare that “if we do not completely cut off all supplies from
France and if the plague and famine do not drive their army out of Cairo
in my opinion the Turk never will.”[437] Turkey, moreover, was suffering
from acute financial embarrassments. “The boasted treasures of the
seraglio,” wrote Spencer Smith, “are to be taken in a very different
estimate from the traditional fables of servile historians or
superficial travellers.”[438] To its repeated request for a loan—not a
subsidy—the English Cabinet literally did not deign to reply. What more,
declared Smith, could a Turkish Army do than fight one pitched battle
and then become a “collection of banditti”?[439] What more could the
Porte do than “fulminate the thunders of Islamism against Bonaparte; a
weapon which he himself has found means to employ.”

The “intercepted correspondence,” moreover, had strengthened him in his
illusion that the army of the East would play the Coalition’s game in
France. Did not a general of that army call Egypt “a perfect Vendée”?
The inhabitants of that country—witness their serious rising in which
Bonaparte or, at least, Berthier, was supposed to have succumbed—were
against the foreigners to a man. To prevent that general from “realising
a part of his revolutionary projects” or recovering “some ground in
public opinion,” Smith thought that there was no time to lose in
carrying out his plans. Their military aspect has already been
described.

For the realisation of his original—and hitherto unnoticed—design of
corrupting the French Army,[440] he had brought with him into Turkey a
band of _émigrés_, foremost among whom was Phélippeaux, Bonaparte’s
schoolfellow and deadly enemy, who had helped Sidney Smith to effect his
escape from the Temple. The _rôle_ of Phélippeaux and his fellow
_émigrés_ would be to attract by his influence most of the discontented
officers of Bonaparte’s army, many of whom, to his knowledge, belonged
to the same party as himself and whose defection, consequently, would be
easy to obtain. The Porte’s duty under these circumstances would be to
encourage deserters by giving them posts in its armies or enabling them
to return home and rise against the Directory. The Turkish ministers
were staggered by the novelty of the suggestion, but, anxious to expel
the French out of Egypt with the least expenditure in men and money,
they readily consented.

Before Sidney Smith however had time to put his schemes in operation,
Bonaparte’s invasion of Syria had deranged them and forced upon the
“Commodore” a defensive _rôle_.

As is the case with the origin of the Egyptian Expedition, the motives
and aims of the campaign in Syria have long been shrouded in mystery.
Was it intended as the mere anticipation of a threatened Turkish attack
or as the preliminary of grandiose projects destined to “change the face
of the East”? In contemporary documents the aim is clearly stated. On
February 10th, when the van was actually in touch with the enemy, he
wrote to the Directory: “In this operation I have three aims: firstly,
to assure the conquest of Egypt by constructing a stronghold beyond the
desert, and thenceforth to remove the armies of any nation whatsoever
from Egypt to such a distance that they can in no way combine with a
European army which should come to land on its coasts; secondly, to
oblige the Porte to explain itself and thereby support the negotiations
that you have doubtless set on foot, and the mission of the Consul
Beauchamp, whom I am sending to Constantinople on the great Turkish
‘caravel’; thirdly, to deprive the English fleet of the supplies that it
draws from Syria, by employing the two remaining months of winter so as
to make all that coast friendly to me through war and
negotiations.”[441] He concluded by stating that he would return to
France “if war was to break out between France and the monarchies.” The
same statements were made in other letters of the same date.[442]
Berthier, his chief of staff, speaks of the motives of the campaign in
analogous terms.[443]

The value of the evidence submitted is beyond assail. It is true that
Bonaparte was not in the habit of confiding his schemes to the
Directory. In this case, however, this does not arise. For, had he
really entertained designs of Oriental conquest, the co-operation of the
French Government was indispensable. Moreover, when Berthier wrote, the
Directory did not exist and there could have been no reason for
reticence.

In later years, however, he stated that he had contemplated vast schemes
of empire and that his vision extended to Hindustan. At St. Helena he
discussed the project at great length and declared that even as late as
1801, after the Treaty of Lunéville, it could have been realised had
Kléber escaped the assassin’s dagger.[444] To Madame de Rémusat, in the
course of the conversations of the Boulogne camp, he made similar
assertions.[445]

In comparing contemporary statements with later ones it is impossible to
accept the latter. For though it is certain that the Egyptian Expedition
was envisaged in some vague way to result in the ruin of British power
in the East, there is not the slightest indication that the Directory
did as much as enter into the schemes formulated by the exile of St.
Helena in his forced leisure. There can be no doubt, moreover, that the
letter addressed on January 25th, 1799, to Tippoo Sahib, in which that
potentate was informed of the arrival on the shore of the Red Sea of his
“innumerable and invincible army, filled with the desire of delivering
you from the iron yoke of England,” was no more than a device to keep
the British in India busy. He was, as has been shown, counting on a
speedy return to France, and was merely waiting to hear that “the pear
had ripened.” In the meantime, a campaign in Syria would give an
opportunity for fresh victories which would efface the impression
produced by the destruction of the fleet, and cover him with the glory
of having outshone the crusaders.

He could at times indulge in grandiose schemes and found empires as vast
as that of Alexander, but he immediately subjected them to the calm
scrutiny of reason. “He was well aware,” writes Bourrienne, “that these
plans were out of keeping with our small forces, the feebleness of the
government, and the soldiers’ loathing of the desert.”[446]

Was he thinking more seriously of proceeding through Asia Minor to
Constantinople? Talleyrand relates “that he spoke several times of
returning to Europe via Constantinople, which was not precisely the
route to India. Yet it did not require great penetration to divine that,
after making a triumphal entry into Stamboul, he neither intended to
restore the throne of the Sultan Selim nor yet to replace the Ottoman
Empire by a “united and indivisible republic.”[447] But believing in the
possibility of peace with Turkey, he could not have aimed at conquering
Constantinople. It is true that the Directory, as shall be seen,[448]
suggested to Bonaparte such a measure; but its despatch did not reach
him before he was occupied in the siege of Acre. In point of fact this
latter design seems to be a gloss on the original plan, which was
nothing more than striking at Djezzar and Ibrahim before their forces
marched into Egypt.

This contention is, finally, borne out by the number of troops he led
into Syria, namely, 12,900 men.[449]

The first success of the invading army was obtained at El-Arish on
February 13th, where a skilful night attack on the force sent by Djezzar
to relieve the fort of that place threw the garrison on its own
resources. On February 20th that garrison, composed of 1500 men, was
released with its arms on condition that it would not take part in the
war in Syria or Egypt for a year.[450] Bonaparte recognised the danger
of allowing liberty to a body of armed men with whom the promise not to
bear arms against him would weigh very little, and sought, consequently,
to enrol them in his army and, in other ways, not to execute the
conditions granted.[451] The next important place, Gaza, did not resist,
and its capture placed in the hands of the French immense stores.[452]
On March 7th, Jaffa was summoned to surrender, but its commander refused
and the messenger’s head was cut off and placed on a pike over the chief
tower.[453] Before nightfall the town was taken by storm and underwent
all the horrors of pillage. Two of Bonaparte’s aides-de-camp, however,
ventured—unauthorised—to spare the lives of a large number of the
garrison who had taken refuge in a caravanserai.[454] No written
engagement was concluded, but merely a pledge calculated to prevent
further effusion of blood on both sides. This placed the General in a
difficult situation. Food was very scarce; to send them to Egypt would
have needed a strong escort and would thus weaken the army, and to
release them on parole would have been a sure means of strengthening
Djezzar. Moreover, some of the garrison of El-Arish, released in that
manner, were found among the prisoners of Jaffa. After deliberating with
some of his generals, Bonaparte took the cruel as well as impolitic
resolution of massacring the 2240 men in cold blood.[455]

The booty taken was immense. But it was during the halt at Jaffa that
the symptoms of the plague first appeared and its ravages began to be
felt. The firm conduct of Bonaparte—commemorated in the famous canvas of
Gros, and the wise policy of Desgenettes, the chief physician, who
refused even to use the word “plague”—kept up the spirit of the
soldiers.[456] It was also at Jaffa that Bonaparte issued manifestoes
similar in tone and spirit to the Egyptian ones, with the difference
that it was Djezzar instead of the Beys who was the enemy he wanted to
destroy.[457] Even after this, he sought to win over the old pasha.[458]

It was on the road to Acre that Bonaparte first discerned the “Theseus”
and the “Tigre,” under the command of Sidney Smith, called to Syrian
waters by the appeals of Djezzar for help.[459] Phélippeaux gave up the
_rôle_ of seeking converts to the Royalist cause among the French
soldiers for the far more fruitful one of repairing the ruined
fortifications. But Sir Sidney did more than place the talents of the
French officer at the disposal of the pasha. After the fall of Jaffa,
Bonaparte ordered the French flotilla at Damietta to set sail for that
town and await instructions, which directed it to Acre.[460] But hearing
of the presence of the English he sent word, on March 18th, to Jaffa to
detain the ships there.[461] It was now too late. For Sidney Smith knew
of their approach and took his measures to fall in with them. Of the
flotilla composed of a corvette and nine sail of gun vessels only the
corvette and two small vessels escaped. The event was decisive; for, in
addition to the fact that the prizes mounted 34 guns, they carried the
siege artillery on which the French reckoned for the reduction of
Acre.[462] They had thenceforth to bear the fire of their own heavy
ordnance.

Though the walls of Acre were very weak, its position on a low-lying
promontory of rectangular shape gave the utmost possible advantage to
the power holding the sea, to the flanking fire of whose ships the
columns of assault were exposed.[463] The first impression the French
had of the town, however, was that it was miserably fortified. The ease
with which Jaffa fell, moreover, confirmed them in the belief that they
would make short work of the defence.[464]

While engaged in the preparations for a regular siege, Bonaparte sought
to win over the enemies of Djezzar, foremost among whom were the Druses,
some Beduin tribes and the townsfolk of the different pashaliks. The
result was,[465] generally speaking, that the people approached adopted
an attitude of benevolent neutrality and waited to see on which side
victory would declare itself. These circumstances gave the “Commodore”
the opportunity of playing a congenial part. He addressed a circular
letter to the Druses “recalling them to the sense of their duty and
enjoining them to cut off their supplies from the French camp” and “sent
them at the same time a copy of Bonaparte’s impious proclamation, in
which he boasted of having overthrown all Christian establishments and
accompanied it by a suitable exhortation, calling upon them to choose
between the friendship of a Christian knight and that of an unprincipled
renegade.”[466] According to him, this had the desired effect.[467] But
unfortunately the “Christian knight” in this case was the ally of the
barbarous Djezzar, and the Druses could not have been deeply moved by
the destruction of the Papacy or of the Order of St. John. They would
have all welcomed the defeat or destruction of the Bosnian tyrant.
Sidney Smith knew that their attitude only depended on the fall or
escape of Acre. “It is on the issue of this conflict,” he wrote, “that
depends the opinion of the multitude of spectators on the surrounding
hills, who wait only to see how it ends to join the victor; and with
such a reinforcement for the execution of his known projects,
Constantinople and even Vienna must feel the shock.”[468]

The failure of an attempt of his on Haifa, on which affair he did not,
in his despatches, waste a word, had brought him into relations with
Bonaparte, who ratified, on March 23rd, an agreement for exchange of
prisoners and thanked Sir Sidney for liberating a French courier from
the unspeakable prison of Constantinople known as the “bagno.”[469]
Neither Bonaparte at this stage, nor historians after him, suspected
that the “humanity” of Smith was the outcome of a nature in which
chivalrous impulsiveness was combined with calculating craftiness. From
the outset he had recommended to the Porte a “humanity offensive” as a
sure means of encouraging the French to desert their colours.[470]

The French broke ground for their trenches on March 19th, and these, as
a result of the prevailing optimism, were indifferently done.[471] The
bombardment began on March 23rd, and on the next day a great part of the
tower fell with a crash. But the far-sighted Phélippeaux had made a
counterscarp which stopped all the efforts of the French sappers. The
assault of March 28th was a disastrous failure, for which the French
paid heavily. This first great check restored the spirit of the besieged
and made them more amenable to carry out the suggestions of their
Christian allies.[472] Smith advised vigorous sallies executed at short
intervals to destroy the French works. In the absence of subordinate
officers, however, “exertions that depend upon order cannot be
executed,” and the various sorties did not have the expected effect and
did not stop the progress of the French in their trenches.[473]

During the early period of the siege, the French had to meet other
enemies. A force intended for the relief of the garrison of Acre had
been gathered on the banks of the Jordan.[474] Detachments of French
under Junot and under Murat, and, later on, the division of Kléber, were
sent against it. Fearing the risks incurred by Kléber, Bonaparte
personally marched to his help, and near the foot of Mount Tabor, on
April 16th, he succeeded not only in extricating his lieutenant but in
destroying hundreds of the Turks, Mamelukes, Nablousians and Syrians
forming the relieving force under the Pasha of Damascus.

On the day of his return to the camp before Acre, April 19th, three
French frigates from Alexandria succeeded in eluding Sir Sidney’s
vigilance and landing six heavy pieces of artillery.[475] Their
battering power thus increased, the assailants showed great vigour. On
April 25th, the French fired a mine with good effect; but the storming
column was baffled by inner entrenchments. Bonaparte did not lose hope,
but he seemed to have made up his mind to return to Egypt once the town
was taken.[476] The assault of May 1st was beaten back by the enfilading
fire from two ravelins, which were built at the suggestion of Sir Sidney
and the musketry poured in from the inner walls.[477] On that day
Phélippeaux succumbed to sunstroke and exhaustion.[478] On May 7th the
Turkish fleet, bringing reinforcements, at last hove to in view on the
horizon. Its delay was due to the fact that it was originally ordered to
proceed to Alexandria, and Sidney Smith found himself “obliged to be
peremptory in the repetition of his orders to it to join him at
Acre.”[479] The sight determined Bonaparte to carry the place at once.
The assault, executed with great vigour, was beaten back, but the French
kept their lodgment in the great tower, and Smith expressed on May 9th
his fear that the defence would be overpowered.[480] He clung on,
however, and the event justified his stubbornness. For after the failure
of the great assault of May 8th, Bonaparte had come to the decision of
raising the siege. Writing to the Directory on May 10th, he asserted
that he had gained his object, that nothing remained but to march in the
streets of Acre, to do which, however, he would have to open a trench
before every house, and that as the season was advanced he would return
to Egypt to meet the Turkish or European Army expected to land there in
July or August.[481] In this analysis he passed over one important
factor, namely, the plague. Its ravages were not relatively very great;
but the medical service was seriously handicapped in various ways.[482]
Another factor was the news of the seething discontent in Egypt and the
rise of a fanatic who proclaimed himself the “Mahdy” or the “deliverer”
who was to regenerate the world. He also had received news in March that
the war had now actually broken out in Europe, and there was no time to
lose.

One of the difficult problems of the retreat was the transport of the
wounded and invalids. There was a suggestion that an appeal should be
made to Sir Sidney to facilitate sending them by sea. This appeal,
however, was impossible; for Bonaparte had already circulated, on April
18th, an order to Berthier in which he held the English officer
responsible for the atrocities committed during the siege by Djezzar,
accused him of embarking French prisoners on plague-infected vessels,
described his conduct as that of a fool, and strictly charged the French
officers not to hold any communications with the English.[483] The cause
of this diatribe has been something of a puzzle. Impartial French
writers, recognising that the accusations were false, could not find an
explanation. British writers were not more enlightened. It has been
suggested that Bonaparte’s hostility to Sir Sidney dated from the
latter’s share in burning the French fleet when Toulon was recaptured by
the Republicans. This conjecture is strengthened by Bonaparte’s habitual
reference to Smith as the “capitaine des brûlots.” There can be no
doubt, however, that the true cause was the English commander’s attempt
to sow the seeds of mutiny in the French ranks. One of the means he used
for that end was to take advantage of the various sallies, notably that
of May 16th, to disperse in the French trenches a proclamation from the
Porte and endorsed by him promising repatriation to deserters. He even
sent a copy to the French general, with a covering letter advising him
to evacuate the territories of the Sultan.[484] He alleged that “the
soldiers held their eager hands above the parapet of their works to
receive the copies of it when thrown from the trench.”[485] Probably
here the wish was father to the thought, for we find French writers of
all shades of opinion unanimous in denying that the proclamation had had
any effect. Sir Sidney, at any rate, was not mistaken as to the cause of
the hostility. “The intention of it (the accusation),” he wrote, “was
evidently to do away the effect which the proclamation of the Porte
began to make on the soldiers.”[486] He, in his turn, brought forward
the accusation that assassins had been set upon him.[487] This was
probably the mere outcome of an exaggerated sense of his importance.

On May 20th, the retreat began in perfect order and without any
interference from the garrison. It was when the Army was leaving Jaffa
that there occurred what has been since known as the “poisoning of the
plague-stricken.” The proposal to administer a strong dose of opium to
the patients whose grave condition would not allow them to accompany the
Army was contemplated four days before the siege of Acre was raised, but
had to be abandoned owing to the categorical refusal of the chief
physician, Desgenettes.[488] Later on, however, Bonaparte found men
willing to carry it out. The number of those to whom the opium was
administered could not have been large and the dose, moreover, did not
have in most of the cases the desired effect, for Sidney Smith
immediately on his arrival at Jaffa on the heels of the French, found
“seven poor wretches left alive in the hospital” and declared that “they
are protected and shall be taken care of.”[489]

In the middle of June the Syrian Army, now sadly diminished,[490]
entered Cairo in triumph, after a retreat the terrible hardships of
which foreshadowed those of the more famous one from Moscow.

The Syrian campaign was, up to a certain extent, a success, for in the
course of it the Mamelukes of Ibrahim, the troops of Djezzar, the
irregular bands, and the regular Turkish soldiers sent from
Constantinople and Damascus were beaten with loss, their preparations
were disorganised and their bases destroyed. The harm that the failure
at Acre did was rather moral than material. Whatever technical
criticisms can be advanced against Bonaparte’s conduct of the campaign
or of the siege, the cause of failure is clear. It was sea power. Sidney
Smith’s real contribution to the success was not his dabbling in the
sordid politics of Syria or his attempt to corrupt the French soldiers;
it was the capture of their heavy artillery.

The lovers of the picturesque dwell on what would have happened had Acre
fallen, and Napoleon persisted down to his closing days in saying that
its mud walls had come between him and his destiny.[491] Probably he
continued to harp on Acre because he fastened his gaze too closely on
what was after all, only one of the manifestations of sea power, its
defence, or because he wanted to demonstrate that the Empire of the East
could be realised, that the failure to win it in 1799 was only due to a
mere accident and that one day, under happier auspices, success could be
achieved.

 VI

 THE DIRECTORY AND THE EXPEDITION


Tracing the attitude of the French Government towards the Army of the
East, we find that its first measure was to stop the departure of the
second expedition upon whose arrival in Egypt Bonaparte was
counting.[492] The cause was the news of the presence of a strong
English fleet in the Mediterranean. Nor were the diplomatic measures, as
arranged with the same general, carried out. Talleyrand, who had played
such an important part during the early history of the Expedition, was
among the foremost to abandon it. He did not proceed to Constantinople
as had been agreed. His attitude in this matter is not clear.[493] It
seems that his acceptance of the post of Ambassador was decided upon
only “in principle,” and that the “affair” was really one between
Bonaparte and Talleyrand. The former, for instance, spoke, in letters of
May 23rd and June 18th, of the departure of the latter to
Constantinople; but, in despatches of the same date addressed to the
Directory, he did not refer to it at all.[494] This noteworthy fact
points to another conclusion; namely, Bonaparte’s desire to exercise
direct control from his headquarters at Cairo over the negotiations.
This is further strengthened by the fact that after sailing from Toulon
he instructed Talleyrand to hold himself in readiness but not to leave
before another order and chose for him the long sea route instead of the
shorter one via Ancona and Corfu. The frigate he sent him for that
purpose was captured by the English on June 27th.[495] Talleyrand, by
that time, had changed his mind. Fear of the “Castle of Seven Towers”
has been alleged as the cause; but this explanation is hardly
satisfactory, for at that time the prospects at Constantinople were not
so gloomy for France. De la Jonquière’s conjecture, on the other hand,
that the Directory kept him to make use of his abilities in the rising
complications is not more satisfactory; for it did not set great value
on these abilities. The truth is that to Talleyrand as well as to
Bonaparte “the East seemed to offer compensation . . . and yet neither
of them dreamt of leaving the West except with great reluctance.”[496]
Finding his position more secure, the ex-bishop gave up all thought of
going to the Bosphorus, and proposed another man, Descorches, for the
post.[497]

The latter was appointed Ambassador on September 2nd, and in spite of
the despondent despatches of Ruffin and of the news of the destruction
of the French fleet which had immediately followed the joyful tidings of
the successful landing in Egypt, the hope that the Porte would not
declare war was not lost. On September 19th, for instance, the Minister
of Foreign Affairs submitted a note on the subject of the presents which
Descorches was to make to the dignitaries of the Divan.[498] The
instructions of the new Ambassador, moreover, did not differ materially
from the _projet_ which the Minister had drawn up at the outset.[499]

This hope, however, was soon destroyed by the news of Ruffin’s
imprisonment. The departure of the Ambassador was suspended[500] and
Bonaparte was plainly informed in a despatch of November 4th that he was
to rely on his own judgment and that the Directory, not being in a
position to support him, refrained from sending orders or
instructions.[501] This important despatch further stated that the
Neapolitan troops were about to take the field and that an Austrian
division had violated the neutrality of Switzerland, and, in view of
these complications, a levy of 200,000 Frenchmen had been ordered. As
for Turkey, its partition was imminent and, to secure a handsome portion
for the Republic, a march on Constantinople would be the surest means.
In the East a treaty with Tippoo had been proposed, and one, Louis
Monneron, who might be of use to Bonaparte, would be sent to that part
of the world.[502] The despatch concluded: “As it would be very
difficult for you at the present juncture to accomplish the return
voyage to France, there seem to be only three courses open to you, to
remain in Egypt, so arranging that you will be safe against all attacks
from the Turks—in which case you will not forget that there are seasons
of the year very unwholesome for Europeans; or to press on to India,
where, once you have arrived, you will be sure to find numbers of people
ready to join you in destroying the power of the English; or, lastly, to
march on Constantinople against the enemy threatening you.” From the
tenor of this despatch, which, incidentally, did not reach Bonaparte
till March 25th, 1799, the Directory evinced small desire to have him
back in Europe. Shortly afterwards, however, it was engaged in a plan of
operations, the success of which would have greatly affected the course
of events in Egypt.

The Minister of Marine, on November 19th, reported against the naval
combinations which Bonaparte had suggested, and proposed that the
vessels of war in the Western ports should be formed into efficient
fleets.[503] Barras endorsed this policy and recommended the Minister
himself as the only Admiral capable of executing it.[504] The
organisation of the fleet was, accordingly, decreed on December 19th,
and was energetically taken in hand.[505] In spite of great
difficulties, Bruix displayed such activity that a line-of-battle ship
of 74 guns, for instance, was fully equipped in three days.[506] In
three months an imposing force of twenty-four sail-of-the-line and eight
frigates was ready to set sail.

The Directory had, meanwhile, returned to a favourite project of the
various revolutionary governments, namely, the junction of the Spanish
and French fleets to secure superiority over those of Great
Britain.[507] The necessary negotiations with the Court of Madrid were
seriously compromised by the incapacity of Guillemardet, the French
Ambassador to that Court.[508] They suffered still more, however, from
the fact that their real purpose was never disclosed to the Spanish
Government. Knowing the repugnance of that Government to co-operate with
the Republic in the Mediterranean, the French negotiators made out that
Ireland was the goal of the operations of the combined fleets. Spain
was, accordingly, invited to hold the ships blockaded at Cadiz in
readiness and to send the squadron stationed at Ferrol to a French
oceanic naval base, and that at Carthagena to Toulon. It would be, in
this way, too much involved in the enterprise to be able to abandon it.

It is easy to see what effect such trickery was likely to have on the
efficiency of the Spanish Navy.

In one direction, however, the prospects of retrieving the situation in
the Mediterranean were for a moment brightened by the brilliant campaign
of Championnet which resulted in the expulsion of the Neapolitan
Bourbons and the establishment of the short-lived Parthenopean
Republic.[509] But the care with which Nelson destroyed what he could
not save of the Neapolitan Navy and the refractoriness of the Republican
authorities rendered illusory the plans of the Directory to relieve the
garrison of Malta from the ports of Naples.[510] France received an
additional check in the western basin of the Mediterranean. In an
illuminating memorandum dated July 10th, Talleyrand had called attention
to the friendliness of the Barbary Regencies and recommended the
Directory to wink at the depredations of the Corsairs on the commerce of
the vassal republics of Italy.[511] The disembarkation of a French army
in Egypt, however, radically changed the situation.[512] The French
Consul at Tunis, officially notified of the event, did his utmost to
allay the fears of the Bey. What rendered the situation critical,
however, was not so much religious excitement as the agitation of the
merchants trading with Egypt. In spite of reiterated orders from
Constantinople, the Bey was eager not to break with France. He had,
however, to submit to his spiritual suzerain, so on January 3rd he gave
orders to intern French subjects and authorised his corsairs to attack
French shipping. The Dey of Algeria showed himself not more eager. He
turned a deaf ear to two missives from the Grand Signior. A third
messenger, said to be authorised to resort to such violent means as the
bowstring or the poisoned cup, could not be deceived by elusive replies
and the Dey had to declare against France.[513]

The Regencies, however, did not take an active part in the struggle; and
the lot of the interned French could not have been very hard since their
Consul could, while imprisoned, take measures for revictualling the
garrison of Malta and could communicate with his Government on the
affairs of Egypt. Their hostility, however, formed a permanent menace to
French operations in the western basin of the Mediterranean and rendered
futile the attempts for the relief of Malta and for establishing
communications with Bonaparte by land.

The Directory satisfied itself with decreeing reprisals against the
Barbary Corsairs[514] and concentrated its attention on the Naval
preparations already described. Their aim was “to penetrate into the
Mediterranean and destroy the enemy fleet or expel them from that
sea.”[515] Bruix was accordingly instructed to avoid an engagement. If
he was able to enter the Mediterranean without being molested, he must
proceed first to Italy and disembark three or four thousand troops there
and then for Corfu, Malta and Alexandria, distributing among these three
places the allocated reinforcements.

Arriving at Brest, the Admiral strove to restore the impaired discipline
and instruct the different ranks in their duties.[516] Before he set
sail the Directory had received Bonaparte’s despatch of February 10th,
in which he had detailed the motives of the Syrian Campaign. The French
Government was thus in possession of definite information which disposed
of the disquieting rumours current on the Army of the East. The
situation was further changed by the defeat of Jourdan at Stockach,
which led to the evacuation of the right bank of the Rhine, by the
retirement of Scherer on the Adda after his defeat at Magnano, and by
the capitulation of Corfu to a combined force of Russians and Turks.
Under these circumstances it was suggested that Bonaparte should be
recalled to retrieve the fortunes of the Republic. De la Jonquière
attributes this to the intrigues of the Bonapartes and their political
friends.[517] Sandoz, the well-informed Prussian envoy, states that it
was Barras who made the proposal to his colleagues and was met by an
angry refusal.[518] Barras himself, in his _Memoirs_, says that the idea
was mooted by the celebrated constitution-monger, Sieyès,[519] who was
looking about for an ally to destroy the Directory. Nothing, in any
case, came of these intrigues. The instructions of Bruix were not
changed and he himself set sail on April 25th.

A few days after a Spanish squadron from Ferrol, composed of five sail-
of-the-line and one frigate, and with 3000 troops on board, anchored in
the roadstead of the Isle of Aix. It is not clear what was the _rôle_
assigned to that squadron. A letter from Talleyrand to the Spanish
Ambassador seems to indicate that the Ferrol ships should have joined
the forces of Bruix.[520] But other documents assign to them the
function of menacing Ireland so as to prevent the English Government
from reinforcing its Mediterranean fleet.[521]

In the meantime Bruix was able to enter the Mediterranean without
engaging the enemy and arrived at Toulon on May 14th. The situation of
the French in Italy had gone from bad to worse and it was finally
decided to evacuate Ancona, Naples, Cività Vecchia and Leghorn. Bruix
was instructed to co-operate in these operations, at the end of which he
was to do his utmost to effect his junction with the Spaniards and wrest
the supremacy of the sea from the English. A new task was soon added to
these.[522] The successes of the Allies were so rapid that the French
frontiers were now menaced and it was decided to employ the fleets in
the Mediterranean in bringing back either all or most of the Army of the
East. Bonaparte was apprised of this decision in a despatch of May 26th.
“You will judge,” so it concludes, “if you can safely leave part of your
forces behind, in which case the Directory gives you full power to hand
over the command of the troops to whomsoever you think fit. The
Directory would be gratified to see you at the head of the Republican
armies which you have commanded hitherto with such brilliant
success.”[523]

Before receiving the new orders, Bruix had set sail from Toulon and
arrived at Savona on June 4th. “The appearance of an imposing fleet,”
wrote an eye-witness, “raised the spirits of the harassed French
troops.”[524] But the Admiral, seeing that his captains were not capable
of seconding him in an engagement and knowing the nearness of enemy
forces, decided to retrace his course and joined the Spanish fleet at
Carthagena on June 22nd.[525] At that port, he was secretly informed not
to count on Spanish support, for Spain was opposed to any campaign in
the Mediterranean which did not have for its first object the reconquest
of Minorca.[526] Finding himself, moreover, indifferently supported by
the French Ambassador and hearing of the _coup d’état_ of thirty
prairial, which caused him to be uncertain as to the views of the
Government, he took the part of withdrawing from the Mediterranean with
the Spaniards, and arrived at Brest on August 8th with an imposing force
of forty-one line-of-battle ships.

Chevalier, the brilliant historian of the French Navy, thus judges this
remarkable episode: “The course of Admiral Bruix was well-conceived, but
failed through the weakness of our Allies and the inexperience of our
own Officers and crews. . . . The Spanish fleet brought to Brest, the
“gage” of an Alliance then tottering, was the only result of this
campaign. It is impossible to have any illusion as to the extent of the
services rendered by the fleet on the coast of Italy; a division of
frigates would have done as much.”[527]

Such a disappointing end could not but render the situation more
critical. In spite of the false reports published by the Directory, much
anxiety about the Army of the East was felt. Everyone who had anything
to do with the Expedition was anxious to disclaim any share of the
responsibility. “This enterprise,” observed Thibaudeau, “which found so
many enthusiasts at the beginning, came to be considered as nothing but
foolish and even wicked.”[528] The recriminations which followed
resulted in banishing all hope in the success of the venture. The
Government gave up the idea of wresting the supremacy of the seas and
had to content itself with securing the coast against an English
invasion.[529] In the diplomatic sphere, Talleyrand had already turned
his attention to persuade Turkey to release the imprisoned French
subjects. Negotiations for a cartel of exchange of prisoners were to be
conducted by the Prussian envoy at Constantinople.[530] This attempt
failed. A second one made by the Spanish envoy was not more
successful.[531] Talleyrand, however, did not despair. He thought that
the presence of the French fleet in the Mediterranean and the success of
the French invasion of Syria would finally open the eyes of the
Sultan.[532] The failure of the Syrian campaign, however, put an end to
all such hopes. On September 3rd, the Minister submitted to the
Directory a proposal for entering into negotiations with the Porte for
nothing less than the evacuation of Egypt[533]. He painted the condition
of the French Army in the most sombre colours and stated that France
could do no more than try to extricate them from their perilous
situation. Talleyrand recognised the obstacles, for though Turkey would
be delighted to see Egypt restored, its allies would not consent to see
the Republican armies reinforced by veteran troops under so famous a
commander as Bonaparte. The solution he suggested was that that general
and his troops should pledge themselves not to serve in the present war.
Anticipating the weighty objection that the situation of the French was
not as desperate as he represented, he proposed that the convention for
the evacuation should reserve to Bonaparte the faculty of rejecting it.

Talleyrand resigned the Ministry of Foreign Affairs on July 20th. His
successor, Reinhard, however, took up the project. While he was occupied
with the preliminary steps of obtaining the consent of Spain to act as
mediator between the Porte and the Republic, there arrived at Paris the
news of the complete victory gained by Bonaparte at Abukir over a
Turkish army.[534] With knowledge of the extent of the Turkish defeat
and of the Porte’s desire for a separate peace, Spanish mediation was
deemed unnecessary and it was decided to place the negotiations in the
hands of Bonaparte.[535] He was to be apprised of this by a despatch
dated October 10th. The day before he had landed at Frejus!
