 IX

 THE FOUNDATIONS OF PEACE
                               1801-1802.


The foundations of the peace of Europe were laid down in a spirit of
rivalry that boded ill for the durability of the structure. The history
of the treaties in the making is not less significant than that of the
actual struggle which they were to terminate.

The “Preliminaries of London” were the first of the instruments by which
the general pacification of Europe was completed. They were the
consummation of the various negotiations between Britain and France
which took place in the years 1800 and 1801. Egypt, as has been shown,
proved a stumbling block in the way of peace, and it was to remove the
obstacle that Dundas urged the necessity of expelling the French from
that country by force of arms. The new ministry under Addington resumed
the negotiations just at the time when Sir Ralph Abercromby began to
act. It was on the 21st March, 1801, that the new foreign secretary,
Lord Hawkesbury, made official overtures to Otto, the French
“Commissioner for prisoners” in London.[772] Hawkesbury hinted,
according to French sources,[773] that France might keep Egypt while
Britain should retain the conquests she had made at the expense of the
Republic and her allies, Spain and Holland.[774] M. Otto, naturally,
conceived the hope that Egypt could, after all, be “saved.” A friend of
Mr. Addington even informed him that Britain had greater reason for
fearing “disorders and revolutions” than for opposing the possession of
Egypt, Belgium or Louisiana by France. The Republic, therefore, could
“settle” that “affair” by an “amicable” arrangement with the sultan.
This done, Britain could not with any “decency” raise the question.[775]
It was rather bold of the friend of the Prime Minister to assert the
disinterestedness of his country when some 15,000 British soldiers were
fighting in the Nile Valley. An interview with Hawkesbury soon convinced
Otto that Britain was not as powerless as his informant gave him to
think. The British Minister made it clear that even if France consented
to restore Egypt to her lawful sovereign, Britain would still retain
“some” of her conquests.[776] In vain did the Frenchman protest against
what he considered to be an unfair bargain and develop a plausible
system of “barrière des intérêts” between the two countries. If only
they could agree on allocating India to Britain and the Mediterranean to
France, quarrels over the “ice-fields” of Newfoundland and the “fosse”
of Chandernagor would no longer arise. Hawkesbury agreed that the
principle was excellent, but, he added, the application was
difficult.[777]

The position of the British negotiator was daily growing stronger. On
the 2nd of April, Nelson destroyed the Danish fleet in Copenhagen. Then
came the news of the murder of the Tsar Paul. The event was a _coup de
foudre_ for the “First Consul”: “Il comptait avoir trouvé dans Paul ce
que Frédéric le Grand trouva dans Pierre III.”[778] Lord Hawkesbury no
longer spoke of the possibility of the retention of Egypt by France,
and, in communicating his terms to Otto even reserved the right of
“reducing the concessions” should “good” news come from Egypt before the
“signature.”[779] It was clear to him that “en stipulant ces clauses
léonines, l’Angleterre nous signifiait nettement qu’elle se refusait à
partager l’empire colonial et maritime.”[780]

A painful suspense then ensued. All eyes were turned towards the
operations in Egypt.[781] By the end of May, it was known in London that
Abercromby had won the victory of Canopus. The news produced an outburst
of joy which Otto, in his bitterness, described as “jactance
impertinente” of a nation unaccustomed to triumphs.[782] The British
Minister pressed him to conclude. France, he pointed out, was
unmistakably losing Egypt. Further haggling, he added, would only mean a
continuation of the carnage and a “stiffening” in the British attitude;
and, he threatened, Abercromby’s victory in Egypt might herald the Third
Coalition, as Nelson’s Battle of the Nile did the second.[783]

Under the stress of the evil news from the East, the resistance of
France subsided.[784] In a draft of a note of 23rd July to be presented
by Otto, the First Consul resigned himself to the loss of Egypt.[785] On
the 1st of October the preliminaries were signed.[786]

“Egypt,” it was stipulated, “shall be restored to the Sublime Porte,
whose territories and possessions shall be preserved entire, such as
they existed previously to the present war.”[787] To Dundas this seemed
a “childish” guarantee. He was not able to see what business it was of
France to insert Egypt in the preliminaries, for the question was one
for settlement between Britain, Russia and Turkey.[788] The late consul
in Cairo, Mr. Baldwin, had written to Dundas that the reason that led to
the sending of an army to Egypt should cause it to remain there. Baldwin
refused to see in this any treachery against an ally, the Porte: Great
Britain did not make great sacrifices for the _beaux yeux_ of the Grand
Signor, but in defence of her interests, and was prepared to send him
the annual tribute which the Mamelukes were supposed to pay but never
did.[789] The First Lord of the Admiralty, the Earl of St. Vincent, held
a different opinion. “I am bold to assert,” he wrote, “that the peace is
the very best this country has ever made, and I consider it as the
happiest event in my life to have contributed to so great a
blessing.”[790] This, in truth, is bold, but in the sense not intended
by the veteran admiral.

It must have been obvious to the ministers that the terms did not offer
desirable security in the East. One great means of obtaining it was by
acquiring the “exclusive merit of the peace,”[791] which was to secure
the integrity of the Turkish Empire. If it were not possible—and it
could hardly have been—to treat the question of Egypt as one for
settlement between Britain and Turkey, there was still time to prevent
separate negotiations between the Sultan and the First Consul. The point
was discussed by Otto and Hawkesbury. The Frenchman pretended that the
“Preliminaries amply provided for the interests of the allies of King
George,” that the First Consul was treating separately with them, that
the presence of their plenipotentiaries in the Congress to be held at
Amiens was sure to prove a cause of delay.”[792] Hawkesbury, however,
refused to concede, and it was finally agreed that the allies of both
countries should be admitted as “contracting parties.”[793]

Two days after the conclusion, the Foreign Secretary informed Elgin that
he had been able to secure the two objects nearest the Porte’s
heart,[794] namely, the preservation of the integrity of its dominions
and the formal recognition of the Septinsular Republic by France. In
order to avoid delay, “His Majesty was induced to hope that the Porte
would find no difficulty in entrusting the management of its affairs in
the Congress of Amiens to the British Plenipotentiary, either by giving
him full powers to that effect or by a declaration that it should accede
to any stipulations into which he might enter at the Congress on its
behalf.”[795]

Hawkesbury, however, was calculating without the First Consul. There had
been living in Paris since 1797 a Turkish Ambassador, Ali Effendi.[796]
Ever since the outbreak of war between the Republic and the Sultan, he
had been instructed on more than one occasion that no grounds for
hostility would remain once Egypt was restored to her sovereign, the
Septinsular Republic recognised, the claims of war-sufferers liquidated
and the old treaties renewed.[797] These were in reality “general
instructions,” but Ali, apparently, did not “formalise” and behaved as
if they were “full powers” to make peace. “Ali can be considered in no
other light than that of an instrument of France whose subserviency was
limited only by the smallness of his mental capacity.”[798] “Il était
tout à fait livré à la France.”[799] But these estimates are rather
unfair. The truth of the matter was, as a French diplomat expressed it:
“En outre que ses (Ali’s) lumières sont peu capables de porter le jour
nulle part, il était timide comme tous les agents ottomans dans leurs
rapports dont ils ne se dissimulent jamais les dangers pour leurs têtes,
surtout lorsque se trouvant hors de portée de la scène très mobile de
leur ministère ils ne peuvent juger l’accueil qu’ils recevront.”[800]
Ali succumbed to ignorance and timidity. On the 9th October he signed
with Talleyrand preliminaries of peace, by one of whose stipulations
France undertook to evacuate and restore Egypt.[801] It was now some
months since Belliard had capitulated in Cairo and Menou in Alexandria.
The preliminaries extended to France the privileges of the most favoured
nation treatment, but did not provide for the manner of concluding a
definite treaty of peace.

This was not, however, the end of the First Consul’s trickery. On 10th
October, Talleyrand and the Russian Plenipotentiary, Morkoff, signed a
secret convention, the third article of which stipulated that peace
between France and Turkey was to be negotiated at Constantinople under
Russian mediation.[802] Did this amount to a recognition of Russian
preponderance in the Levant?[803] It was rather a platonic homage to a
young and inexperienced autocrat.

The idea of mediating between France and Turkey had been handed down
from the previous reign. It was adopted partly from the desire to “have
an ostensible share in the completion of an arrangement which interested
Russia so nearly,”[804] partly in order to use Turkish affairs as a
lever for the “restitution of Piedmont.”[805] But this was not all.
There was a sincere desire on the part of Alexander to see general peace
reign everywhere. “The set of young men” by whom he was surrounded and
whom he was pleased to describe as the “committee of public
safety”—their name was the “comité secret”—“had been almost equally
zealous in promoting the intended peace with France, it being their
professed system to endeavour to disengage the Emperor from all foreign
concerns as well as connections and induce him to direct his principal
attention to the affairs of the interior.”[806] This is why the “Comité
secret” turned down a scheme of that inveterate self-seeker, Valerian
Zouboff, for military intervention in Georgia.[807] Kotschubey, one of
the Comité, expressed his relief that the Tsar decided not to “meddle”
in “toutes ces sottises de Malte.” He was for “une ligne de conduite
entièrement passive, au moins pour quelque temps.”[808] But what of
Russia’s interests in the period of quiescence? The Minister trusted to
Britain and France “neutralising” each other. “Les Anglais seuls
balancent par leur prépondérance sur cet élément (mer) la suprématie des
Français sur terre. Que cette balance disparaisse et rien ne s’opposera
plus à ce géant.”[809] Let Britain do as she liked with Malta, let her
even occupy the Ionian Islands or annex them,[810] let her destroy
French influence in the Divan by the sending of “proper” ambassadors,
appointed by Government and not by a company of merchants, and by the
lavish use of presents;[811] but, lest Britain grew too powerful, the
same speeches were made to France. The Tsar told Duroc—sent to
congratulate him on his accession—that France should not give up Egypt
(this was in June) and that the two powers should co-operate in curbing
British ambition in the Near East.[812] The Tsar promised to use his
influence to obtain for the Republic the privilege of navigation in the
Black Sea, only asking that this might be kept secret.[813]

There were now three courses before the Turks; to invest the British
Plenipotentiary at Amiens with authority to act in their name; to ratify
the preliminaries signed by Ali and enter upon separate negotiations
with France, or to accept the Russian mediation and transfer the
negotiations to Constantinople. To take one course to the exclusion of
the others was fraught with danger. The Porte could not afford to
alienate Britain, who held Egypt and had in the Mamelukes security for
the Porte’s good conduct. Nor could it entertain the thought of
incurring the wrath of the First Consul, whose friendship would prove a
welcome check on British power and whose enmity should be dreaded. The
Tsar, moreover, might soon weary of his quiet mood and was not likely to
bear meekly the rejection of his proffered mediation. The only hope of
safety lay in exploiting the rivalries of the three powers.

The Porte ratified the Preliminaries of London[814] and endeavoured to
persuade Elgin to consent to its ratifying Ali’s act “with some
modifications.”[815] The British Ambassador refused. He pointed out that
it had an excellent opportunity of displaying its real sentiments
towards Britain, its dispositions towards the French, its sense of
dignity and adherence to engagements—and all this could be done by
granting Lord Cornwallis, the Plenipotentiary at Amiens, authority to
act in its name.[816] He gave it the benefit of a commentary on the
terms agreed to by Ali. There was no difficulty in showing that the Turk
had no powers, that the instrument was otherwise unacceptable on account
of faulty drafting and differences from the act concluded in London. To
bestow on France, he concluded, privileges granted to the Allies was a
“procedure” humiliating to the Porte, unjust to the Allies and unmerited
by the French.[817] The Turkish ministers were loud in expressions of
gratitude.[818] They informed the French Government of the intention to
ratify the Preliminaries of Paris only in so far as they did not vary
from those of London, and to invest Ali with full powers to attend the
Congress of Amiens.[819]

Their courage, however, was soon put to a severe test, for there arrived
on the scene on the 26th of November, Colonel Sebastiani with[820] a
copy of the Preliminaries and an autograph letter to the Sultan[821]
which was to be delivered in person. The mission had other aims. The
desire of the Consul—so ran the instructions to Sebastiani[822]—was to
strengthen the Turkish Empire, to use her against Britain and Russia and
to realise, in a different way, the designs, which were hoped for by the
invasion of Egypt in 1798. “Il désire avant tout étendre l’influence
française dans ces régions grâce à une augmentation du commerce et
essayer de reconquérir l’Egypte et la route des Indes par les moyens
pacifiques puisqu’il n’a pu les conserver par les armes.” Sebastiani
was, therefore, to observe “dans quelles conditions s’effectuera
l’évacuation de l’Egypte et comment se fait la remise de ce pays aux
autorités ottomanes, quels sont leurs intentions, leurs projets pour
l’avenir de ce pays.” He was also to make a special study of Russo-
British relations and to disavow rumours of French complicity with
various rebellious Pashas.

The first “call”[823] of Sebastiani was on Tamara, the Russian envoy,
who happened to be celebrating the coronation of the Tsar. The Russian
showed a great deal of curiosity. Towards the end of the dinner he lost
all self-control, condemned the convention by Morkoff, expressed his
conviction that Codrika—the dragoman of Ali—was in French pay and
“promised” that he would have him beheaded. On the next day, however,
his language—now that he had apparently recovered from the effects of
the banquet—was entirely changed. He pressed the Turks to be less
obdurate on the point of etiquette and allow Sebastiani to deliver
personally the letter to the Sultan. In return, he asked the Colonel to
persuade the Porte to accept the Russian mediation. Sebastiani,
naturally, refused to do this, for he was himself endeavouring—now by
persuasion and now by threats—to press the Porte to negotiate separately
with his country. It was finally agreed that the Sultan should meet
Sebastiani “accidentally.” The Colonel presented himself in his uniform
(not in the customary pelisse) delivered his letter and a “suitable”
speech.[824] The Sultan enquired after the health of Bonaparte, and
expressed his pleasure at Sebastiani’s speech. All this, no doubt, was
bound to make a stir in Pera; but the fact remained that Ali’s
“Preliminaries” were still unratified.

The Russian envoy now tried what he could do. M. Tamara was a
naturalised Russian of Greek origin. “Il a,” wrote Sebastiani, “toute la
finesse et, j’ose dire, toute la fausseté de ses anciens
compatriotes.”[825] He seems to have been particularly amenable to
bribery.[826] It was on the 1st of December that he officially notified
the Porte of the Russian mediation.[827] His position must have been
embarrassing, for he had been pressing the Porte—in ignorance of
Morkoff’s Convention—to disavow Ali on the grounds that what he had done
was contrary to the Preliminaries of London. The Russian mediation was
not less contrary. No wonder that Tamara could offer only “lame
explanations” and unconvincing arguments.[828] The Turks, seeing that
France was pressing one thing and the Russians another, turned towards
Britain. Elgin advised them to thank Russia for her mediation, to
observe that it would have been more “efficacious and beneficial” had it
been accepted by France when she was in possession of Egypt, and to
state that the negotiations—except for “trifling arrangements of
trade”—would be completed in Amiens.[829]

This communication infuriated Tamara. He complained that Russia had been
treated like “an old shoe”; addressed “harsh” notes to the Reis Effendi,
and warned him that any concessions granted to Britain in Egypt were
sure to provoke French jealousy and a new attack on that province.[830]
The language of his dragoman was not less violent. He used to declare
“that Russia could have conquered Egypt as well as England, that she
offered forces repeatedly, that any concession to England in Egypt must
be common to all nations in amity with Turkey, that the restitution of
Egypt by England was no favour, for Europe would not have permitted her
to remain there.”[831] Elgin became so alarmed that he believed possible
a concert between France and Russia for the partition of the Turkish
Empire. He entreated Lord Hawkesbury to probe the matter before
evacuating Egypt and Malta. In the meantime he advised the Porte to ask
for delay.[832]

These alarming reports arrived in London in the early days of the new
year. They strengthened the suspicions entertained by the British
Government with regard to the real intentions of Alexander. His language
had not been clear and his somewhat ostentatious attitude of abstention
lent itself to misrepresentation. They had hoped a great deal from the
successor of Paul. They had believed that “no connection was more
natural and more reciprocally advantageous—especially under present
circumstances—than that between Russia and Britain.”[833] But Alexander
did not respond to British advances. He refused to take part in the
arrangement concerning Malta. In May, 1801, the British Ambassador was
instructed to ascertain “whether the Russian Government was disposed to
guarantee, jointly with His Majesty, the province of Egypt to the
Turkish Government in the event of the Expulsion of the French . . . the
guarantee might include mutual stipulations to settle the proportion of
force which each party should be obliged to furnish hereafter, should
Egypt be again attacked.”[834] The overture was, at any rate, premature:
the French were still strong in Egypt. The Ambassador not only did not
receive a reply but also suspected the causes of the delay.[835] In
point of fact, the delay was due to Panin, who was trying to persuade
the Tsar to consent to the joint guarantee.[836] “Malheureusement,”
wrote Panin, “l’Empereur ne pense pas de même et j’ai vu le moment où il
aurait consenti de plein gré à ce que les Français conservaient cette
colonie”[837] The Minister was “anxious that His Imperial Majesty should
take the leading part in the affairs of Egypt.”[838] The advocates of
non-intervention defeated him and succeeded in having him dismissed. The
overture of a joint guarantee was rejected on the grounds that the
Triple Alliance between Great Britain, Russia and the Porte was still in
existence and was sufficient.[839]

The rejection of the overture, the Russian mediation and the violence of
Tamara caused a very bad impression in London. The Cabinet was
“disinclined” to believe in the “opinion” prevalent in Naples, Vienna
and Constantinople to the effect that there was “a plan agreed upon
between France and Russia for the dismemberment of the Turkish
government.”[840] George III unburdened his soul to the sympathising
Simon Vorontsoff.[841] The Ambassador found Hawkesbury “accablé de
douleur.”[842] The Minister relieved his pent-up feelings in a strong
official note.[843] The Russian mediation he described as “a most
unfriendly act.” He trusted “that the Russian ministers would not permit
the agents of His Imperial Majesty in foreign countries to become agents
under the influence of other powers for the purpose of counteracting the
just pretensions and rights of His Majesty.” Vorontsoff—a warm friend of
Britain—was opposed to the policy of his country. But, being a true
Muscovite, he attacked the policy of the Tsar in the person of Panin, to
whom he attributed all that had taken place.[844]

The ill-humour of the British Government was well-founded. The mere fact
that Russia was pressing upon the Turks a third line of action meant a
division of front that was sure to be to the profit of France. Efforts
were now directed to obtain the admission of a Turkish Plenipotentiary
into the Congress, either as an acceding or a contracting party.[845] If
these succeeded, a separate peace between France and Turkey would be
prevented, and the French Government would have failed “to obtain
greater advantages with respect to the Porte than were held out to her
by the Preliminaries.”[846] The efforts of French diplomacy, naturally,
tended to prevent the admission of the Turks.[847]

Lord Cornwallis raised the question in the sessions of 11th and 18th
February, but on both occasions the French Plenipotentiary, Joseph
Bonaparte, reserved his answer.[848] It was in the session of 21st
February that he delivered what Talleyrand had instructed him to
say.[849] Joseph declared that the Porte had ratified the Preliminaries
of Paris in so far as they conformed to those of London, and so, as they
entirely conformed, they stood ratified. The Porte was, moreover,
anxious to conclude separately with the Republic and had invested Ali
with full powers to that effect. He informed the British of this only
“par honnêteté et déférence.” Only “direct negotiations” could lead to
treaties of peace between such “great” powers as the Republic and
Turkey, otherwise a state of war would only be replaced by a truce. To
act differently was to place both powers in the second order. Both of
them were, on the contrary, first-class states. The French delegate,
however, expressed his readiness to give a pledge that nothing would be
inserted in the treaty to be concluded with the Porte contrary to the
Preliminaries of London.

The statement of Talleyrand was a mass of sophistry and falsehood. Ali
had been pressing to be allowed to proceed to Amiens since January,
1802. In the early days of January, M. d’Hauterive, of the Ministry of
Foreign Affairs, told him (Talleyrand being then in Lyons) that even if
he went he could not be sure that Cornwallis or Joseph would consent to
negotiate with him. He advised Ali to wait and spend the time “more
agreeably in Paris.” The Turk expostulated on his difficult position. He
even spoke in French, “with a facility and warmth that surprised M.
d’Hauterive.”[850] His efforts proved of no avail.[851] M. Morkoff spoke
to Talleyrand on the subject, but the French Minister pretended that “it
would be too great a humiliation to the Turks not to allow them to
negotiate separately.” The Russian could not help a smile.[852] The
Greek dragoman, Codrika, mysteriously disappeared, and, without him,
Ali—in spite of his warm effusions in French—could transact no
business.[853]

Lord Cornwallis pointed out the falsity of the French statements with
regard to Ali,[854] so much so that Joseph had to request that Ali
should write to the British delegate, “dans le sens des dispositions que
j’ai communiqués dans ma dernière note à ce sujet.”[855] Talleyrand,
however, could not be so easily put out. He now declared that Ali was
instructed to act with Morkoff as well as Cornwallis, that all that was
meant by this was “concert” and not “action simultanée,” otherwise the
Russian envoy would also be admitted to the Congress.[856] On the 12th
March, Joseph Bonaparte, acting on the discretion allowed to him,[857]
“consented” to the admission of the Porte “as an acceding party.” There
followed some debates on the insertion of the words “ally of Great
Britain” with respect to the Porte.[858] The Treaty was finally signed
on the 25th March.

It was a great mistake on the part of the British Government not to
insist on the admission of a Turkish delegate. By presenting an
alternative demand, namely, the accession of the Porte, Britain
facilitated the task of French diplomacy. Separate negotiations were
made thereby inevitable.

The ground had been prepared for them by a letter from Talleyrand to the
Reis Effendi, dated the 18th January, 1802. The threat in it was
unmistakable. “La Sublime Porte,” so it ran, “n’aurait à s’en prendre
qu’à elle-même et à son refus de rétablir les anciens rapports qui
subsistaient entre les deux états, si la France, justement blessée du
peu d’égards que l’on aurait eus pour ses efforts et pour son amitié,
faisait servir son influence et son immense force à faire tourner contre
la puissance qui l’aurait dédaignée tous les événements politiques qui
peuvent avoir lieu.”[859] “The principles of the Porte were staggered,”
wrote Lord Elgin;[860] he had to use threats. He pointed out that—in the
event of acquiescence in the demands of France—he would have to acquaint
the naval and military commanders in the Mediterranean and gave the
warning “that the fate of Egypt would be considered from a different
point of view.”[861] But what could the Turks do? Great Britain had
failed to obtain the admission of their delegate to the Congress. It was
Lord Elgin, himself, who advised them to send to Paris or Amiens “some
person well versed in the situation who, after examining everything
would take a decision on the spot conformable to the state of affairs as
he might find them.”[862]

The advice was highly acceptable, the more so as Tamara had now come
forward with a new Russian proposal. The protests of Great Britain and
Simon Vorontsoff had had their effect in St. Petersburg. All that
happened was laid to the account of the unfortunate Tamara. He was to be
recalled, and, in the meantime, “severely reprimanded.”[863] “There
never was a period,” wrote Lord St. Helens, “even while France and
Russia were actually in a state of professional warfare, when the
Government of this country was less disposed to place confidence in that
of France, or even to keep up with that power the ordinary relations of
amity and good correspondence.”[864] But still Russia could not forego
all share in the Turkish peace. She now proposed that the Franco-Turkish
negotiations should be transacted in Paris under the mediation and
direction of the British and Russian ministers in that capital.[865]
Elgin refused to support Tamara[866] and the Porte satisfied itself by
promising to instruct its plenipotentiary in the sense of the Russian
demand.[867]

The British Cabinet was still in its ill-humour. It refused to accept
the Russian proposal—on the grounds that even if it were reasonable in
itself it came too late.[868] This was another blunder. For it enabled
the First Consul to disregard the Russian demand and have a free hand
with the Turk. Galeb, the Turkish Plenipotentiary, “was a man of much
experience and apt in business, of clear judgment and an accurate
knowledge of the views and interests of the Porte.”[869] But he received
no support from the Allies of the Porte and had to consent to the
according of the navigation of the Black Sea to France.[870]

The Franco-Turkish Treaty was signed on the 25th June, 1802, and
ratified in August of the same year.[871]

This act concluded the pacification of Europe. Commenting on what had
passed, Dr. Rose states that “Bonaparte saw that a separate treaty with
the Sublime Porte would enable him not only to extract valuable trading
concessions in the Black Sea trade, but also to cement a good
understanding with Russia on the Eastern question, which was now being
adroitly reopened by French diplomacy. Against the exclusion of Turkey
from the negotiations at Amiens, Great Britain firmly but vainly
protested. In fact, Talleyrand had bound the Porte to a separate
agreement which promised everything for France and nothing for Turkey,
and seemed to doom the Porte to certain humiliation and probable
partition.”[872] This view is hardly tenable. As has been pointed out,
the action of the First Consul was not exactly directed to “cement a
good understanding with Russia” with a view to a probable partition of
the Turkish Empire. He used Russia, on the contrary, to defeat British
diplomacy and succeeded in depriving Britain of all the advantages which
her occupation of Egypt ought to have conferred upon her. The victory of
Bonaparte did not lie in the terms of the Treaty concluded with
Galeb—for it does not contain anything contrary to the Treaty of
Amiens—but in his contriving to deprive both Russia and Britain of the
strong position in Constantinople which the Expedition to Egypt had
enabled them to occupy, and to regain for France the place which her
attempt to seize Egypt had caused her to lose. “I am anxious to declare
positively,” wrote Lord Elgin, “as a conviction stamped upon my mind by
the whole of this transaction, that Great Britain, in pursuing her
general principles of politics, can never attempt to maintain a
preponderating influence here or rely on any favour or return from hence
for supporting this country after France shall resume her position and
connection in Europe.”[873]
