 X

 THE FIRST BRITISH OCCUPATION OF EGYPT
 1801-1803


The general success of British policy has misled continental writers as
to its essentially conservative trend. One searches in vain for schemes
of expansion or empire; for “it was the active far-seeing and ambitious
statecraft of France which opened up new arenas of strife and ambition,
while Britons followed doubtfully at first, but in the long run
doggedly. Dupleix, Montcalm, and Napoleon—to mention the greatest
names—precipitated a conflict which was ultimately to turn to the profit
of the Sea Power and to plant the Union Jack on many lands.” “C’est
ainsi que presque toujours nous avons ouvert les voies à des nations,
non plus ingénieuses mais plus persévérantes et plus sages que
nous.”[874] writes M. Pelissier, of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs; and
he asks dolefully, “Serons-nous donc toujours destinés à montrer comme
Moïse le chemin de Terre Promise sans qu’il nous soit jamais donné de
nous y établir solidement?”[875]

There can be no better illustration of that characteristic of British
foreign policy than the scrupulous abstention from exploiting the Turko-
British Alliance for commercial privileges in the Levant.[876] Lord
Elgin, the British Ambassador at Constantinople, who drew part of his
appointments from the Levant Company, refrained from seeking openings
for British trade either in the Red or the Black Seas. In point of fact,
Britain did not press the demand for permission to navigate in the
latter sea till after France had obtained it by her treaty with Turkey
of 1802.[877] The far-reaching policy of Wellesley—probably the first of
British statesmen to think, as it were, in terms of imperial
geography—in establishing relations with the Shah of Persia, the Imam of
Muscat and other Arab chiefs was flatly opposed by the Directors of the
Company.[878] Enterprises were dreaded as likely to produce revolutions,
and revolutions were regarded as having no other but injurious effects.

“Egypt,” wrote Captain Blankett to Windham from the Coast of East
Africa, “must not remain in an active state or in active hands.”[879]
This well expresses the thought of the British Cabinet. There is
evidence that the ultimate fate of Egypt was discussed long before it
was decided to send an army to dislodge the French. The decision of
Pitt, Grenville and Dundas, as Lord Elgin then understood it to be, was
that it would be the wish of His Majesty’s Government to restore Egypt
to the Turks.[880]

There was no departure from this principle after the arrival of the news
of the first successes of Sir Ralph Abercromby. The Russian Ambassador,
Simon Vorontsoff, represented to Thomas Grenville, brother of Lord
Grenville, the necessity of “not entirely abandoning” the coast of
Egypt.[881] Lord Grenville, now out of office, recommended Addington,
the new Prime Minister, to adopt the policy of the provisional
occupation of Alexandria and other posts.[882] The new Cabinet “entirely
concurred,”[883] and Hutchinson and Elgin were both instructed to that
effect.[884] But, it should be remembered, the provisional occupation
had been intended from the beginning.[885]

No decision, however, was taken as to the settlement of the affairs of
the country after the expulsion of the French. The only guide the
British Commander had was a private letter from Huskisson.[886] The
Under-Secretary conveyed to Abercromby “various suggestions” which had
occurred to him in consequence of conversations he had had with a “well-
informed gentleman.” Huskisson recognised that a coalition between the
Beys and the French or even their neutrality would add to the
difficulties of the campaign, but feared that “any intercourse” with
them might arouse the “jealousy” of the Turks, the allies of Britain.
He, therefore, recommended “caution and management.” It was reasonable
also to assume that the Beys would not voluntarily declare against the
French, for the Turks had “very foolishly” made clear their intention of
destroying the power of the Mamelukes. It was probable, in consequence,
that the “Cophts,” the Greeks and the mass of the people would side with
the Beys against the Porte. It was also almost certain that none would
respond to any promises, which the Grand Vizier might with “Turkish
treachery” make, till assurances had been given by the British to see
justice done. Were they to give these assurances or not? Huskisson
observed that it was a “most delicate affair,” that Abercromby should
obtain “much information” from Sir Sidney Smith, that if he could secure
the Vizier’s concurrence in detaching the Mamelukes from France it would
be well; but should the Turk, however, “prove headstrong and blind,” the
success of the expedition should by no means be jeopardised through the
“vanity, ignorance and arrogance” of the Turkish Councils and Turkey
should be saved in spite of herself.

This letter makes it clear that the British Government was not ignorant
of the problem. The resolution of the Porte to have done with Mameluke
usurpation had been reported by Elgin and by Tamara, the Russian envoy
at Constantinople.[887] The strained relations between the Turks and the
Beys in the Grand Vizier’s camp had been described by General Koehler,
then attached to Turkish headquarters.[888] Ignorance, therefore, was no
plea for refusing to face a difficulty. It was no solution to recommend
officers not to meddle in Turkish domestic affairs, for an army
operating in a country could not very well avoid interfering in matters
affecting the success of its operations and even its very security.
Events proved this to be so.

Abercromby, before setting foot in Egypt, wrote Mourad—the Bey who had
made his peace with the French—a “general letter of friendship” without
making any specific promises.[889] Sidney Smith had been occupied all
through 1800 in establishing connections with the Beys in Egypt and
Syria.[890] His chief agents in this were the Austrian, Hammer,[891] and
his own secretary, Keith. These activities gave umbrage to the Porte.
Hammer was “banished” by the British commander and Sidney Smith was sent
back to his ship.[892] But Elgin persuaded the Porte to issue a
proclamation promising forgetfulness of the past and rewards for good
conduct.[893] This, however, could have hardly tranquillised Mourad. His
position was difficult. He aspired undoubtedly to regain his old power,
but he was weary of war and by no means certain of the ultimate triumph
of the enemies of France. He feared that the Turks might bestow their
favours on his rival, Ibrahim, who had been with them since 1798. He
feared still more that they would play false with Mouradites and
Ibrahimites alike and take vengeance for past insults and disobedience.
“The arrow,” as he put it, “that struck the eagle was an arrow made of
an eagle’s feather.”[894]

The assurances of the British, however, determined him to cast in his
lot against the French. His scheme,[895] according to Elfi, was to
propose the re-establishment, as far as possible, of things as they were
in 1798; the Beys were to pay the Porte an annual tribute of 1200
purses, besides 30,000 purses for the expenses of the war (in six annual
instalments), the British were to guarantee this settlement in return
for the possession of Alexandria, Rosetta and Damietta. Mourad was
actually moving towards the scene of operations when he died of the
plague. His successor, Tambourdji, was a fine warrior but devoid of the
judgment which misfortune had ripened in his predecessor. He sent a
confidential agent to Sir John Hutchinson and deemed the assurances
received sufficient. He joined the British Army on the 15th May,
1801.[896]

It is important to enter closely into the motives of Hutchinson and to
determine, as far as is practicable, the nature of the engagements he
undertook. He himself endeavoured later to justify his conduct on
grounds of military expediency.[897] But his earlier despatches clearly
show that he looked beyond the needs of the hour and tried to settle the
affairs of Egypt for the future. Thus before he left his camp near
Alexandria on the march towards Cairo he wrote[898] Dundas that he was
in doubt as to the real attitude of Mourad, that the Bey seemed to be
the doubtful ally of the French, and certainly not the friend of the
Turks, though he had lately sent a confidential messenger to the Capitan
Pasha soliciting oblivion and pardon, which were granted—probably with
as much sincerity as they were asked. Hutchinson went on to make the
significant observations that the Turks were in a “deplorable state,”
that their Empire was falling to pieces, that he “thought no event so
unlikely as that Egypt would long continue to belong to Turkey, that it
would probably fall to the lot of some European Power.” He expressed
identical views in other reports.[899] In a letter[900] to Lord Elgin,
he made a fuller statement. His position was most difficult. “The Turks
without money, without provisions, without resources of any kind,
pillage or plunder everything that fall into their hands.” Any
interference on his part would arouse their enmity and any acquiescence
would alienate the “natives” from the British. The Sultan’s dominion had
ever been nominal “almost since the time of Selim.” Great Britain,
therefore, would probably give him “a hold of Egypt” which he never
before possessed. But that “temporary hold” would rather weaken than
strengthen the Turkish Empire. The point was that some European power,
probably France, would step in as easily as it did in 1798. How was the
danger to be averted? By the indefinite occupation of Alexandria?
Hutchinson foresaw great objections in this solution. In the first
place, it would entail no less than 7,000 or 8,000 men. In the second
place, the British force must have an unimpeded communication with the
interior and with the mouths of the Nile. The inference to be drawn from
this, but which he avoided drawing, was that Rosetta and Damietta and
posts in the interior would have to be garrisoned as well as Alexandria.
He feared that the “pride, ignorance and jealousy of the Turks would
prevent them ever agreeing to these concessions, that the problem of
Egypt would be an insoluble one and that the Porte would soon revert to
its “old and natural connection with France.”

The proper interpretation of this statement is that the British
commander was convinced that the Turks would prove incapable of
defending the country against France. At the same time he was averse
from laying the burden on his own country. There was nothing for it but
to entrust the Beys with that duty. Hence his engagements to them.

To believe Hutchinson, “he never entered into any stipulations with the
Mamelukes which were not reasonable in the extreme. They had desired
nothing but protection for their lives and security for their properties
under the guarantee of the British government.”[901] But it is certain,
however, that the letter which he wrote them on the 5th May, 1801,[902]
while it is vaguely worded, “opened a large field to their
expectation.”[903] A passage like this, . . . “We have solely come to
drive out the French usurpers who have overset and ruined the country
and to establish all in those possessions and rights, which belonged to
them,” can lend itself to any interpretation. The Beys understood
British promises to mean “an ample indemnification for their losses and
the full restitution of their property, power and influence in the
country”[904] and emphatically called the letter of the 5th May “their
charter.” Elfi was right to criticise the affair as “done without union,
order, or any definite objects.”[905]

The promises were not immediately carried out. The exigencies of the
command allowed Hutchinson time for no more than a “talk” with the
Capitan Pasha. The Turk merely gave “solemn assurances.” After the
surrender of Cairo, the Mamelukes pressed for the performance of the
engagements and pointed out that the Turks were manifesting the “most
hostile intentions” against them. The British general accordingly saw
the Capitan Pasha, but only obtained “fine exclamations.”[906] On the
evening of the 9th July, 1801, he brought the subject before the Grand
Vizier. “His Excellency” expressed his satisfaction, accepted his
intercession and promised to restore the Beys to their villages and
properties. The Capitan Pasha concurred in the views of his colleague
and added a “panegyric” on Hutchinson’s character and the “honesty of
the British Government.” These excellent professions were,
unfortunately, belied by unmistakable symptoms of hostility towards the
Beys. The latter, on the 14th July, made bitter representations and
announced the intention of withdrawing to Upper Egypt, if justice were
not meted out to them. It was most important to avert this step for, it
is to be remembered, Menou was still holding out in Alexandria and
French reinforcements were in daily expectation. The British Commander,
accordingly, “determined to go through with the business and convince
the Turks that he was no longer to be trifled with.”[907]

What passed between him and the Turks is not exactly known. Colonel
Anstruther, who was employed in the business, outlined in a later
statement[908] Hutchinson’s plan as follows:—

1. That the Turks were to possess exclusively Alexandria, Rosetta and
Damietta and keep a garrison in the Citadel of Cairo. This condition,
however, is not stated so definitely in the general’s reports. “He did
not touch upon Alexandria and Rosetta for obvious reasons.”[909] The
“obvious reasons” are the instructions from the Government to occupy
them himself.[910]

2. That the Pasha should be appointed as formerly by the Porte, that he
was to decide all disputes betwixt the Beys, appoint their chiefs in the
event of a vacancy, and have the general government of the country.

3. That the “miri” or land-tax should be augmented and paid without any
deductions whatever to a Turkish receiver-general.

4. That the Beys should be reinstated in all their properties, have the
entire management of them, subject, however, to certain limitations with
regard to the rents to be levied on the peasants and under the
obligation of keeping up a certain number of men in proportion to their
respective properties.

The Beys, after some bickering, accepted these terms. The Turks,
according to British reports, assented to them, after some hesitation,
without reserve.[911] But in his reports to Constantinople, the Grand
Vizier made out that he did nothing of the sort. “En considération des
services qu’il (Hutchinson) avait rendus, le Grand Vizier n’a pas
répondu à sa note par un refus direct pour ne pas lui faire de la peine,
mais il lui adressa un billet en réponse où, sans entrer en détail, il
lui marqua en termes généraux qu’il aurait employé ses soins au sujet
des Beys . . . réponse qui ne porte point le caractère de promesse ou
d’engagement et qui l’on peut moins encore appeler du nom de condition
ou de stipulation quelconque.”[912] The Capitan Pasha disavowed any
share in the transaction.[913]

It was clear that the Turks were determined to carry out none of the
terms they seemed to accept.[914] The Beys had identical intentions. It
is curious that Hutchinson did not realise this. It is possible to
admire his sympathy with the under-dog, but it is difficult to
understand the illusion not only of Hutchinson, but also of his
colleagues as to the real character of the Mamelukes. “Que de
supériorité,” wrote a French _émigré_ in British service, “les Mamelukes
n’avaient-ils sur les Turcs! francs, loyaux, généreux et braves, tous
étaient en leur faveur. Nous les regardons pour ainsi dire comme des
nôtres.”[915] “There never was a stranger mistake than that of these
gentlemen (British officers in Egypt) who thought of reposing confidence
in the honour of the Beys, who believed them to be a highly-minded,
generous race of men, and who seemed almost to fancy in the exaltation
of their romantic enthusiasm that they had found in the Mamelukes the
courage, the sentiments and virtues of heroes.”[916]

The “atmosphere” had thus become most unsuitable for the carrying out of
a scheme long maturing in London. The Cabinet had at its disposal the
clear representations of Hutchinson, but it was not in a hurry to make a
pronouncement. It was not that “His Majesty’s confidential servants were
insensible to the magnitude of the problem, but because circumstances
had unavoidably narrowed it to their views.”[917] “Expert evidence” was
not wanting. J. P. Morier, of the embassy in Constantinople, had been
sent by the Ambassador expressly for the purpose of giving first-hand
information and advice. He examined the problem very fully in an
interesting memorandum of 7th July, 1801.[918] The question was: should
Egypt be delivered to the Turks, whose right was nominal, or to the
Mamelukes whose right was that of conquest? There was no comfort in
either solution, for neither of the two parties could well defend the
country. The failure of the French was, indeed, only due “to the want of
proportion between the means employed and the magnitude of the
enterprise.” In the opinion of Morier, the solution lay along one or
other of three lines. The first was British occupation, open and
unabashed; it was somewhat “naïve” on the part of Mr. Morier to suggest
that the Turks should give, “in return,” “certain commercial
advantages.” An _entente_ between Great Britain and Turkey might
conceivably be created. The Beys, in that event, would naturally pay the
piper. A second alternative was that “Egypt might be kept upon the
principle which the French had adopted,” namely, to take possession of
the country while acknowledging the suzerainty of the Porte and even
offering to pay a tribute. Should this be adopted, “it would be prudent
and politic to reinstate some of the most popular Beys in the
governments of districts contiguous to our own.” (Morier already uses
the possessive pronoun.) To “shuffle” them from one district to another
and to leave with everyone a British “commissary.” The idea was to avoid
the “odium attached to invaders and the difficulties arising from the
prejudices and fanaticism of the ‘natives’ before they had tasted the
sweets of a mild and regular government.” It was to be feared that
Russia and France might unite with Turkey to prevent this from taking
place. A third alternative was to destroy Egypt by inundation. It is
only fair to add that he went on to state that should this be done, his
country “would transmit to posterity a name as black as that of the
incendiary of Ephesus.” Morier was evidently bewildered by the
insolubility of the problem.

The Cabinet adopted none of his alternatives. It reaffirmed its self-
denying ordinance[919] and informed Elgin that under the circumstances
the “obvious policy” would be for Britain to act as far as possible “in
the character of mediator between the different parties.” To help her
agents to arrive at that desirable result, “a paper with the ideas of
the ministers” was sent them. The Ambassador was not to allow it to fall
into anyone’s hand and was to communicate to the Porte only extracts of
it as he thought fit.[920] The ministers were satisfied that the Porte
“appeared to have a just sense of the necessity of their acting upon a
systematic plan for the future government of Egypt,” and were hopeful
“that measures might be suggested which would cause the British name to
be hereafter as much reverenced by all descriptions of the people of
Egypt for procuring to them the blessings of security under a regular
government as they have been taught by the gallant army . . . to respect
it for distinguished valour.”[921]

What were these “ideas of the ministers”?[922] In form they offered the
greatest possible contrast to the creations of Menou’s fertile mind. The
British scheme, as was proper, bore the cachet of the practical; it
burnt no incense to liberty or to equality and omitted to theorise on
“feudal principle” or _impôt unique_. Had it been carried into effect,
however, it would have proved just as revolutionary as any of Menou’s
projects.

It was the first authoritative pronouncement on the problem of Egypt.
The second was the famous Dufferin report of 1883.[923] A slight
comparison between the two documents reveals how fast events had moved
between 1801 and 1883. During the interval, the viceroys had built up
their power, expelled the Turks, welded the heterogeneous elements into
a compact nation, accumulated a burdensome debt and attracted into the
country a powerful _clientèle_ from all lands. The plan of 1801 was
intended to make the presence of the British unnecessary; in 1883 the
British occupation was commencing its indefinite course.[924]

The British ministers summed up the “condition of Egypt” prior to 1798
in three “facts”: the sovereignty of the Porte was nominal, the
Mamelukes were masters of the situation, the people were most wretched.
Had the French been suffered to remain, there could be no doubt that
they would have effected a revolution in the social system of the
country. The same result would have taken place, had Great Britain been
in the place of France. The withdrawal of the British Army would be
followed very probably by a relapse into the anarchy and the oppression
that facilitated the French invasion in 1798. To prevent the recurrence
of the evil the Porte should carry out the following measures; Great
Britain being inclined to take part in the “arrangement.”

1. The rights, privileges and territorial jurisdiction of the Mamelukes
should be ascertained, the nature and extent of their military services
should be defined and the performance of that service should be made the
condition of their tenure.[925]

2. The revenues of the State, from all sources, should be placed under
fixed regulations, the “rates and proportions” to be paid to the State
under each head should be established and the collectors should be
severely punished if they exacted more than was due.

3. A fixed portion of the revenue should be appropriated for the pay and
disbursements of the regular military force to be formed under the
direction of British officers.

4. The “nizamia”[926] troops now serving in Egypt should remain there
and form the nucleus of the new establishment.

5. The remainder of the establishment should be recruited in Egypt,
Albania and other European dominions of the Grand Signior.

6. The chief command should be vested, if possible, in a British
officer, and no payments from the appropriated funds should be made
without his orders.

7. The commander should have the power to make “respectful
remonstrances” to the representative of the Porte in Egypt whenever the
privileges of the Mamelukes, the recognised rights of the people, the
collection or the application of the military funds were infringed.

8. The right of promotion and military administration should be entirely
his.

9. During the continuation of the war, Alexandria should be garrisoned
by troops in the British service to be paid from the appropriated funds.

There could have been no fairer and more beneficial a settlement than
this. But it had from the outset no chance. The “many clashing
interests” were too difficult “to reconcile.”[927] “The Porte refused to
acknowledge what its representatives had agreed to; the interposition of
England was delayed from day to day, in expectation of positive orders
from home, and it appeared that, from the want of a communication
sufficiently full and explicit at first and from the different lights in
which the whole business had been reviewed and represented at Alexandria
and Constantinople, the opinions which were held and acted upon at the
one place were decidedly opposite to those which were held and acted
upon at the other.”[928]

A large share of the responsibility for this unhappy state of affairs
rests with the Ambassador at Constantinople. The “Elgin marbles” have
shed lustre on a diplomat of mediocre ability and doubtful
integrity.[929] Pitt used to describe him as “the most discreet of His
Majesty’s ministers, for he never reveals what he knows even to the
Secretary of State.”[930] With all his “cant jargon,” he was not a match
for the Divan.[931] He was in the habit of delivering “as his own
private opinion” what he had been instructed to communicate to the
Porte. His object was to “commit His Majesty’s ministers as little as
possible.”[932] The mischief of this lay in the fact that what was
communicated as “private opinion” could not, naturally, have with the
Divan the weight of an authoritative statement of the views of the
Government. His desire to commit the Government as little as possible
proceeded from some vague, inarticulate ambition on his part. As early
as February, 1801, he conceived the inactivity of the Turks in the
operations in Egypt to be “a good thing,” as it would entitle Britain
“to some compensation.”[933] Three months later, alluding to the
conversations he had with Pitt, Dundas and Grenville, on the intention
to restore Egypt to the Porte, he observed that “the events . . . which
have taken place in Europe since that period . . . may lead to
combinations that were then unforeseen.”[934] The pity of it is that he
did nothing to prosecute an ambitious scheme.

In the early days of June, 1801, long before the relations of Hutchinson
and the Mamelukes were exactly known, a conference took place between
the Reis Effendi and Elgin on the affairs of Egypt.[935] The Turkish
Minister demanded a provisional British occupation and advice on the
internal settlement.[936] Elgin, who had kept from[937] the Porte—as was
his wont—his instructions to ask authorisation for the garrisoning of
Alexandria by British troops till the general peace, was naturally
delighted that the demand came from the Turks. He affected to see some
obstacles against this, but finally “gave way” and promised to request
Hutchinson to do what he could for the Porte. On the second point, he
“advised,” on the 20th June, the adoption of the following measures: the
separation of the administration of justice and the collection of
revenue from the military establishment, the division of the country
into a number of small pashaliks, the strengthening of the army, the
fixing of the amount of pay and salaries, the bestowal upon the
Mamelukes of the government of the territory above Girgeh in Upper
Egypt, and the conciliation of the Bedouins.[938] He followed this by
sending his secretary, William Hamilton, to Egypt for first-hand
information. But[939] he did nothing to shape the arrangement entered
into by Hutchinson into definite and workable terms. In the month of
September he was still carrying on “inconclusive discussions” on the
extent of British pledges.[940] The Turkish ministers argued that the
British General certainly went beyond the intentions of his Government
if he meant to replace the Beys in their old position and requested the
Ambassador to introduce such modifications in Hutchinson’s pledges “as
might still leave the Mamelukes an adequate compensation for their
services without endangering the government of the Porte,”[941] and to
“propose that each Bey and Kashef should individually collect and ensure
their respective properties, that they should quit Cairo and assume
distinguished situations in other parts of the Turkish Empire and that
the Mamelukes should be received, as a military corps with proper
emoluments, into the service of the Porte.”[942] Elgin admitted that
“the discussion enabled him to see how far he could start negotiating on
the plan of Hawkesbury.”[943] But he never started. His idea was to
visit Egypt himself and settle the matter on the spot,[944] though one
could not help suspecting that the motive was the collection of
antiquities. The Turks affected[945] eagerness that this should take
place soon, and M. Tamara, the Russian envoy, considered that the trip
was “beneficial.”[946] The Ambassador “hoped that if any accident
happened to him, his family might be allowed to consider it as happening
in the execution of duty.”[947] His Excellency, however, was to be
denied the renown of martyrdom. The voyage was postponed from day to
day, till the affair of the separate peace with France made his presence
at Constantinople indispensable.[948]

What passed in the capital thus added to the complications in
Egypt.[949] There was, on the one hand, the deadly rivalry between the
Capitan Pasha and the Grand Vizier and the consequent desire of each of
them to show greater zeal—or, rather, ruthlessness—in the service of the
Sultan.[950] There was, on the other, great dissension and blindness
among the Beys. Some “were little better than beggars at the gates of
the Vizier,” “supplicating indeed through form” but very distrustful:
some “pretended to act with deeper policy” and made friends with Turks
and British, while several were still attached to France.[951] Elfi was
the only one who saw clear and acted well. He advised his brethren to
withdraw from Cairo and frankly put themselves under British protection,
but they very foolishly refused to seek the mediation of
“infidels.”[952] Elfi coolly told them that the Sultan himself had
sought and benefited from the alliance with these very “infidels” and
began to act on his own account. He persuaded the Grand Vizier to let
him proceed to Upper Egypt to collect the estates of several people who
had died in the previous year’s plague without leaving issue. There he
fed the “imperial” cupidity for some time and waited in safety for
events.[953]

“It was plain” that an “explosion” was imminent, but “none ventured to
predict from what quarter it would come.”[954] Hutchinson warned the
Beys not to “enter a Turkish vessel or boat.” But they were too jealous
of each other to show suspicion. At the invitation of the Capitan Pasha,
the leading Mouradites proceeded to Alexandria. General Baird, commander
of the Indian troops in the neighbourhood, took some measures for their
protection.[955] On the 22nd October, 1801, they were embarked in two
barges, but after some time the Capitan Pasha left them on the pretext
of the arrival of important despatches. As soon as he withdrew, the
barges changed course and made towards a large gunboat. “Upon getting
alongside, before they had the opportunity of resistance or even drawing
their swords, they were fired upon from the lower part of the boat and
five of them instantly fell.” The rest were arrested and taken on
board.[956]

Hutchinson believed that the murder was premeditated. He adduced as
evidence the fact that the Pasha ordered the execution of the Greek
sailors, who had completely failed to accomplish the design.[957] The
Greeks fled and took refuge with the British, affirming “on the Gospel”
“that they were told that the Beys were brave and resolute and not
likely to surrender without resistance, that, therefore, they were
determined they should make none and fired upon them before they could
put themselves in a posture of defence.”[958] The Capitan Pasha, on the
other hand, “confessed that he disapproved the mode of attaining the
object (i.e. the arrest of the Beys), that he had at first remonstrated
and had not executed the orders till they were insisted upon by the
Grand Vizier” . . . that the intention was to send them to
Constantinople, that had their destruction been meant, they would have
been slaughtered on board.[959] The “murder” does not seem to have been
a part of the programme, for, almost at the same time, the Grand Vizier
in Cairo was able to apprehend the Ibrahamites without bloodshed.[960]

The Turks thus attempted their own “solution” of the problem. Hutchinson
was infuriated and individual soldiers and officers were even anxious
for a “scuffle.”[961] The British Commander delivered an “ultimatum” for
the delivery of the prisoners and began to march troops to enforce his
demand,[962] though he was most anxious that matters might not be pushed
to extremes.[963] The Turks very wisely liberated the Beys, but feeling
ran so high that the opportunity for a compromise was irreparably
lost.[964] The Turks could never forget that the prize was in their
grasp and had now escaped them.

Elgin awoke at last to the urgency of the problem.[965] He decided to
send Mr. Straton, the Secretary of the Embassy, to settle the quarrel.
He was now convinced “that His Majesty’s intention of not interfering in
the internal affairs of Egypt was ever distinct and positive, that no
engagement of Sir John Hutchinson’s could be right if of a nature to
endanger the authority of the Porte.”[966] Straton was, accordingly,
instructed to persuade the Beys to leave the country, and was even
authorised “to engage in his name that they should be received in
England or British-India and should enjoy pensions equal to those
offered by the Porte in return for military service.”[967]

Arrived in Egypt, Straton communicated his instructions to Lord Cavan,
the successor of Hutchinson. The new commander, however, made it clear
that “he neither could nor would use any violent means to send the Beys
out of Egypt or even to detain them at Gizeh[968] longer than they
should think proper.”[969] But he sounded the Beys secretly. They flatly
refused to consent to exile and announced their intention to withdraw to
Upper Egypt. The Turks tried to prevent this. But Cavan would neither
consent to detain them nor to issue, at the request of the Grand Vizier,
a statement denying the assertion that the Beys were under British
protection. He based his refusal on the curious plea—after all that had
passed—that such an act would constitute an interference in Turkish
domestic affairs.[970]

Cavan thus was certainly not the partisan of the Turks that he was taken
to be, nor did his command mark, as some French historians allege, a
change on the part of Great Britain from patronising the Mamelukes to
throwing them overboard.[971] It was true that neither he nor Straton
were very capable:[972] but under the circumstances the failure simply
could not be averted. The Beys left for Upper Egypt on 25th January,
1802, and a dismal chapter in the history of the country began.[973]

When the unfortunate event of October was known in London, the good
opinion entertained of the Turk received a serious setback. A British
Government could hardly refuse to approve an act of humanity on the part
of its generals.[974] The Ambassador was instructed to express
disapproval of the conduct of the Turkish officials “in a calm and
temperate manner.”[975] But the Addington Administration was weak. It
was only too conscious of the importance of settling the affairs of
Egypt, but it was lost between the clashing opinions submitted to it.
Lord Elgin, on the one hand, was sending veiled attacks on the conduct
of the generals and expatiating—rather boldly—on the “humaneness” of the
Turk.[976] Lord Hutchinson, on the other, was ruthlessly tearing into
shreds the plan, which the ministers had brought forth with exaggerated
hopefulness. Hutchinson, it is important to remember, did not receive
that plan till after the “massacre” had taken place and he himself had
left the country.[977] This naturally colours his criticism; but, still,
his comments possess more than passing interest. The question was not
the future of this or that Ottoman province: it was the prospects of
Turkish Reform.

[978]Hutchinson thought that though the Mameluke system “was certainly
not to be applauded,” it was “toleration and mildness” when compared to
that of the Turks. It had the advantage, moreover, of being the one to
which the inhabitants had become used. As to the suggested employment of
Albanians, it was, in his opinion, fraught with danger: they were too
“ferocious” to be useful, they would not serve under Christian officers,
but would only follow “some favourable officer, who had probably been a
robber,” like Tahir Pasha, their “present” commander. The Turks, also,
would only employ “renegadoes.” They were, besides, “not susceptible of
improvement”; and “he never could betray his own opinion so far as to
assert that the presence of the British officers had instilled one
single principle of military order or discipline amongst them.”

The “proposed financial arrangement” was certainly most desirable, but
it would “take years of peace, order and good government to effectuate”
and, in the end, the difficulties might prove insurmountable. The French
example was instructive. They attempted “some changes,” but ended by
giving them up and resorting to the traditional bastinado. The British,
after all was said and done, would have to take the whole arrangement
into their own hands or else the funds would be misapplied and the
troops would mutiny. He could only, therefore, give a “decided opinion”
against “entering into any permanent arrangement with the Turks for
Egypt,” “for the country that adopted them would soon repent of its
error and find that it was hanging a living body to a putrid carcass.”
“As for establishing a regular system of government in Egypt with the
Turks in the country, no man could conceive it probable. Before it could
take place, a revolution in their habits, their morals and their
government must occur. They must be taught to think as they never yet
thought and to act as they never yet had acted. It was not in the power
or competency of any foreign nation to effect such a reformation.
Inveterate habit had so confirmed their abominable system that he was
thoroughly convinced it would never change but with the downfall of
their Empire.”

There was great truth in all this, but only a Pitt would have had the
strength to “take it or leave it.” As it happened, Stuart gave the
Government different and more congenial advice and thus occasioned
another compromise.[979] In his view, the best policy would be “to
endeavour to restore in its true spirit the Mameluke Government,
increasing perhaps the tribute of the Beys under British guarantee.”
This guarantee would necessitate the presence of a British officer as
agent, who might remonstrate against infractions and even receive the
dues of the Porte. The advantage of this arrangement would be to secure
the good-will and gratitude of the Mamelukes. These were certain—when
left to themselves—to drive out the Turks, so Great Britain might as
well help them and attach them to herself. He believed that they would
prove “the most efficient barrier and now the only one within reach
against the immediate enterprises of the French,” and that they were
inclined to submit to “partial sacrifices,” and he recommended that
whatever plan might be adopted the Turks should not be left “in
dominion.”

The Government decided to send Stuart on a mission of reconciliation. He
was given the chief command in Egypt[980] and was instructed[981] to go
first to Constantinople and, then, in concert with the Ambassador, to
persuade the Porte “to restore the _status quo_ with an augmentation of
the tribute. Should this prove unacceptable, Stuart was to advise the
Divan to grant the Beys the Upper Egyptian provinces above Gizeh.” The
ministers expressed their anxiety that a settlement should be arrived at
before the evacuation took place, as the occupation would not be
prolonged—except from an “indispensable necessity”—beyond the month of
July (1802). They, however, passed over in silence some significant
queries raised by the General.[982] He had asked what he should do if
the Turks and the Mamelukes were in “active hostility” and one party had
acquired such advantages as should indispose it against reconciliation,
or if the Turks still adhered to their original scheme of exile and
showed an “intention” of calling in French help, or if they accepted
British mediation, but requested a guarantee for the execution of an
agreed arrangement. What should he do if the Porte “requested” him to
postpone the evacuation? Again, supposing that by the time he arrived in
Egypt, the British force had been withdrawn “by any construction of
orders,” what should be his conduct? Stuart had an “object” in all this.
He hinted at establishing a connection with the Mamelukes at the expense
of the Porte and postponing the withdrawal _sine die_. But the
Government, and this should be noted in view of French
misstatements,[983] did not gratify him.

Stuart arrived at Constantinople on the 19th July, 1802. On the 22nd, he
and Straton (Lord Elgin was then voyaging for the benefit of his health
and his collections) conferred with the Reis Effendi. The Porte refused
either to restore the Beys to their old position or to grant them Upper
Egypt, and held to the project of expulsion, but with double the
pensions originally promised. Its argument was unanswerable. The
question was not “whether the Beys should or should not be rich or
powerful subjects of the Sultan, but whether the Sultan or they should
be the sovereign of Egypt.[984] There was nothing to do; and Stuart had
to proceed to Egypt empty-handed save for “warm praise” and the “Order
of the Crescent.”[985]

The Turks were, in truth, in no mood for concessions. “The issue of the
affair had caused the deepest and most universal mortification among
them. They well knew that the claims of the Beys were claims for the
sovereignty of the country, and that under all former circumstances the
contest had ended fatally for the Porte. Even in the conferences with
Straton,[986] the Beys in demanding Upper Egypt refused at the same time
to give up their houses at Cairo, insinuating that they would soon have
occasion for them.”[987] There is a “traveller’s tale” that the Capitan
Pasha received Stuart in a room which he had taken care to “infect” with
the breath of two persons dying from the plague; he, as a good Turk,
never worrying about himself, though his dragoman, Prince Callimachi,
being in the secret, was “shivering with terror.”[988] The
disappointment at the failure of their first attempt confirmed them in
their obstinacy, and “pride as well as policy contributed an impulse to
their resolution.”[989] “They were only waiting for the evacuation of
Egypt ‘to act systematically against the Beys, being resolved on their
destruction.’”[990] “This was not only a resolution of the Divan but a
plan congenial to their ideas and suited to their mode of warfare.”[991]
They, moreover, “conceived that the evacuation was no longer at the
discretion of Britain,” and Stuart lamented that “he had no power to
refer to it as conditional upon settling the affairs of the Beys.”[992]
“It would have been a very effective argument.”[993] Elgin once, rather
naïvely, expressed his disappointment that the Reis Effendi “did not
even insinuate a wish for the continuance” of the British
occupation.[994] In the heat of discussion, the Turkish Minister once
“slipped” something about calling in the “aid” of Russia in the affairs
of Egypt, and quickly solicited Elgin to take no notice of it.[995]

One of the motives for this ardent desire to have the country evacuated
was to allow the First Consul—now that he had made his peace with the
Sultan—no pretext for bickering and interference. A man like Napoleon
could not fail to take umbrage at the continued infraction of the terms
of the Treaty of Amiens. Stuart, apparently, had no mind to evacuate. He
turned out the Turks from some forts, which Cavan had allowed them to
garrison, and put down “disorderliness” on their part with a ruthless
hand.[996] He professed to believe that the withdrawal of his army “had
become an event of great seeming uncertainty.”[997] The First Consul was
fully informed of this state of affairs. There was not yet a French
official representative in Cairo; but there was no lack of
correspondents. There was Royer, the chief “pharmacist” of the Army of
the East. He had been suspected of having poisoned some of the wounded
soldiers during the Syrian campaign and of trafficking in liquor. He,
apparently, thought it advisable to remain in Egypt after the expulsion
of the French.[998] Another correspondent was Mengin, the future
historian of Mehemet Ali.[999] Of the others mention might be made of
the monk Raphaïl and the “scribe” Mikhaïl Sabbagh,[1000] both of them
Syrians. The Beys, Ibrahim and Bardissy, had also been sending petitions
to the First Consul, abusing their British “protectors” and clamouring
for help.[1001] So much for the “loyalty and sincerity” of the “heroes”
of Hutchinson and Stuart! There were also some letters from Sheikh El
Messiry, the leading “savant” of Alexandria: they were simply
complimentary epistles.[1002]

These communications seemed to have led Napoleon to seek more
information on the real position of affairs. Hence the new mission of
Colonel Sebastiani to the Levant. The colonel was a Corsican “not
deficient in talents but a great coxcomb.”[1003] He possessed in an
ample degree the “swagger” deemed essential for service in the East, for
missions of _éclat_ and for heightening prestige.

Sebastiani was instructed[1004] to proceed first to Tripoli to obtain
the Pasha’s recognition of the flag of the new Italian Republic, and
then to Egypt. He must take note of everything of interest: the
“situation” in the harbour of Alexandria, the British and Turkish forces
there, the condition of the fortifications, the history of what had
taken place after the expulsion of the French, the condition of the
people, the views of leading men. In Cairo, Sebastiani was to do the
same thing. He must repeat to all and sundry “agreeable things on the
part of Napoleon,” for example, he could tell them “that he loved the
Egyptians, and desired their happiness, that he often talked about
them.” He was to inform Khosrev, the new Viceroy, of his intention to
send an official agent for commercial relations and to find out if the
First Consul could offer his good offices for settling the “differences”
with the Beys. Sebastiani also carried a letter from Talleyrand to
Khosrev, in which the object of the mission was declared to be the
finding out whether the country had been evacuated or not. This was held
to precede the arrival of an official agent.[1005]

Having accomplished successfully his mission in Tripoli[1006] Sebastiani
arrived in Alexandria on 10th September, 1802.[1007] On the same day he
called on Stuart and demanded the prompt execution of the Treaty. The
British general at first gave a “vague answer,” but, when pressed,
declared that he thought that neither Malta nor Egypt was to be
evacuated before a treaty of commerce between France and Britain had
been concluded. Sebastiani thought that Stuart was “excessively vain,
though of mediocre ability,” that he wanted to remain in Alexandria “to
have himself talked about,” that he, therefore, sent his Government
“alarming” reports to retard the evacuation. The Turks, on the other
hand, expressed anxiety at the apparently “interminable” occupation;
while the inhabitants, who were under the illusion that Sebastiani came
to take possession of their city, were “overjoyed” so long as the
illusion lasted. All the way to Cairo, he was met—if he can be
believed—by demonstrations of joy and attachment to France and the
“person” of Napoleon. But in the capital the Viceroy—though courteous
and lavish in flattering expressions—maintained towards him the attitude
he had invariably held towards Stuart, and rejected the proffered good
offices, remarking that Constantinople was the proper place for
them.[1008] Sebastiani, consequently, solaced himself with interviews
with various Sheikhs and leading Christians, and expatiated in his
report on their enthusiasm at seeing portraits of the First Consul. He
himself, however, admitted that “three or four” of the most eminent
savants abstained from meeting him in order not to “compromise”
themselves. One of his companions—and Sebastiani himself narrated it,
though in a diluted form—described how the party were obliged to hide
themselves, fearing a hostile demonstration on the part of the
inhabitants.[1009] Djabarti—usually very well-informed—merely notes the
arrival of a “French ambassador.”[1010] His relations with the Beys—it
should be remembered that he was not meant to establish any “connection”
with them—did not amount to more than assurances of the disposition of
Napoleon to mediate between them and the Porte to the advantage of both
parties.

This did not amount to much. But the mission[1011] and the publication
in the _Moniteur_—the official organ—of extracts from the Report were
destined to have important effects. The Porte was greatly alarmed,
although Napoleon tried to prevent this by omitting from the published
version most of the strongest assertions of Sebastiani. The new French
Ambassador, General Brune, in Constantinople, presented the Divan with a
mild translation of the document,[1012] but the enemies of France took
good care not to let the opportunity slip. Fonton, the French dragoman
of the Russian Legation—“cet homme qui a perdu sans ressource (_sic_:
retour?) tout sentiment français, dont le cœur ne s’ouvre plus qu’à la
haine et qui déteste d’autant plus son pays qu’il le trahit tous les
jours”[1013]—made an accurate translation of the report and had it
transmitted to the Sultan himself.[1014] The alarm led to a
rapprochement between the Turks and the British. “The Turkish Minister
spoke with great emotion, expressing the sincerity of the friendship of
the Porte towards Great Britain and its determination to meet every
event with firmness.”[1015]

The mission also revived the hopes of the Beys of French help. Bardissy
and Ibrahim made new appeals to the First Consul;[1016] but they did
not, however, abate anything of their professions to the British. They
even moved from Upper Egypt to the neighbourhood of Alexandria, with the
object of being close to Stuart. It seemed that they succeeded in
deceiving him, for he warmly denied that they had any relations with
France, calling this one of Sebastiani’s “inventions.”[1017]

Piqued by the conduct of the Frenchman and the continued silence of the
Government[1018] and hoping against hope that something might “turn up”
to prevent the evacuation, he took upon himself to postpone the carrying
out of the orders[1019] for withdrawal and resolved to do
something.[1020] “It was painful for him as a soldier and as a man to be
a tame spectator of carnage almost at his very gates.”[1021]

The result was the mission of Lord Blantyre[1022]—one of his aides-de-
camp—to Constantinople. That officer found Elgin on the eve of returning
to England and, therefore, most anxious to settle the long-protracted
quarrel. Reading in Stuart’s instructions “that the Beys would acquiesce
in any terms short of exile”—words which Stuart did not mean to be taken
too literally[1023]—he entered on the negotiations with a light heart.
The outcome of them was neither a treaty nor a convention, but
instructions to the Viceroy to grant the Beys the imperial pardon and
the rocky province of Aswan in the extreme south of the country.[1024]
The Reis Effendi affirmed to Brune that Elgin tried hard, but in vain,
to add a guarantee on the part of Britain.[1025] The Turks secured,
thus, the advantage of making the _rôle_ of the British the rather
modest one of “carrying the agreement to Alexandria.”[1026] They had the
impudence to ask Blantyre to sign a document in the name of Stuart to
the effect that the affair had been finally settled to his satisfaction,
and that there was nothing further to be expected or asked from the
Porte,[1027] and to vaunt the concession as an act of friendship to
Great Britain.[1028]

It was no great consolation to Stuart that the Porte had climbed down
from its old scheme of the wholesale expulsion of the Mamelukes. He
bitterly observed that “deception had been the instrument as well as the
object of the Turks in the whole of this unpleasant question and that it
was not difficult to discover that even the present concession . . . was
far less a testimony of their respect, however professed, to the wishes
of our government than of their alarm from the threatened interposition
of France.”[1029] He had, however, to evacuate the country in March,
1803.

Before he did so, he took some important steps. He took advantage of
Khosrev’s demand for a “liaison” officer—intended naturally to reside in
Cairo during the British occupation—and for the “loan” of an officer of
the engineers, to fix Major Missett as agent for the British Government
and Major Hayes to help in the repair of the fortifications.[1030] Their
presence was considered to prove advantageous “when neither Turks nor
Mamelukes should any longer be overawed by the presence of the British
Army and when they should lose their recollection of the wholesome
counsel of the British commander, given as it had been to them from the
most upright and disinterested views and supported by a calm,
conciliatory and dignified conduct.”[1031] Missett was instructed to
send in accounts of the councils of the Viceroy, to forward the views of
the British Government, to counteract the intrigues of the French and to
avoid interfering between the Turks and the Mamelukes and in internal
concerns except on orders from the Government.[1032] At the same time he
gave the Beys secret supplies of arms and ammunition,[1033] describing
it as a price for their retirement into Upper Egypt.[1034] In truth no
price was necessary, for with the withdrawal of the British there was no
point in the Mamelukes remaining in the barren territory near
Alexandria. He, further still, revealed his policy by taking with him
Elfi Bey. That Bey had been thinking for some time of “emigrating,[1035]
and his brethren were anxious to rid themselves of a masterful colleague
by sending him to London as their official representative.”[1036]
Stuart, however, refused to accept him in that character. The point is
of no great practical importance, since he admitted that the voyage of
Elfi “suggested an opening to a chain of influences which he thought
might be cultivated” and that “as the Bey’s consideration was high with
both Mamelukes and Arabs, he might be a forcible instrument in the hands
of government in the event of those occurrences against which (the
government) had expressed such desire to be prepared, and at the same
time counteract any project of the French to build a rival interest with
those parties in their disappointment at our departure.”[1037]

Thus ended the first British occupation of Egypt, and with it the
possibility of effective interference in her domestic concerns.

As for the Black Sea:

Spencer Smith had obtained the right in 1799, but it seemed to have been
allowed to lapse on account of the prohibition of imports from Russia.

Elgin despatch, 11 July, 1800:

Elgin, in his despatch of 11 July, 1800 (F.O. Turkey, 29), alludes to
conversations he had with Pitt and Grenville on the “inexpediency of
opening that trade.” He repeats this in despatch of 31 May, 1801 (F.O.
Turkey, 32). Before this last despatch reached London, Hawkesbury had,
on the representations of the Levant Company (Levant Company to
Hawkesbury, no date, F.O. Turkey, 31), instructed Elgin to obtain the
privilege (Hawkesbury to Elgin, 28 July, 1801. F.O. Turkey, 32). But
soon, however, he changed his orders. On the 10 May, 1802, he wrote that
after weighing the advantages and disadvantages it was thought better to
leave things as they were. But if France obtained this, Great Britain
should have it (F.O. Turkey, 36). The Porte itself extended the
privilege to Great Britain after giving it to France. (Note from the
Porte to Straton, 29 July, 1802, enclosed in Straton’s despatch of 3
Aug. F.O. Turkey, 37).]
