 XIV

 TILSIT TO BUCHAREST: THE STALEMATE IN THE EAST
 1807-1812


There is a legend of Tilsit. The meeting of Alexander and Napoleon on a
raft in mid-stream, the scenes of fraternisation designed by two masters
of stage effects, the rumour that a British Agent lay hidden behind a
curtain and overheard the conversations between the two autocrats[1502]
are bound to stir the dullest imagination. The belief that the partition
of the Turkish Empire had been resolved upon[1503] has taken root, and
the question as to which of the two emperors duped the other has never
yet been solved.[1504] Tilsit, on the contrary, created a situation in
which the powers balanced each other so equally as to enable the Turks
to emerge from the conflict without serious loss of territory. “Les
Projets de guerre dans l’Inde, les projets de partage de l’Empire
ottoman semblent des fantômes produits sur la scène pour occuper
l’attention de la Russie,” wrote Talleyrand in his memoirs.[1505] “Au
fond,” said Metternich, “Napoléon ne songeait ni à la Porte ni à
l’Asie.”[1506] Tilsit for him and for Alexander was an expedient.

There were some important considerations which weighed with the Tsar.
Austria had left the Coalition at the beginning of 1806, Prussia had
been overrun, British help had never come.[1507] He desired to save as
much of Prussia as he could. He found an opening for the annexation of
the Principalities in an understanding with France. Friedland had
convinced him that he could gain nothing by war. His “experts” argued
that the Ionian Islands and Cattaro did not lie in the line of “natural”
advance.[1508] There is a significant letter from him to his favourite
sister, the Grand Duchess Catherine, written on 17th June, 1807, “Dieu
nous a sauvé; au lieu de sacrifices, nous sortirons de la lutte avec une
sorte de lustre,” he wrote.[1509] To come to Tilsit in a gloomy mood of
despair and to return the ally of Napoleon, to make sure that France
would no longer raise trouble in Persia and the Balkans, and to keep in
his memory a mass of Napoleonic “promises” to be used or neglected as
should be necessary were no despicable gains.

For France, Tilsit meant the completion of the Continental system, the
annexation of the Ionian Islands and Cattaro, the recognition by Russia
of the vast changes Napoleon had introduced in the map of Europe and the
new thrones he had raised. The “promises” might keep Russia dependent
upon him. The dependence of Russia might enable him to browbeat Prussia
and keep Austria in hand. The West would lie at his feet.

It was on a report of the May Revolution in Constantinople and the
deposition of Sultan Selim, that Napoleon expressed himself to have been
released by the event from all obligations to Turkey. It was a “decree
of Providence,” he said.[1510] The decree was rather one of Napoleonic
expediency. The French Chancery had already examined the possibility of
considering the Turkish Empire as “le théâtre et le moyen d’une
négociation qui mettrait fin aux démêlés subsistants entre l’Empereur et
le Tsar et qui ouvrirait la carrière aux combinaisons les plus vastes et
les plus importantes.”[1511] The Prussian, Hardenberg, elaborated a
scheme of partition, the adoption of which would not only restore his
country but would also round off her frontiers.[1512] In the midst of
defeat the Prussians never lost sight of the Hohenzollern “rage
d’expansion.”[1513]

All these plans provided material for talk. The treaties were of a
totally different nature. They stipulated the conclusion of an armistice
between Russia and Turkey, the evacuation of Wallachia and Moldavia, the
non-entry of the Turks into them till after the ratification of a Russo-
Turkish definitive treaty of peace, the acceptance of French mediation
between the two belligerents. The eighth article of the Treaty of
Alliance laid down, however, that in the event of the Sultan refusing
the offer of mediation or of the negotiations for peace leading to no
“satisfactory result” after three months, France would make common cause
with Russia and the two “s’entendront pour soustraire tous les provinces
de l’Empire Ottoman en Europe, la ville de Constantinople et la Province
de Roumelie exceptées, au joug et aux vexations des Turcs.”[1514]

The Russians evacuated the Ionian Islands and Cattaro. A French officer,
Guilleminot, helped to bring about the conclusion of an armistice
between the Russians and the Turks. This was the armistice of Slobodzie,
which was signed on the 24th August, 1807.[1515] But the Tsar refused to
ratify it, basing his refusal on the “fact” that the Russian general had
no powers, that the terms were humiliating and that as soon as the
Russians began to withdraw the Turks followed on their heels and started
plundering and murdering the inhabitants.[1516]

Russia, thus, was violating the treaty. To justify his action, the Tsar
had recourse to “l’air de Tilsit.” Referring to his conversations with
Napoleon, Alexander stated that the French Emperor “m’a souvent dit
qu’il ne tenait point à cette évacuation, qu’on la traînerait en
longueur pour se dispenser et qu’il n’était pas possible de souffrir
plus longtemps les Turcs en Europe, il me laissait même entrevoir le
projet de les jeter en Asie. Ce n’est qu’ensuite qu’il est revenu à leur
laisser Constantinople, et quelques provinces environnantes.”[1517]
Russia was by no means trying to raise the question of partition. The
object in view was to keep Moldavia and Wallachia; if a secret treaty
excepted only Constantinople from the process of “liberation,” why could
not a treaty of peace extend the frontier of Russia to the Danube?[1518]
If the Ottoman Empire was to be soon “dead” and its heirs were to be
soon assembled, why should not one of them keep what he had in
hand?[1519]

The instructions and the behaviour of Count Tolstoï, the new Ambassador
in Paris, further elucidate the point. He was to insist on the
indefinite occupation of the two provinces, to present the proposals for
partition as proceeding from his “personal initiative” and to refrain
altogether from making them if he found the French Government
disinclined to listen.[1520] Tolstoï was not the man to err in this
respect. “Il est brave militaire,” so Napoleon describes him, “mais il
est rempli de préjugés et de méfiance contre la France et est bien loin
de la hauteur des évènements de Tilsit et de la nouvelle position où
l’étroite amitié qui règne entre votre Majesté et moi ont placé
l’univers.”[1521]

Napoleon, however, was not the man to let go his advantage for nothing.
If the Tsar refused to withdraw his troops from Turkish territory, he
would not evacuate Prussian Silesia.[1522] His Minister for Foreign
Affairs, Champagny, wrote to Caulaincourt, the French Ambassador in St.
Petersburg, “L’Empereur voit toujours avec peine qu’on s’attache plus à
des prétendus [_sic_] propos qu’à la lettre du traité, qu’il appelait en
donnant suite à la métaphore de M. de Roumiantsoff l’air noté et, par
conséquent, l’air véritable de Tilsit.”[1523]

But he was careful not to go far in this direction. He kept up all the
language of yore.[1524] He did not insist on mediating between Russia
and Turkey, though Mouhib Effendi, the Turkish Ambassador in Paris, had
been pressing to enter into negotiations with Tolstoï.[1525] The
complications in Spain, moreover, led him to instruct Caulaincourt to
discuss the partition of Turkey. The Tsar responded with equal
“sincerity.”[1526] Lack of response would have shown Napoleon that
Alexander was looking for some other system. It was too early yet not to
keep up appearances. The result was a series of conferences between
Caulaincourt and Roumiantsoff, known as the “entretiens of St.
Petersburg,” in which Turkey was partitioned on paper.[1527] Napoleon
did not disapprove, he merely stated that the affair was too important
to be settled by any but the two emperors.[1528] An interview was
necessary. In the meantime Russia could swallow Finland.

Turkey, the unfortunate object of ambition and abuse of power, was then
in an indescribable state of confusion. A revolution in May, 1807, cost
Selim his throne, a second in November his life and hurled down Mustafa
from power. Most of the prominent officials of the Porte had been
massacred. But the outbreaks had nothing to do with foreign
policy.[1529] They marked the beginning of the struggle between the Old
and the Young Turks. The former sought salvation in a return to the
past, the latter in innovations. “Le gouvernement,” wrote Sebastiani
after the first revolution, “sera une oligarchie ecclésiastique en
défiance de toutes les nations chrétiennes, de l’Angleterre et de la
Russie, comme de la France, à moins que les évènements n’entraînent
cette nation vieille et débile.”[1530] Their foreign Policy was not
changed. Their sound instinct convinced them that Napoleon and Alexander
could never agree on a scheme of partition. They accordingly determined
to keep to France. They threw themselves on the “benevolence” of
Napoleon and notified the intention to accede to any arrangement as to
peace.[1531] They broke off the negotiations with Paget. It was an
experiment on their part, for, it may be recalled, the Porte informed
him that it would apply within four months for a resumption of the
negotiations.

But as time went on it became clear that a change was necessary.
Sebastiani could not take upon himself to assure them of the real
sentiments of Napoleon. He himself was as much in the dark and believed
that his remaining at Constantinople after Tilsit savoured of treachery.
The signs were that they could obtain peace only at the price of a
cession of territory. Mouhib Effendi received no support from Champagny
in Paris. The French Minister instructed Sebastiani to inform the Porte
that the misfortunes of Turkey were due to internal evils.[1532] This
was true, but it could have brought no comfort. Then came the query if
they were prepared to cede their two provinces, and, if not, what means
had they to carry on the war.[1533] There were, moreover, threats of
punishing the Pasha of Janina; and a disquieting association of ideas
reminded the Porte that Bonaparte invaded Egypt in 1798 with the object
of punishing the Mamelukes.[1534] The experiment of working with France
had led to no result. The Porte, accordingly, took two bold steps. It
consented to negotiate with Russia directly and without French
intervention,[1535] and it made overtures to Great Britain.[1536]

The occasion, for Canning, was not merely one for making peace with the
Turks. His object was to forge a weapon against France. He had already,
on the first news of Tilsit, endeavoured to dissociate Alexander from
Napoleon by the two missions of Sir Robert Wilson to St. Petersburg and
by the attack on Copenhagen.[1537] Sir Robert, “a rather sanguine,
credulous man,”[1538] brought back from his first trip a favourable
report, and Canning, “making due allowance”[1539] for the character of
the agent, sent him back again.[1540] “Nothing important, however, was
to be left to his sole discretion. If Russia were sincere, it would be
worth while to keep her up to it by showing a disposition to cultivate
and strengthen the former good understanding; if she were not sincere .
. . there would be no harm done.”[1541] On his arrival in the Russian
capital, Wilson told Roumiantsoff that Britain was desirous of seeing
the power of Russia augmented, whereas France was as anxious to see it
reduced. He proceeded to state that his country would never propose the
partition of the Turkish Empire, but would consent to the annexation of
the Danubian principalities as a result of negotiations between Russia
and Turkey—negotiations which might lead to the former “Union” between
them and would “protect the interests” of the Tsar “on the side of
Greece,” which he had to abandon by his present system.[1542] But the
Russian Government could hardly break with France on “mere verbal
affirmations” which were not officially confirmed.[1543] Wilson,
moreover, seemed to have behaved with much “swagger” and kept company
with all those who were disaffected against the French alliance.[1544]
He was told to quit, and war was soon declared on Britain.

But Canning did not lose hope. In his instructions to Sir Robert Adair,
who was to negotiate with the Porte, he states that if Russia,
“resenting the delusion” which, probably, had been practised upon her or
“taking a more enlarged view” of the dangers of French predominance,
should desire peace with Turkey, Britain would be willing to help and
would accede to a Russo-Turkish treaty stipulating the determination of
both parties to resist French designs. The continuance of the war
between Russia and Britain would make no difference with regard to
this.[1545]

There was also another project, namely, an alliance between Austria,
Britain and Turkey.[1546] The choice of Sir Robert Adair, a _persona
grata_ in Vienna and in confidential communication with Austrian
circles,[1547] pointed to this. The appointment does credit to Canning,
for Adair belonged to the Opposition and had been the subject of one of
the Minister’s witticisms.[1548] The Minister made it clear that,
whatever was to be done, there was to be neither a guarantee of
integrity nor a subsidy. “Money,” he wrote, “we have not and a
guarantee, though cheap enough, is hardly honest.”[1549]

If there was to be no guarantee was there to be no help? If it came to
the worst, Britain would help the “removal” of the seat of government
and would recognise the Porte wherever it might be found. She was
disposed, in the meantime, to “utilize those local Turkish advantages”
of which the Porte was unable to make use. “In this description,” wrote
Canning, “I do not mean to point at Egypt, the occupation of which would
probably be a ground of incurable jealousy and alarm to the Turkish
government and the continued possession of which is known by experience
to require a larger army than His Majesty could appropriate for that
object.” The difficulty applied to any other “station”—the term is
significant—that would require a large garrison. An island would serve
the purpose better, Crete, for example. But it was to be made clear that
the offer of help was to be contingent upon the demand of the Turks and
upon their co-operation.[1550]

Egypt, thus, did not find place among the “local advantages.” The
position of Ali Pasha of Janina was totally different from that of
Mehemet Ali. The geographical position of his territory, the fact that
co-operation with him need be no more than naval, his avowed enmity to
the masters of the Ionian Islands—be they French or Russian—endowed Ali
with a degree of prominence which the extent of his power and resources
would not otherwise have justified. Ali had sent an envoy to London and
had made his desire to annex the ex-Venetian possessions on the mainland
abundantly clear.[1551] J. P. Morier advised the granting of Egypt to
Ali on the grounds that there were already Albanian soldiers in that
province.[1552] Leake, who had been for years with Ali, recommended that
his ambition should be gratified.[1553] The Government, however, took a
different line. The Pasha was by no means to be encouraged to expect any
aid in the realization of his schemes of independence—supposing that he
had any. Should the Porte succumb to France or abandon resistance to
that power, Ali would then receive naval “co-operation” in his defence
against France and the establishing of his independence. If the
immediate attainment of any of the “objects” desired by him would enable
him to resist France, the British fleet would help him to attain that
object.[1554] A “quantity” of artillery and munitions of war were also
to be given to him.[1555] This is not exactly the “convention” of which
M. Driault speaks as having been concluded between Ali and the British
Government.[1556]

Sir Robert Adair arrived off Tenedos in September, 1808. After three
months of arduous negotiations with Vehid Effendi—the same Turk who
proved so “disagreeable” to Talleyrand at Warsaw—the Peace of the
Dardanelles was concluded.[1557]

The Peace is in many ways a remarkable one. It was the first breach in
the wall against Britain.[1558] It was the first great act of a reign
destined to occupy a prominent place in Turkish annals. Six months
before, Sebastiani described Mahmoud as “un prince faible, doux et
valétudinaire, atteint d’une épilepsie incurable.”[1559] The peace was a
great act of wisdom and courage. The French “chargé d’affaires” had used
violent threats. Peace with Britain, he said, meant, _ipso facto_, war
with France.[1560] To withstand the threats of France and to take three
months to make peace with her mortal enemy would hardly justify Dr. Rose
in attributing the delay to “Oriental Pride and Lethargy.”[1561] The
Treaty is also notable for the article stipulating reciprocity for the
privileges enjoyed by British subjects in Turkish dominions.[1562]

Thenceforward the Porte determined to steer clear of all foreign
connections and to await the outcome of events.[1563] It is doubtful if
any but Turks could carry out successfully a policy of that nature. It
was the wisest under the circumstances and it served the purpose well.
The price they paid was by no means excessive: violent accusations by
disappointed diplomatists and abusive epithets by unsympathetic
historians.

Napoleon had, in the meantime, become hopelessly entangled in Spanish
complications; and Austria had been showing unmistakable signs of
restiveness. He tried hard to avert the war by dangling before the eyes
of Metternich a share in the spoils of Turkey or threatening to make her
the helpless spectator of the partition.[1564] It happened that the
Turkish Ambassador was present during the conversation and the
scandalised Metternich commented, “Cette conversation est sans doute
sans exemple dans les fastes de la diplomatie.”[1565] There was nothing
for it but to proceed to Erfurt and pay the Tsar’s price. Talleyrand was
sent beforehand “to speak to the Tsar the language that suited him.” To
tell him of “l’intérêt bien entendu du continent, sept millions de Grecs
rendus à la liberté, etc.” “Voilà,” continued Napoleon, “un beau champ
pour faire de la philanthropie, je vous donne sur cela carte blanche, je
veux seulement que ce soit de la philanthropie lointaine.”[1566] But
Talleyrand had formed his own programme and went to Erfurt to execute
it. It was not certain that to thwart the Emperor’s ambition meant
treachery to France. “Talleyrand,” as M. Sorel neatly put it, “ferait la
France européenne et lui ministre européen de la France en Europe, de
l’Europe en France.”[1567] Even if he was to use the language of
seduction, Alexander would not have listened. At Tilsit, it was to his
interest to listen and to seem duped. Rightly or wrongly he did not
judge the moment opportune to break with Napoleon.[1568] But he, all the
same, exacted the price for joining forces against Austria. Napoleon
recognised the annexation of Moldavia and Wallachia up to the Danube
with the sole proviso that this recognition should be kept secret.[1569]
The situation was, apparently, changed beyond recognition. The Tsar now
expatiated on the necessity of the continued existence of the Turkish
Empire for the preservation of the peace of Europe.[1570]

The war between Austria and France was to prove the extent of his
sincerity. His contribution to the common effort was beautiful phrases
and no more, “des guirlandes de lauriers et de Cyprès mêlés, mais de
Cosaques point,” says M. Sorel.[1571] There could be no mistake about
it: Alexander had become an ally only in name. Napoleon saw it but found
it necessary to simulate. “Il lui importe,” wrote Champagny to
Caulaincourt, “que cette erreur dont il est désabusé soit partagée par
toute l’Europe.”[1572] He kept up the appearances of alliance. He
refused to answer a letter from Mahmoud announcing his accession to the
throne.[1573] He openly avowed in a speech to the “Corps Législatif” his
satisfaction at the expansion of Russia to the Danube.[1574] But he
worked beneath the surface in the other direction. He seemed even to
have welcomed the Peace of the Dardanelles as strengthening the Turks,
for he disapproved of the threats used by his chargé d’affaires in
Constantinople, instructed him to remain at his post till the British
should succeed in having him dismissed and to bear always in mind that
France did not desire a war with Turkey.[1575] Latour-Maubourg was
informed that the continuance of the war in the Balkans was to the
advantage of France, that she would resist the expansion of Russia
beyond the Danube by all her forces and would be disposed to guarantee
the integrity of Turkey apart from Moldavia and Wallachia.[1576]
Napoleon, moreover, scrupulously avoided all language that might savour
of hidden designs on the territories of the Sultan or of the expectation
of the fall of his empire through decadence.[1577] His most effective
weapon, however, was his exploitation of Austrian jealousy of Russian
aggrandisement in the Balkan peninsula. To discover her views, he
invited her to join in a scheme of partition, and when she turned a deaf
ear he instigated her to do all she could against his ally.[1578]

The Tsar also, after all that had happened, was maintaining a simulation
of alliance. He demanded from the Porte the expulsion of Sir Robert
Adair, the newly-installed British Ambassador, and, on this being
rejected, he broke off the negotiations that were being carried on at
Jassy.[1579] But he secretly tried to obtain Austrian pressure on the
Porte by inviting Vienna to have an “equivalent” in Serbia and
Bosnia.[1580] Austria felt the strength of her position between
Alexander and Napoleon and rejected the overture.

It was clear, therefore, that a stalemate had arisen and that it would
only cease with the outbreak of war between Russia and France. Till that
event had taken place, diplomacy—however active or ingenious were the
diplomatists—was impotent. The activity of Sir Robert Adair and his
successor, Stratford Canning, should not, thus, deceive us as to their
influence on the course of events. The biographer of the _Great Elchi_
quotes with approval a statement that Stratford took upon himself the
task of “bearding Napoleon in his dream of universal empire . . .
without a single person’s advice and on his own responsibility.” It must
have been “downright madness,” “yet it was precisely what Canning
did.”[1581] Language like this seems rather excessive when we remember
that for some two years the Foreign Office did not send instructions
worthy of the name to Constantinople. Dr. Rose and Mr. A. Cecil seem to
be at a loss to explain this serious omission.[1582] Lord Wellesley was
Foreign Secretary and was not the man to neglect his duties. Canning was
inclined to put it down to his youth and insignificance.[1583] But this
is hardly more tenable, since the Government could have appointed some
one else in his place. The truth was that the British Government
postponed all diplomatic interference till it could be sure of the real
attitude of the Tsar. In the spring of 1811, the signs were favourable.
Mr. Robert Liston was appointed to the Constantinople Embassy and was
instructed to hold himself in readiness to depart.[1584] His
departure—the Tsar had now veered back temporarily to the alliance—was
put off till September. In that month he actually embarked his luggage,
but the orders were countermanded and the frigate was diverted to
another service.[1585] “The apprehension of embroiling Britain with
Russia or Turkey,” this was written in November, 1811, “still prevents
Wellesley from sending out Liston, to whom appeals would doubtless be
made by both, very difficult to be avoided or decided upon.”[1586] In
February, 1812, Liston gave up all hope of the embassy.[1587] It was
only in April, 1812, that he sailed. He arrived in Constantinople to
find that peace had been concluded between Russia and Turkey.[1588]

There is thus no mystery about the causes of the apparent negligence of
the embassy in Constantinople. It is difficult, in face of this, to
imagine that young Canning’s influence would carry things very far. All
that he did was to carry forward Adair’s correspondence with various
persons in Vienna and St. Petersburg.[1589] It does not seem that his
correspondents were in the secrets of their governments.

When war broke out between France and Austria, Adair was not enlightened
as to the way Russia was helping her ally and tried to “injure” the
cause of Russia by “preventing” peace between her and Turkey and even
thought of a combined Turco-British attack on “Sebastopolis.”[1590] When
Napoleon married Marie Louise, Adair turned against Austria. His
informants gave him the impression that there was a scheme on foot by
which Austria would receive the Principalities, the Porte the Crimea,
and Poland would be restored.[1591] The diplomat, accordingly,
endeavoured to “promote” peace between the Tsar and the Sultan. He sent
to his Russian friends copies of the Franco-Persian Treaty of 1807 and a
letter from Napoleon to the Shah,[1592] to convince them of the
treachery of France! This was rather dead history. The problem for
Adair’s and Canning’s diplomacy was how to bring about peace between
Russia and Turkey when neither of them was willing to give up her
pretensions. What effective arguments could they use to persuade the
Turks to cede their provinces when they were convinced that sooner or
later a war with France would force the Russians to evacuate?[1593] What
compensation could they give the Russians for renouncing their
pretensions? They had no authority to offer a British alliance. The Tsar
was sure to have it when it suited him to break with France. At one
time, Sir Robert Adair sought a solution by offering Russia a colony in
the West Indies![1594] Diplomacy was impotent, whatever biographers
might say.

Latour-Maubourg had no greater influence on the march of events. His
hands were tied by his instructions to make the most cordial professions
to the Porte but to remember always that the Tsar was still the ally of
the Emperor.[1595] He was instructed to press the Porte to send an
ambassador to Paris furnished with full powers to conclude an offensive
and defensive alliance, but to make clear that the necessity for such an
alliance might never arise.[1596] The language of the French “chargé”
was accordingly vague. The Turks perfectly understood him, but saw his
embarrassment and mercilessly demanded an explicit statement in
writing.[1597] Why should they help him out of his difficulties? But
they were not foolish enough to cut a line of possible retreat. They
assured Latour-Maubourg that they were ready to do anything, only there
was the difficulty arising out of the fact that Mahmoud’s notification
had been left without answer.[1598] When the “difficulty” was removed by
the falsehood that the answer had been sent and lost,[1599] the French
position was not improved by the nature of the overture they made. It
was to be nothing less than a mass rising of the whole Moslem world
under its personal leader—the Sultan—and against the Muscovites.[1600]
Talk like this could lead to nothing. Latour-Maubourg was blamed for
what was not his fault.[1601] “Ma diplomatie,” wrote Napoleon, “eût dû
faire pour moi la moitié de la campagne et à peine y-a-t-elle
songé.”[1602] The truth was that he gathered the fruits of a system in
which he tried to combine the opposites, “managing” Russia till the
right moment and searching for allies against her.

All through the year 1811 the Tsar had been pressed to make peace with
the Sultan.[1603] But he could not bring himself to give up all hope of
the Principalities. He tried to the last moment to wear down Turkish
resistance. But neither military disasters nor the machinations of a
strong peace-party supported by the Janissaries could weaken the grim
resolution of Mahmoud. At last, when the evil hour came, the Tsar had to
renounce the fruits of six years of efforts and sacrifice. But he
cherished the illusion of obtaining the alliance of the Porte against
France and a combined effort against Dalmatia! The project had to be
dropped.[1604]

The Treaty of Bucharest marks the end of a phase in the diplomatic
history of the Near East. The French invasion of Egypt in 1798 had
thrown the Turks into the turmoils of the wars of the Empire. The Greek
War of Independence opened another phase: the struggle of the Near
Eastern nationalities for emancipation. Never was Turkey so weak, nor
its fall so imminent as in the years 1798 to 1812. Well might the Porte
have adopted as its motto the saying of l’abbé Sieyès when asked what he
had done during the French Revolution: “I have survived.”

 XV

 MEHEMET ALI: THE YEARS OF PREPARATION
 1807-1812


In the years between Tilsit and the invasion of Russia no change took
place in the situation in the East. Napoleon was without a navy worthy
of the name and Great Britain was not disposed to renew the venture of
1807. Egypt was too far afield: Spain provided an excellent field where
the Mistress of the Seas could come into grips with the Master of the
Continent. It was not till the latter half of 1811 that Major Missett
came back to Cairo as British Consul, probably as a result of the
representations of Stratford Canning, who was the first to direct the
attention of the British Government to the great qualities of Mehemet
Ali and to draw a parallel between him and his fellow Albanian, Ali of
Janina.[1605]

Mehemet Ali was engaged in those years in crushing possible domestic
rivals. The “ulemas” of Cairo had endeavoured to use against him the
weapons employed by them against his predecessor—Khourshid. But the
results were different. He succeeded in dividing them into warring
cliques, deprived the refractory of lucrative posts and “exiled” the
maker of his own fortunes—Omar Makram—out of the Capital. “Les cheikhs
et ulemas,” wrote a French resident, “firent entendre leurs plaintes
auxquelles le Pasha ne prêta qu’une oreille indifférente. La plupart lui
sont vendue et il a éloigné ceux qui ne favorisent pas ses
objets.”[1606]

The Mamelukes presented a more serious problem. All efforts at a pacific
accommodation between them and himself proved of no avail. “Les
stipulations,” wrote the French consul in 1808, “exigent une confiance
difficile à établir et les Mamelukes ne peuvent se résoudre à payer le
tribut qu’on leur demande. Malgré leur faiblesse et leur état de
décadence, pour ainsi dire, journalière, ils ne peuvent se faire l’idée
de renoncer à la possession de l’Egypte en favour des Osmanlis.”[1607]
They could not dispossess the Pasha and he could obtain no decisive
victory over them. The result was treachery and the massacre of the
major part of them in March, 1811.[1608]

The immediate object of his other activities in these early years was
the hoarding of treasure. It had played an important part in the history
of Near Eastern adventures—be they Jewish or Turkish. The hoard was a
motley collection of precious stones, ornamental daggers and swords,
costly stuffs, snuff-boxes, pipes and specie. Over it lurked a Jew or an
Armenian, crafty, cruel, treacherous and devoured by the mania of
hoarding. It was he who suggested the distant expeditions and pointed
out the unhappy individuals to be fleeced and the means of filling the
coffers. Astrologers, divines and prophets seconded his operations.

For the Pasha of Egypt it was an imperious necessity. He had to feed the
cupidity of the Porte. He had to bribe the Mamelukes into submission in
the years preceding their destruction. But it was his Albanians who
devoured his treasure. He was at their mercy but was unable to do
without them. In 1809 they numbered 10,000, but received the pay of
30,000.[1609] Three years later, they increased to 15,000 and the
troubles of the Pasha rose in proportion.[1610] In 1810, the French
consul described his position as the most critical since his coming to
power.[1611] The soldiers were the only persons in the country who had
money, all the rest had been fleeced.[1612] It was, accordingly, with
undisguised relief that the Viceroy heard of the defeat of his own
soldiers in Arabia.[1613]

Money was to be obtained by all means. When Pahlin, the Swedish Chargé
at Constantinople, cited to Mehemet Ali a passage from Diodorus on the
supposed existence of some buried treasure, the Pasha started digging
with zest.[1614] The taxes were collected with exactness, the slipshod
methods of the Beys were a story of the past. To give one example, the
customs at Bulak—the river port of Cairo—which brought in under the Beys
no more than thirty purses, amounted in 1812 to 1500, and this without
hardship or injustice to anyone.[1615] But the processes of legality did
not answer the necessities of the times. It was in 1808 that Ali began
to lay violent hands on property. All titles were to be produced, most
of them were quashed and the rest had to be registered at a fee. Time-
honoured exemptions from taxation were stopped.[1616] The result was the
destruction of the class of multezims and the passing of the land into
the hands of the Viceroy, who distributed it among members of his
family, officers, favourites and the peasants.[1617] “Allez trouver un
autre que moi,” the peasant would say to the multezim when requested for
his labour, “je suis occupé à mes affaires. Vous n’avez plus rien à
faire dans ce pays, car vos jours sont passés et nous sommes devenus les
paysans du Pasha.”[1618] These years present Mehemet Ali at his worst,
destructive, avaricious and rigorous.[1619] But they were not unredeemed
by prognostics of the coming statesmanship.[1620]

One of his great sources of revenue then was the sale of wheat to the
British. There was a great dearth in the years 1809, 1810 and 1811 in
all Mediterranean countries except Egypt. The Porte laid an embargo on
the export, but the Pasha did not heed it. “He refuses,” wrote Missett
in 1811, “to obey any orders from his highness that may stand in
opposition to his interests.”[1621] The sale brought in what must have
been an amazing quantity of bullion, specie, and manufactured articles.
It was an operation that must have relieved the difficult situation of
British industries. The Pasha used to buy from the peasants at a low
price, employ false measures and force them to carry the wheat to his
depots. He used to sell it to the British agents at a price ranging from
ninety to a hundred piastres per ardeb, whereas it was selling in the
markets at twenty.[1622]

It was not merely the excessive profits which caused the transaction.
There were political considerations. “Le Bacha,” wrote Drovetti, “a cru
qu’en accordant aux Anglais ce qu’ils demandent ils cessent d’entretenir
des relations avec les Beys et qu’il n’aura plus rien à craindre
d’eux.”[1623] When Drovetti protested, he frankly answered that if the
British could not obtain wheat by sale, they might take it by
force.[1624] The events of 1807 seemed to have impressed his mind
deeply. He could not but have been struck by the extreme decadence of
Turkish power and was resolved to secure Egypt for himself. When, in
1809, the Porte made its peace with Great Britain, Mehemet Ali assured
Drovetti that even if the Sultan should declare war on France, he would
remain neutral.[1625] The most important article of the “treaty” which
he signed in 1810 with an agent of the East India Company stipulates
that in the event of a war between Britain and Turkey, the Pasha would
not molest British subjects or touch their property, but would, on the
contrary, afford them every protection and assistance.[1626]

But he thought of something more than extricating himself from possible
embarrassments resulting from the Porte’s situation with regard to
Europe. He sought some security against the Porte itself. The
precariousness of the position of all “rebellious” pashas has never been
sufficiently allowed for.[1627] The Porte could shut its eyes but it
never forgot nor forgave. The revolt of Latif Pasha in 1813, after
Mehemet Ali had restored the Holy Cities to the Sultan, was instigated
from Constantinople.[1628] The Porte any day might set a neighbouring
Pasha against him, it might have him poisoned or strangled. It was
permanent, he was transient. It was an institution, he was an
individual. His Albanians were more of an obstruction than a help. He
had not yet thought of the latent soldierly qualities of the Egyptian
peasant. There was no security for him but in a connection with some
foreign power. His sentiments did not come into the matter. “Il n’aime,”
wrote a French agent in 1811, “ni les Français ni les Anglais.”[1629] In
March, 1810, he asked Drovetti if Napoleon would assist him in
establishing his position on the same footing as that of the Barbary
Regencies. Drovetti could not listen to proposals of this nature.[1630]
It was with some repugnance that he had to turn to Missett, for “some
circumstances” had made him suspect “that the British Government did not
want him to rule in Egypt.”[1631] But he made his overtures all the
same. The answer of the Government was clear. “As there is no prospect
of a war with the Porte,” wrote Bathurst to Missett, “it would not be
consistent with sound policy and good faith to enter into a negotiation
with the Viceroy of Egypt on the subject of his meditated attempt to
establish his independence.” Missett, however, was to give him amicable
assurances of the disposition of the Prince Regent to maintain friendly
intercourse with him, even if hostilities should ever break out between
Britain and Turkey.[1632]

Foiled in his attempts, Mehemet Ali continued the precarious practice of
buying “friends” in the Divan, and obeying the Sultan’s behests. Hence
the campaign against the Wahabis.[1633]

Thus conclude the years of a process of transition which began with the
French Invasion in 1798. Mehemet Ali had established his power in Egypt
and was henceforth free to turn the country into a vast personal estate.
But in the exploitation—ill or well—of the estate, the inhabitants were
transformed. They became homogeneous in serving one master, they were
drilled into soldiers and sailors and marched to victory against the
Sultan, they were forced into schools and the production of wealth, they
were governed and taught to expect and find security.[1634] Mehemet Ali
made modern Egypt.

The years, moreover, marked the end of the isolation of the Egypt of the
Mamelukes. The country was opened to European influences and enterprise.
Its ultimate fate and its relations to the suzerain-state became
diplomatic problems of the first magnitude, problems that divided or
united the Powers and even once brought them to the verge of war.

 APPENDIX I

 REVISIONS AND NOTES


1. _J. P. Morier and the “Ruse de Guerre.”_

(“Dictionary of National Biography.” Article, J. P. Morier.)

In his note on J. P. Morier, Mr. S. Lane-Poole writes:

  “According to the ‘Nouvelle Biographie,’ Morier was taken prisoner by
  the French, but in spite of his character as the representative of a
  hostile power, entrusted, moreover, with a secret mission to co-
  operate diplomatically with the Turks with a view to the expulsion of
  the French from Egypt, he was set at liberty with a warning that
  should he again be found in Egypt he would meet with the fate of a
  spy. _No authority, however, is adduced for this story, which is
  unsupported by any public or private evidence._” This assertion is
  totally false. The “story” is supported by public and private
  evidence.

  M. Charles-Roux in his recent study of the “Convention of El Arish”
  (“Revue des Etudes Napoléoniennes for 1923,” pp. 319-20, 340) notes a
  “ruse de guerre,” by which the French evacuating Egypt on the faith of
  a solemn convention would be “destroyed” at sea. But he does not
  critically examine this curious episode, probably on the assumption
  that any “horror” might be conceived by a Turk.

  It was on the 30th October, 1799, that the Reis Effendi, in a
  conference with Mr. Spencer Smith, alluded to the overtures made by
  Kléber for the evacuation of Egypt, and, anticipating the objection
  that the return of so large an army as that under Kléber to Europe
  might turn the balance against the allies, stated that “means would be
  found of taking them to some place where half of them are to be
  massacred and the rest despoiled of everything and are even to be left
  with nothing but their shirts.” (S. Smith to Grenville, 12 Nov., 1799,
  F.O., Turkey, 23.) The “ruse” was enthusiastically taken up by Tamara,
  the Russian envoy. (Tamara to S. Smith, undated, F.O., Turkey, 23.)
  When Lord Elgin arrived in November, 1799, he at first objected.
  (Elgin to Grenville, undated, F.O., Turkey, 24), but he was soon
  “converted” to it and sent his secretary, J. P. Morier, to Turkish
  headquarters to arrange the execution. The written instructions to
  Morier, dated 22nd December, 1799, naturally do not refer to the
  “ruse” (F.O., Turkey, 24). But the object of his mission is clearly
  attested in a letter from Elgin to Lord Keith: “The Turks had
  conceived the utility to the common cause of destroying the French
  army and the benefit to ourselves of taking every degree of vengeance
  for so unprovoked an aggression and of making an example of such
  unprecedented atrocity of conduct: and if little less precipitancy had
  been used in concluding the capitulation (i.e. Sidney Smith’s
  successful conclusion of the Convention in January) _these ideas,
  which were sufficiently important for me to induce me to send my
  private secretary with them to Sir Sidney Smith_, would have been
  carried out” (enclosed in Elgin’s despatch of 10th March, 1800, F.O.,
  Turkey, 28).

  Morier could not convince Sidney Smith to adopt the measure. He
  entered Egypt with the Turks after the successful conclusion of the
  Convention. But when the rupture occurred and the Grand Vizier was
  defeated near Cairo, Morier had to make the best of his way out of the
  country. He arrived at Damietta on the 23rd March, 1800 and sailed in
  a small boat to join Sir Sidney Smith, but a storm forced him to take
  refuge in the Lake of Bourollos. He was surrounded by the French and
  could not save his incriminating papers, as there were only three feet
  of water, so he buried them in the ballast sand. They were, however,
  taken by the French. He, himself, was taken to Rosetta and, because he
  had a “safe pass” from a French officer, he was allowed to join Sir
  Sidney Smith in the “Tigre.” (This account is from Morier to Elgin,
  5th April, 1800, F.O., Turkey, 28.)

  The papers of Morier were published in the “Courier d’Egypte” (Nos. 70
  and 73, 21 prairial an VIII and 18 messidor VIII). There is no doubt
  as to their authenticity. Neither Morier nor Elgin said a word. Elgin
  satisfied himself by stating that there was no “harm” in the loss of
  the papers, “although the opinions expressed in them differ from those
  of Sidney Smith and the persons he employs” (Elgin to Grenville, 9th
  July, 1800, F.O., Turkey, 28).

  The point remains: were the Turks serious in this “ruse de guerre”?
  They were not. Their only interest was in the evacuation of Egypt and
  whether the French were destroyed or not “en route” was of no
  importance to them. As a matter of fact, Elgin states that after it
  had been suggested the Porte dropped all mention of it in its
  communications with the British embassy. The Grand Vizier who was to
  arrange the execution “notified the Porte of his determination not to
  engage in so scandalous a transaction.” (Elgin to Grenville, 9th May,
  1800, F.O., Turkey, 28.) But the Russians, on the contrary, were keen.
  Orders were sent from St. Petersburg to the fleet under Admiral
  Ushakoff to carry out the “ruse” “if appearances could be saved.”
  Italinsky, then Russian Minister in Palermo, informed Nelson of
  Ushakoff’s readiness, and Tamara repeatedly urged it upon the Porte.
  (Elgin to Grenville, 28th January, 1800, 9th May, 1800, F.O., Turkey,
  28; Italinsky to Nelson, 7th March, 1800, B.M. Add. MSS. 34916, f.
  311.)


2. _C. Arbuthnot and the venture on the Dardanelles._

(“Dictionary of National Biography.” Art., C. Arbuthnot.)

  W. P. Courtney states Arbuthnot was instructed to “demand from the
  Porte the dismissal (among other things) of the French ambassador,”
  the rejection of which led to the forcing of the Dardanelles—“it was
  mainly owing to his firmness that whatever success attended the
  operations was achieved.”

  It has been made clear in Chapter XIII of this essay that the demand
  for the dismissal of Sebastiani was not intended to be insisted on,
  that whatever qualities were displayed by Arbuthnot, firmness was not
  among them, and that, considering the object of the fleet to be the
  elimination of French influence, no success attended its operations.


3. _General Sherbrooke in Egypt in 1807._

(“Dictionary of National Biography.” Art., Sherbrooke.)

  The note states that General Sherbrooke was sent to Egypt to enter
  into arrangements with the Mamelukes.

  In point of fact, Sherbrooke was on the staff of General Fraser, and
  it was he who signed with the Pasha of Egypt the convention for the
  evacuation of Alexandria by the British. (Fraser to Castlereagh, 26th
  September, 1807, W.O. 1, vol. 348.)


4. _Great Britain and the Convention of Bartenstein._

  In the year 1807, three diplomatic instruments were signed. They have
  caused unnecessary confusion. On the 28th January, 1807, General
  Hutchinson signed a Treaty of Peace with Prussia; on the 26th April,
  1807, Alexander and Frederick William signed the Convention of
  Bartenstein, to which Great Britain and Austria were invited to
  accede—an invitation which both declined; on the 27th June, 1807,
  Canning and Jacobi-Kloest signed a treaty of subsidy, before the
  exchange of ratifications of which, Tilsit changed the whole
  situation, and so it was not ratified. Apart from rectifying
  misstatements which have found their way into authoritative works, the
  question is not without importance. Canning would not subscribe to the
  vague commitments of Bartenstein, which foreshadowed the settlement of
  1814, but would conclude, on the other hand, treaties of subsidy for
  the prosecution of the war.

  Now Colonel Lloyd (“Cambridge Modern History,” vol. IX, p. 289) states
  that on 27th June, Great Britain acceded to the Convention of
  Bartenstein, undertaking to pay a subsidy of £100,000 to Prussia in
  the course of the year. He thus confuses accession to the Convention
  with the treaty of subsidy. Dr. Rose (“Cambridge Modern History,” vol.
  IX, p. 296) states that Britain “became a party” to the Convention. In
  the “Cambridge History of British Foreign Policy” (vol. I, p. 356) he
  writes that “she soon acceded to it.” Garden (“Histoire des Traités de
  paix,” vol. X, p. 200) writes that she “s’empressa d’accéder à la
  Convention.”

  On 29th June, 1807, Jacobi-Kloest, the Prussian Minister in London,
  officially communicated the Convention to Canning and announced that
  he had full powers to sign an act of accession with a British
  plenipotentiary (F.O., Prussia, 77). On the same day, D’Alopeus did
  the same thing and declared that “Son Excellence M. Canning, ayant
  déjà _fait connaître au soussigné les dispositions de son auguste
  souverain de concourir à l’accomplissement de l’important objet que
  les hautes parties contractantes ont en vue_.” (F.O., Russia, 73.)
  Matters did not go beyond that stage. The Treaty of Tilsit was
  announced officially on the 1st August (D’Alopeus to Canning, 20th
  July-1st August, 1807, F.O., Russia, 73). In his answer to this
  communication, Canning expatiated on the dispositions of George III
  and adduced as a proof “_the willingness expressed by His Majesty to
  accede to the Convention of Bartenstein_.” (Canning to D’Alopeus, 5th
  August, 1807, F.O., Russia, 73.) It was now too late.


5. _Sir W. Sidney Smith and the Convention of El Arish._

  In the excellent articles referred to above, M. Charles-Roux considers
  that Sidney Smith, in working for the evacuation of Egypt, was
  actuated by the desire to see them quit that country and by the
  conviction that the Turks could never succeed in driving them out.
  These, doubtless, were important motives. But there was another one
  not less important, and not, by any means, disguised, namely, turning
  the Army of the East into an anti-revolutionary force.

  His view of the Revolution was moderate and his methods of fighting
  were at one with those of Windham, namely, co-operation with those
  opposed to extremism. “My experience,” he wrote to Windham on the 13th
  August, 1794, “has proved to me that this (a rising against the
  ‘Convention’) never can be expected if the ‘white flag’ is shewn to
  them as an earnest of the return of the antient [_sic_] system in its
  full extent. . . . There can be no doubt that there exists a party in
  France and even in Paris itself of the moderate kind impotent under
  the present tyranny, which put their persons and property in such an
  irksome state of insecurity. This party might be induced to shew
  itself if support were near and such support cannot be so quickly
  conveyed as by the shortest route and that on which there are the
  fewest barriers.” (Namely, that of Dieppe) (B.M. Add. MSS. 37852, f.
  32.)

  In the years that followed he was in close touch with “émigrés” of all
  kinds. His escape from the Temple was effected by them. He maintained
  his relations with them with the approval of Lord Grenville. (Lord
  Grenville to Windham, 12th May, 1798, B.M. Add. MSS. 37846.) When he
  went to the Levant at the end of the year, he took with him, among
  others, Phélippeaux, the codisciple of Bonaparte, de Fromelin, alias
  Bromley, and the young Frotté, for no other object than to attract by
  their influence most of the discontented officers of Bonaparte’s army,
  many of whom, to his knowledge, belonged to the same school of thought
  as Phélippeaux. He advised the Porte to encourage would-be deserters
  by promising them posts in its armies or a safe return home to rise
  against the Directory. (Minutes of a Conference on 17th January, 1799,
  F.O., Turkey, 21.) He was convinced that the Army of the East
  “belonged to the dissatisfied part of the army which the Republican
  tyrants wished to get rid of,” “that France can only be set to rights
  by Frenchmen,” and that, till then, there could be no peace. (To
  Keith, 13th March, 1800. Barrow, I, 385, 387.)

  During the siege of Acre he did not neglect to use these means of
  seduction. (De La Jonquière, IV, pp. 526-7; to Nelson, 30th May, 1799,
  Barrow, I, 307; to Spencer Smith, 14th May, 1799, Barrow, I, 296.) The
  result was a violent proclamation from Bonaparte against Sir Sidney,
  whom he dubbed for his achievements in Toulon, “Le Capitaine des
  brûlots,” and strict injunctions to his men not to enter into
  communications with him. (“Correspondance,” t. V, 4098.)

  With the accession of Kléber to the command, the prospects brightened.
  He believed that Kléber and his officers were animated by one
  sentiment of indignation against their “ci-devant” commander, deluded
  himself with the fact that none of them wore the tricolour cockade and
  the “indignation of the French against the Jacobin faction for having
  brought it and them into such a desperate state.” (9th November, 1800,
  F.O., Turkey, 23.)

  Sidney Smith had no success. But was his failure complete? By no
  means. His insinuations had created a strong party in the army,
  opposed to the occupation of Egypt. As for the wider aspect of his
  programme, it was impossible of realisation. In the way, however, that
  Bonaparte treated Poussielgue, Reynier and Damas—exactly the men who
  were in close touch with Sidney Smith—there is evidence of his
  suspicion that Sir Sidney had created enemies of his person and
  grandeur.


6. _Transactions of Lord Elgin._

  The ambassadors in Constantinople drew handsome profit from the sale
  of diplomas of protection or “barâts.” Liston, in 1795, denied himself
  the privilege of drawing upon so “turbid a source.” The protected
  person exploited “all the weight of the British embassy,” even in law-
  suits. (Drummond to Hawkesbury, 22nd June, 1803, F.O., Turkey, 40.)
  The lengths that Lord Elgin went in the art of making money was
  astounding. (Drummond to Hawkesbury, 13th July, 1803, F.O., Turkey,
  40.) In August, 1801, “an attention” was paid him on the surrender of
  Cairo in the shape of 25,000 piastres in gold. In September he
  received 32,000 and his “Lady” 15,000. This was on the news of the
  surrender of Alexandria. On the 14th January, 1803, the sum of 25,000
  was sent to them upon their departure. During his embassy, there
  “occurred” only five barâts, upon one of which Elgin received nothing,
  being a transfer from one person to another. The “highest
  consideration” to the “ambassadoress” upon another was 5,000. The rest
  brought in less. As for procuring “firmans” for individuals, Elgin
  received 1000 for his interposition at the Porte. “They fell in more
  frequently than Barâts.” He also received 10,000 as “equipage money”
  when he proposed to go to Egypt to settle the affairs of the Beys. The
  visit was cancelled, but the money was not returned. There was also
  the sum of 30,000 piastres for furnishing the French Palace when it
  was occupied by Elgin during the war between Turkey and France. The
  money, apparently, was not all spent on furniture, for the French
  Chargé, when he took over the palace, refused to accept the furniture
  on account of its badness.

  Elgin also received—but this in common with all his predecessors—a
  lump sum of 27,000 for himself and 10,000 for his wife in lieu of the
  “Taayn,” which was an allowance of 500 piastres per day granted to the
  embassies by the Porte.

  As for “jewels” and “presents,” excluding the legitimate ones on the
  occasion of presenting credentials, a ring of the value of 10,000
  piastres presented by Selim to Lady Elgin in an audience in October,
  1801, a diamond snuff-box in June, 1800, worth 10,000 piastres, an
  elegant diamond “aigrette” from the dowager Sultana, worth 10,000, an
  “aigrette” on the recovery of Egypt valued at 12,000, a snuff-box
  worth 5000 and a dagger worth 4000 from the Capitan Pasha, to Lady
  Elgin a parcel of shawls and a diamond flower from the sister of the
  same dignitary, another and a diamond watch and chain from the dowager
  Sultana—the whole fetched 20,000. (Reports of Pisani to Drummond II,
  25th July, 1803, F.O., Turkey, 40.) Pisani observes “that the above
  pecuniary compliments (as well as the other presents in jewels, etc.)
  were not made without some previous suggestion and solicitation on the
  part of the person acting in the ambassador’s interests at the Porte.”

  It might be asked, however, could Pisani’s deposition be accepted? It
  could, because, in stating every item, he gave his, his wife’s and his
  daughter’s share. The Capitan Pasha told the French Ambassador, Brune,
  that “Elgin a demandé et obtenu cinq audiences de congé parceque des
  présents sont attachés à cette sorte de cérémonie, pour des présents
  sa femme était allé visiter jusqu’aux dernières concubines du sérail;
  enfin le capitan Pasha se dit avoir été presque butiné ou dépouillé
  par l’indiscrétion de toutes les personnes qui composaient la légation
  anglaise.” (Brune to Bonaparte, 16 pluviôse an XI, 4th February, 1803,
  Arch. Nat. AF. IV, 1688.) Elgin waxed indignant over the fact that
  Christopher Hutchinson went straight to the Porte with the news of the
  surrender of Alexandria and thus first received the full benefit of
  Turkish joy. (Elgin to Hawkesbury, 19th October, 1801, F.O., Turkey,
  33.) But, we have seen, he was not forgotten.

  No excuse could be pleaded for him. The dragomen, however, were in a
  peculiar situation. They were not paid any salary at all. In point of
  fact, Pisani, in making his above-mentioned declaration, requested
  Drummond to receive it—not as a matter for censure—but as proof of the
  necessity of establishing a new system which would free the dragomen
  from worry and ensure their independence of the influence of the
  Porte. With Elgin it was a different matter. His appointments came to
  £4500 a year. (Elgin to Hawkesbury, 13th January, 1803, F.O. Turkey,
  38.) The Levant Company paid for all the customary presents to the
  Porte. Straton, who succeeded Elgin as mere “Chargé d’Affaires,”
  voluntarily divested himself from profits resulting from the sale of
  barâts and other sources, but could not deprive the dragomen of what
  was, for them, a means of livelihood.

  When Lord Hawkesbury received the accounts of the “very improper
  practices with respect to pecuniary transactions which have prevailed
  at Constantinople at different periods under the sanction or with the
  connivance of former ambassadors,” he announced the intention to
  reform the practice. (Hawkesbury to Drummond, 9th September, 1803,
  F.O., Turkey, 40.) As a matter of fact, efforts were made in the
  succeeding years to do this. Scales of pay were proposed for the
  dragomen; but the practice was not changed during the years under
  review.


7. _The “Egyptian Archives.”_

  M. Maunier states that his valuable “bibliography” “exclura les
  documents publiques ou privées dont le recensement voudrait un travail
  special” (p. XII).

  There is an official “Records Office”—but not a Public Records
  Office—in the Citadel of Cairo. As far as I have been able to find
  out—the contents are not catalogued—it mainly contains title-deeds,
  and old administrative and judicial documents. The King of Egypt is
  said to possess a large collection of political documents, mostly
  written in Turkish and relating especially to the wars of Ibrahim
  Pasha. I have been informed that these documents are in the course of
  being translated into Arabic.

 APPENDIX II

 THE REPORT OF SEBASTIANI


Historical criticism of this document has moved, as it were, in the air.
Historians have relied mainly on “mémoires”-writers and confined their
labours to ingenious guesswork. According to Lucien Bonaparte
(“Mémoires,” II, ch. IX), Napoleon “dictated” the Report with the
express purpose of rousing “John Bull.” Meneval (“Mémoires,” I, ch. III)
says that Jaubert saw a proof of the Report as it was to be printed in
the “Moniteur” and advised the omission of the most irritating passages.
But Maret refused to take upon himself to do this. We have also to take
into account the fact that a few days after the publication, Sebastiani
himself met the British Ambassador at Talleyrand’s and “recanted
everything he had said in his report disrespectful to General Stuart and
mentioned him in terms of great esteem.” (Whitworth’s despatch, 3rd
February, 1803, Browning, p. 59.)

Fournier (“Life of Napoleon,” I, p. 316, note) dismisses the hypothesis
that Sebastiani told Napoleon something other than he told the British
Ambassador and believes that Napoleon “altered” the despatch of the
Colonel. The inference is that Napoleon fabricated a report and
attributed it to his agent.

But all this is wrong. We will print below a letter sent from Sebastiani
from Alexandria which is couched in the same style and expresses the
same views as the Report itself. We will also print those passages from
the manuscript report which have been either omitted from or changed in
the “Moniteur” version. The conclusions are:—

1. The Report was entirely Sebastiani’s.

2. Napoleon certainly “altered” the Report, but not in the sense implied
in Fournier, i.e. he omitted or changed all passages that might give
umbrage to the Porte or incriminate individuals and left intact all
passages against British policy and agents. The alterations on the
original manuscript are in pencil and are more or less becoming effaced.

3. The “recantation” of Sebastiani is of no importance. He probably
recanted at the orders of his master in pursuance of a momentary need.

 SEBASTIANI TO TALLEYRAND.
 Devant Alexandrie à bord de la “Cornelie.”
                                      2 vendémiaire XI (23 Sept., 1802).

. . . On arrival I went to Stuart, spoke to him about the evacuation.
His answer, as I had expected, was that he had received no orders and
that he believed he would have to pass the winter in Egypt. “Le peuple
d’Alexandrie a montré le plus grand joie en nous voyant. Déjà on disait
que je venais prendre possession de la ville au nom du Grand Consul et
le contentement des habitants se manifestait de manière à attirer même
l’attention des Anglais.” The English occupy Alexandria with 4500
soldiers only. The Turks occupy the rest of the country with 20,000.
They had been driven out of the forts of Alexandria by Stuart. Great
“mésintelligence” between the two armies. The Mamelukes are in the
Fayyoum or at 10 or 12 leagues from Cairo. They number 3 to 4000,
defeated the Turks and popular with the inhabitants. The English openly
favour them. The Arabs are with them. Djezzar, it is said, will join
them. Great disorder in Syria. “L’Armée d’Orient et surtout le Premier
Consul ont laissé ici de grands souvenirs. Son nom est en vénération et
on peut compter entièrement sur tous les habitants de quelque nation et
de quelque religion qu’ils soient. Ce que j’ai l’honneur de vous dire
est la plus exacte vérité.” If the Pasha accepts my mediation I shall be
in position to know the exact situation of the Beys.

 (Arch. Aff. Et. Turquie, Corr. Pol., vol. 205.)


 THE REPORT


                                  (Arch. Nat. AF. IV, 1687—“Moniteur.”
                                        10 pluviôse XI—30 Jan., 1803.)

N.B.—The passages that occur in the two versions are briefly stated in
English. We will insert only those that have not been published and
those that have been altered.

 _“Moniteur” Version._ _Manuscript Version._

 “La mission dont j’ai été chargé
 avait pour objet de connaître
 l’état actuel de l’Egypte et de la
 Syrie sous les rapports
 politiques, administratifs, et
 militaires. J’ai pensé que pour
 répondre aux instructions que
 j’avais reçues je devais voir par
 moi même les hommes et les choses
 et surtout les voir sans
 prévention. Cette opération
 n’était pas sans difficulté
 cependant je crois être parvenu à
 me procurer des renseignements
 positifs. Je vais les réunir et
 afin que l’on connaisse mieux ce
 que j’ai fait, ce rapport sera le
 journal de mon voyage.”—Mission in
 Tripoli—Arrival and interview with
 Stuart—“Il ajouta qu’il pensait
 que les Anglais ne quitteront
 l’Egypte et Malte que lorsqu’un
 traité de commerce aurait été
 conclu entre la France et
 l’Angleterre.”

 Stuart is a mediocre spirit, “et
 d’une vanité excessive. Il croyait
 fixer l’attention de l’Europe sur
 lui, en conservant le commandement
 de l’armée anglaise en Egypte. Je
 sais d’une manière positive qu’il
 cherche à alarmer le cabinet de
 St. James sur la situation de ce
 pays, afin d’en empêcher ou d’en
 retarder l’évacuation.” He has for
 A.D.C. a French émigré, Chevalier
 de Sades, and is under his
 influence. I saw Khourchid Pasha
 and announced the speedy arrival
 of French commercial agents, which
 gave him great pleasure. He did
 not hide that he saw with pain the
 British occupation I told him that
 it could not be prolonged.

 On 25th I saw Sheikh Messire.
 “C’est de lui que j’appris qu’au
 moment de mon arrivée on avait cru
 que je venais prendre possession
 de la ville et que les habitants
 en avaient été dans
 l’enchantement.” I was a witness
 of their joy.

 On the same day saw Sheikh Ibrahim
 Mufti. “C’est un homme entièrement
 dévoué à la France dont il a une
 pension, mais très décrié pour ses
 mœurs et ayant fort peu de
 l’influence.”

 Visited forts, details, Voyage to
 Cairo, saw various people. “Tout
 le monde m’assûra que les Français
 y sont vivement regrettés et que
 le nom du Premier Consul y est en
 vénération.” Details on Foué,
 Rahmanieh, Menouf. Here Sheikh
 Abdine, “m’assûra que l’Egypte
 faisait constamment des vœux pour
 rentrer sous la domination du
 Premier Consul.” I told him and
 others, “Ce que j’ai toujours dit
 en Egypte,” that 1st Consul loves
 their country, will not forget it
 and is interested in its welfare
  [a] et vous recommandera à la that[a] “Il commande aux deux plus
 Porte. puissantes et plus riches nations
 de l’univers, la France et
  [b] “fait.” l’Italie.” He had dictated[b]
 peace to Europe and Egypt will
  [c] “Conserve aux pauvres Cheikhs  feel the interest he _takes in
 d’Egypte.” her_.[c]

 In Cairo, meeting with Khosrev.
 “Le Pacha sentait si bien qu’il
 n’était pas en état de résister
 longtemps aux Beys qu’il me
 chargea de prier le Premier Consul
 d’ouvrir cette négociation (with
 the Beys) à Constantinople et de
 la terminer promptement.” Visits
 to Forts. Meetings with Sheikhs.
 “J’ai été étonné du courage avec
 lequel ils avouent leur désir de
 redevenir les sujets du Premier
 Consul. Pour être sûr de la
 sincérité de leurs vœux et de
 leurs sentiments” one should have
 seen as I did their enthusiasm on
 receiving the portraits of
 Bonaparte. Sayed Omar ill and I
 saw his son, Bekri “qui est fort
 mal avec le Pasha, me pria de ne
 point le voir, craignant de se
 compromettre. El Arichy, El Mohdy
 et El Arice me firent dire la même
 chose.”

 On 7th called on the widow of
 Mourad Bey. “Elle me parla avec
 force des vexations des Turcs et
 me pria de contribuer au
 rétablissement des Beys qui
 conserveraient à la France et au
 Premier Consul une reconnaissance
 éternelle.” I told her of my
 overture for mediation and its
 rejection and added “qu’elle
 pouvait cependant assurer les Beys
 (dont elle est l’agent au Caire)
 que le Premier Consul avait reçu
 leur lettre, qu’il avait pour eux
 de la bienveillance et qu’il
 s’intéresserait pour terminer
 leurs différends d’une manière
 avantageuse aux deux partis.”

 Visits to Citadel, Roda, Gizé,
 Bulak. Murmurs of Turkish
 soldiers, but I did not heed. A
 Turk pointed his yatagan at me,
 but as he was drunk and the people
 indignant at his behaviour, I took
 no notice. Moustapha Oukil
 promised to bastinado my servant.
 I was near having his head cut
 off, but I forgave him. Albanians
 were instigated against me.
 English spread rumours of a French
 invasion. Pasha not duped by this.
 Stuart instigated murderers
 against me. Two most influential
 persons with Pasha are Rosetti and
 Mahrouki. There is also a
 “defterdar,” Sherif Effendi,
 “Homme qui entend les affaires et
 qui jouit d’une bonne réputation.
 Il gémit de l’influence de Rosetti
 et de Mahrouki, et j’ai été très
 satisfait de lui. Au divan de
 Constantinople dont il est membre,
 il a toujours été pour le parti
 français.” Was Effendi, and
 refused Pashalik of Jedda.

 “Un jeune homme qui m’a été
 vraiment utile, et qui est aussi
 dévoué à la France qu’il est
 enthousiaste du Premier Consul,
 c’est M. Stefanachi, drogman du
 Pasha. C’est un Grec de la Morée;
 il jouit d’une fortune
 considérable, il a de
 l’imagination et croit à une
 prompte dissolution de l’empire
 Ottoman. Il ne doute point que
 dans le bouleversement de le vaste
 empire sa patrie ne devienne le
 partage de la France, et il a déjà
 pour elle tous les sentiments
 d’affection d’un citoyen français.
 Il a de l’indiscrétion de son âge,
 mais, bien manié, on peut en tirer
 un grand partie.”

 Khosrev Pasha is a Georgian slave,
 brought up in household of Capitan
 Pasha. He has much of character of
 his master. “Il aime la gloire
 sans la connaître, aussi toutes
 ses vues se portent sur des objets
 futiles, tels que des habillements
 des troupes, etc. Il n’a aucune
 connaissance sur la guerre, la
 politique et l’administration; il
 ne sait que couper des têtes: il
 est vrai que sa cour lui donne des
 ordres bien barbares. Son drogman
 m’en a communiqué un entre-autres,
 par lequel le divan lui enjoint de
 faire périr tous les Mamelukes qui
 sont répandus dans l’Egypte,
 qu’ils conservent ou non des
 liaisons avec les Beys. J’en ai vu
 un autre par lequel on le charge
 de humilier tous les principaux
 Cheikhs, de les dépouiller, autant
 que possible, de leurs richesses
 et de frapper notamment sur les
 Cheikhs el Bekery et el Sadat.”
 Sadat bitterly complained to me
 and protested attachment to
 Consul. “Et ne désire que
 l’occasion de lui prouver son
 dévouement.” He said that the time
 that Great man spent in Egypt had
  [d] “ne doit s’en ressouvenir been marked by benefits to himself
 qu’en le bénissant, il était and “ma patrie[d] _ne peut être
 juste et bon.” heureuse que sous lui_.”

 “L’intendant Cophte m’a proposé
 d’entretenir une correspondance
 par laquelle il informerait de
 tout ce qui pourrait survenir
 d’intéressant en Egypte et en
 Syrie. Il m’a offert ses services
 et ceux de sa nation, dans le cas
 où l’on aurait des vues sur
 l’Orient. J’ai tout lieu de croire
 à sa sincérité, mais je lui ai
 répondu que je n’avais aucune
 instruction là-dessus.”

 Saw Beduin Chieftains, received a
 deputation from Monks of Convent
 of Sinai. Wrote to Pasha in their
 favour. Accorded them your
 protection. Monks of “Progagande”
 celebrated a Te Deum for
 prosperity of First Consul. On eve
 of departure again saw Pasha and
 recommended to him all Christians
 as well as Moslems who had had
 connection with our army. Left for
 Damiette.

 “Au Caire, comme dans toute
 l’Egypte, j’ai dit beaucoup de
 bien de la Porte et le Pasha en a
 été extrêmement satisfait.”

 En route for Damiette, son of
 Hassan Toubar visited me. “Il se
 plaignait amèrement des vexations
 qu’il éprouvait de la part des
 Turcs, pour l’attachement qu’il
 avait montré à l’armée française.
 Il me déclara qu’il n’attendait
 que l’occasion de donner de
 nouveaux témoignages de ses
 sentiments.” His influence very
 great. Saw various people en route
 and in Damiette.

 “Le 20e j’ai reçu une lettre
 d’Osman Bey, El Bardissy, qui m’a
 été adressée au Caire. . . . Dans
 ma réponse à Osman je lui fis part
 que le Premier Consul m’a chargé
 de terminer les différends des
 Beys avec les Osmanlis, mais que
 cette affaire ne pouvait se
 traiter qu’à Constantinople, que
 le Premier Consul avait pour eux
 de la bienveillance et qu’il
 chercherait à leur être utile.”

 “La situation actuelle de l’Egypte
 ne saurait être plus malheureuse.
 Le système de contributions adopté
 par le Pasha est un mélange
 informe de celui qui avait été
 établi dans les derniers tems
 [_sic_] de l’armée française et de
 celui qui existait du tems [_sic_]
 des Mamelukes. Le peuple paye le
 double et sa misère est à son
 comble. Cette province est traitée
 en pays conquis, et le chef et les
 subalternes craignant également de
 la voir passer en d’autres mains
 se hâtent de faire fortune. Les
 avanies sont fréquentes, le
 prétexte en est l’intelligence des
 victimes avec les Beys, les
 véritables motifs ce sont les
 richesses de ceux qu’on veut
 perdre. Le délateur le plus
 furieux et le plus à craindre,
 c’est Mahrouki: C’est lui qui a
 fait périr dernièrement le
 commandant de Menouf. Les Osmanlis
 sont en horreur et je ne doute
 point que le Caire et toute
 l’Egypte ne s’insurgent en faveur
 des Mamelukes, quoi qu’ils ne
 soient pas aimés, mais le souvenir
 de leurs vexations est effacé par
 le sentiment de souffrances
 actuelles des habitants. Il est
 certain cependant que les
 Mameloukes ne peuvent plus régner
 en Egypte. Leurs propres forces ne
 se montent pas au delà de 3000
 hommes et l’achat des esclaves
 Géorgiens leur étant défendu à
 Constantinople il leur est
 impossible de réparer leurs pertes
 et d’organiser un corps d’armée,
 qui leur assure la possession de
 ce royaume. Les Arabes qui
 combattent aujourd’hui dans leurs
 rangs ne tarderont pas à les
 quitter, d’ailleurs ils n’ont plus
 pour eux l’opinion. On les croyait
 invincibles avant l’arrivée de
 l’armée française, aujourd’hui
 leur prestige est détruit.”

 In Egypt, Chieftains, merchants,
  [e] aiment à s’entretenir du ulemas, “[e]_tout attend sa
 Premier Consul, font des vœux  délivrance du Premier Consul, tout
 pour son bonheur. Toutes les fait des vœux pour rentrer sous sa
 nouvelles qui le concernent domination et il est certain qu’à
 se répandent d’Alexandrie ou l’apparition d’une armée française
 de Damietta aux Pyramides et le pays se déclarera pour elle_.”
 grandes cataractes avec une
 rapidité étonnante.

 Visit to Acre. Interview with
 Djezzar (with no changes or
 omissions from the original).

 Details on the British, Turkish
 and Mameluke forces in Egypt, and
 forts. 6000 Frenchmen should
 suffice for the reconquest of the
 country.

 Details on the Mamelukes. Power is
 divided between Ibrahim, Elfi and
 Bardissy. “Ce dernier passe pour
 être fort attaché aux Français et
 cette opinion lui donne beaucoup
 d’influence.”

 Details on Syria.

Obvious typographical errors have been fixed. French spelling has been
corrected where necessary. Closing or opening quotation marks have been
inserted where necessary; a few quotes have been left unclosed.

to: "II"
